Idaho Cannabis & Hemp Research — Audited Final Report, Parts 0–12

Last audited: 1 November 2026. This is an independent audit and corrected rewrite of the combined Parts 0–12 file dated 1 November 2026. The audit table below is part of this file. Every correction in the audit table has been applied in the rewritten report that follows it, so the rewritten report does not repeat outdated values alongside their corrections. Verify / Unavailable flags remain only where the audit could not confirm a claim from a primary source.

Election status on the audit date: the 3 November 2026 election had not occurred on 1 November 2026. HJR 4 (2025) was on that ballot and no certified result existed on the audit date. Do not carry this file forward after 3 November 2026 without replacing the HJR 4 passages with certified Secretary of State / VoteIdaho results.

Federal baseline (given, not re-audited): split scheduling — FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed medical marijuana are in Schedule III under a final rule at 91 FR 22714 (effective 28 April 2026); all other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Hemp: Public Law 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesized; the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement is delayed until 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. Idaho conclusion, confirmed: Idaho has no state-licensed medical programme, so no Idaho marijuana activity falls inside the federal state-licensed medical Schedule III carve-out. Non-FDA-approved marijuana remains Schedule I in Idaho. Whether a tribal medical programme would count as “state-licensed” is Verify — no authority in the federal rule text was located in this audit to answer it, and a USDA-approved tribal hemp plan is not a state licence.

How to read the audit: Confirmed = opened in a primary source. Corrected = a primary source supports a different wording, date, vote, fee or status. Removed = wrong and no correct version was found, or a secondary claim that must not be published as fact. Unverified = only a secondary source exists, a primary page could not be opened, or the claim is an absence that cannot be proved by search alone. Primary sources used: legislature.idaho.gov, Idaho Constitution and Administrative Code / Administrative Bulletin, ISDA, Idaho State Police, State Tax Commission, Board of Pharmacy (DOPL), Attorney General, Secretary of State / VoteIdaho, Idaho and federal court records, USDA AMS, and official tribal, county and city sources.

PART A — AUDIT TABLE

Audit counts (rows = 158): Confirmed: 54; Corrected: 35; Confirmed in part: 31; Unverified: 36; Removed: 2. Counts sum exactly to 158 rows. Verdict key: Confirmed = opened in a primary source; Confirmed in part = the core claim is primary-confirmed but a sub-point remains Verify (see Corrected wording); Corrected = a primary source supports a different wording/date/vote/fee/status; Removed = wrong and no correct version was found, or a secondary claim that must not be published as fact; Unverified = only a secondary source exists, a primary page could not be opened, or the claim is an absence that cannot be proved by search alone.

Material corrections in one line: HB 777 is enacted law (ch. 90, eff. 1 July 2026), not 'Verify'; the 2021 psychoactive-drugs ban is SJR 101, not HJR 4, and 2021 HJR 4 failed 42–28; Morin did not hold the magistrate erred in denying in-limine exclusion — carboxy-THC was relevant and properly admitted with other impairment evidence; Van Camp is a cyclobenzaprine case, not a THC holding; §18-3302(11)(a) is age under 21, not a general ineligibility catch-all; Clean Slate trafficking (§37-2732B) is not listed, while §37-2732(a) is; §6-303(5) prescribes no notice period; retail THC must be presented in three layers (statute 0.3% criteria, ODP zero position, proposed no-detectable-THC rule, not final); Sandpoint '1%' is removed; Crexi/Land & Farm snippet counts are removed as inventory figures; Part 8 '0' is downgraded to 'none found in the searches tried'.

Part 12.3State v. Morin, 158 Idaho 622, 349 P.3d 1213 (Ct. App. 25 Feb 2015, review denied 22 June 2015): Court held magistrate erred in denying motion in limine to exclude carboxy-THC, but error was harmless; facts: failed walk-and-turn/one-leg-standCorrectedhttps://isc.idaho.gov/opinions/41832X.pdf ; https://static.case.law/idaho/158/case-pdfs/0622-01.pdfCORRECTED HOLDING: Court held discovery response was deficient as a matter of law but did not warrant reversal, AND held carboxy-THC evidence was relevant and properly admitted when combined with other impairment evidence. It did NOT hold magistrate erred in denying in-limine exclusion — district court had held magistrate correctly held it relevant/admissible, and Court of Appeals affirmed. Facts corrected to: inactive metabolite, no pharmacological effect, detectable ≥10 days to a month after use; other evidence slurred speech, strange responses, failed FSTs, lack of convergence, dilated pupils, abnormal eye tremors. Safe formulation: 'relevant when combined with other evidence of present impairment.'

Part 12.4§67-3004(11)(a) eligibility; 'Verify whether trafficking counts as possession'; 'delivery/manufacture at higher risk of exclusion'Correctedhttps://legislature.idaho.gov/statutesrules/idstat/title67/t67ch30/sect67-3004/RESOLVED: (11)(a) lists misdemeanor NOT an assaultive/violent misdemeanor in (11)(b), OR felony possession under §37-2732(a), (c), or (e). So (i) ≤3 oz misdemeanor marijuana possession is on its face a non-assaultive misdemeanor in eligible class, subject to (11)(c); (ii) felony possession under §37-2732(e) (>3 oz) IS listed; (iii) trafficking under §37-2732B is NOT listed and is therefore NOT shieldable under (11) — remove trafficking Verify flag and replace with this; (iv) §37-2732(a) manufacture/delivery/possession-with-intent IS listed, so some delivery/manufacture convictions under (a) are eligible — correct the 'higher risk' wording with the (a) vs (37-2732B) distinction.

Part 12.5No marijuana-specific landlord/tenant statute found; §6-303(3) 3-day perform/quit; §6-303(4) assignment/subletting/waste 3-day; §6-303(5) controlled-substance ground — conflict 'no notice vs 3-day notice to quit'; tenant scopeCorrectedhttps://legislature.idaho.gov/statutesrules/idstat/Title6/T6CH3/SECT6-303/RESOLVED RN-43: §6-303(5) itself prescribes NO notice period, unlike (2)-(4) which each prescribe 3 days. A 3-day notice to quit for drug activity in forms/guides is practice/form, not text in (5). Do not quote either 'no notice required' or '3-day notice required' as statutory text for (5); state (5) is silent on notice. Hemani outcome, §922(a)(6)/§924(a)(8) penalty detail, §18-3302 permitless-carry criteria + 2027 version, zero-THC CBD + §922(g)(3) ATF guidance (Federal Register / ATF / Supreme Court docket / U.S. Code, re-checked on publication day); ISDA animal-feed memo currency (ISDA feed programme); veterinary-board CBD guidance, district medication/Epidiolex policies, UI current research/funding, IDWR hemp water rules (Board of Veterinary Medicine; specific districts; UI; IDWR); polling decks/crosstabs (pollsters); NASS Idaho tables (NASS PDFs); Tax Commission CBD ruling (Tax Commission).
  20. Idaho response to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 after 1 Nov 2026 — ISDA + AG indexes re-checked after 11 December 2026, incl. whether tribal hemp-code federal-definition references auto-incorporate the new definition.
  21. Personal data — no personal phone/email for an individual licence holder is reproduced in this file. If a future edition lists a licence holder, keep name + official-source link only.

Idaho Cannabis & Hemp Research — Complete, Parts 0–12

Both runs in one file. This file combines Run 1 (Parts 0–6) and Run 2 (Parts 7–12) in full, unchanged apart from this cover. Research date and date accessed for sources opened for both runs: 1 November 2026, unless a source itself is dated otherwise. The two original files are preserved separately and are not altered by this combined file: Run 1 at idaho-cannabis-hemp-parts-0-6-2026-11-01.html and Run 2 at idaho-cannabis-hemp-parts-7-12-2026-11-01.html.

Contents: Part 0 Headlines; Part 1 Laws; Part 2 Taxes; Part 3 Local Rules; Part 4 Licence Types; Part 5 Licence Data Sources; Part 6 Market Data; Run 1 interim gaps, conflicts and follow-ups (as written in Run 1); Part 7 Hemp Licences; Part 8 Cannabis-Zoned Properties; Part 9 Regulators and History; Part 10 Tribal and Interstate; Part 11 Operations Rules; Part 12 Consumer and Social Rules; Research Notes covering both runs (RN-01 to RN-52).

Note on the two notes sections: Run 1 ends with an interim gaps list that was written to be carried into Run 2. It is retained here as written. The final Research Notes at the end of this file supersede it: they record which interim items were resolved or corrected in Run 2 and which remain Verify. Election status — read first: the 3 November 2026 election had not occurred on the research date, 1 November 2026. HJR 4 is on that ballot. No official result for HJR 4 exists in this file, and none is invented. A Ballotpedia table showing “Yes 0.00% / 0” is a pre-election placeholder, not a result. Re-check the Secretary of State / VoteIdaho results after 3 November 2026.

Federal baseline used as fixed context (given by the commission, not re-researched): split scheduling — state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026; after 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. There is no federal cannabis banking law.

Idaho conclusion on that baseline: Idaho has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme. Marijuana therefore remains Schedule I in Idaho under Idaho law, and there is no Idaho activity that falls inside the federal “state-licensed medical” carve-out. Whether any tribal medical programme would count as “state-licensed” for federal purposes is marked Verify throughout.

Run 1 begins here — Parts 0–6.

PART 0: HEADLINES

  1. Illegal for all marijuana use. Recreational and medical marijuana are both illegal in Idaho. Marijuana is Schedule I under Idaho Code §37-2705(d)(19), and tetrahydrocannabinols are Schedule I under §37-2705(d)(29). There is no medical programme, no patient registry, no dispensary licensing, and no reciprocity for out-of-state medical cards in Title 37, Chapter 27. Sources: Idaho Code §37-2705; Idaho Code §37-2701. Accessed 1 November 2026.
  2. Federal split scheduling does not change Idaho law by itself. Idaho Code §37-2702(d) requires the Board of Pharmacy to similarly control a federally rescheduled substance by temporary rule or proposed statutory amendment within 30 days of a Federal Register final order, unless the Board objects within that period, publishes reasons, holds a hearing and publishes a decision. Whether the Board objected, made a temporary rule, or proposed a statutory amendment on the April 2026 federal order was not found as of 1 November 2026 — Verify. The 30-day clock in §37-2702(d) runs from publication in the Federal Register, not from the 28 April 2026 effective date, so that window had expired by 1 November 2026; on publication of an objection, control is stayed until the Board publishes its decision. Source: Idaho Code §37-2702. Accessed 1 November 2026.
  3. HJR 4 (2025) is on the ballot, not yet law, and not yet voted on. It would amend Idaho Constitution Art. III, §26 so that only the Legislature may legalise marijuana, narcotics or other psychoactive substances. House adopted 58–10–2 on 5 March 2025; Senate adopted 29–6–0; delivered to the Secretary of State at 10:59 a.m. on 14 March 2025 (reported 17 March 2025). It is on the 3 November 2026 ballot. As of 1 November 2026 that election has not occurred, so no result exists to report. HJR 4 by itself would neither legalise nor ban anything new; it changes who can legalise. Source: HJR 4 legislature page. Accessed 1 November 2026.
  4. The 2026 medical cannabis initiative failed to qualify and is NOT on the 3 November 2026 ballot. The Idaho Medical Cannabis Act (Natural Medicine Alliance of Idaho) submitted over 150,000 raw signatures, but the Secretary of State announced 14 July 2026 that only 58,024 county-certified valid signatures were counted, 12,701 short of the 70,725 required, and the 6%-in-18-of-35-legislative-districts requirement was also not met. The campaign stated 17 July 2026 it would not pursue a legal challenge. Source: Secretary of State 2026 ballot initiatives announcement (search-result summary; open the release to confirm wording before republication). Accessed 1 November 2026. Secondary corroboration dated July 2026: Cannabis Business Times and Marijuana Policy Project summaries, both July 2026.
  5. Penalties tightened, not loosened, in 2025. HB 7 (2025), Session Law ch. 7, signed 24 February 2025, effective 1 July 2025, added a $300 mandatory minimum fine for possession of ≤3 ounces by a person 18 or older. Source: HB 7 legislature page; Idaho Code §37-2732. Accessed 1 November 2026.
  6. Hemp is legal only under licence, and retail THC is effectively zero. HB 126 (2021) legalised licensed industrial hemp, but Idaho never adopted a 0.3% retail THC tolerance. The Idaho Office of Drug Policy (page accessed 1 November 2026; no update month is stated as primary-confirmed) states retail/marketplace products must contain zero percent THC, and licensed handlers must dilute the final product to zero percent THC. Source: Office of Drug Policy, CBD page. Accessed 1 November 2026.
  7. Hemp acreage and licensees collapsed in 2026. ISDA “Approximate Acreage Approved to Date”: 1,860 acres (2024) → 1,135 (2025) → 233 (2026); licensee rows counted from the ISDA table: 23 → 16 → 11. Source: ISDA Current & Past Idaho Hemp Licensees, opened directly 1 November 2026. The ISDA page states no reason for the fall; do not attribute it to a single cause without a further ISDA source.
  8. Border leakage is Ontario, Oregon’s economy, not Idaho’s. Ontario, OR — in Malheur County, OR, about 50–55 miles west of Boise, with Fruitland and Payette, ID as the Idaho-side neighbours — reports about $100 million per year in cannabis sales (city testimony to the Oregon Legislature, 2022; Portland Monthly for 2023, secondary), with the city and operators estimating about 90% of customers are Idahoans. That 90% is an estimate, not OLCC purchaser-residency data, which does not publish buyer state of residence. The City of Ontario’s FY 2026–27 Budget Message estimates $2.5 million from its 3% local marijuana tax — an estimate in the proposed budget, not a certified outturn — and states 2025–26 looks like it will come in higher than budgeted. No official Idaho-resident purchase count exists for Oregon, Washington, Nevada or Montana. See Part 6.
  9. Enforcement near the borders has not changed the underlying law. Marijuana lawfully bought in Oregon, Washington, Nevada or Montana may not lawfully be brought back into Idaho; Idaho possession and trafficking law in Part 1 applies on return. No official seizure/return-traffic count for 2025–2026 was found — Unavailable in this run.
  10. 2026 hemp-law changes already in force. HB 772 (2026), ch. 293, effective 1 July 2026, amends §22-1705 on negligent violations (grain/fibre-only growers acting in good faith are not negligent if total THC does not exceed 1% dry weight, where the hemp is produced for the sole purpose of producing grain or fibre and the producer has made reasonable efforts to grow within the acceptable hemp THC level as defined by rule and in accordance with Idaho statutes, rules and the state hemp plan). HB 879 (2026), ch. 292, effective 1 July 2026, amends §22-1705(4) so establishments offering hemp products for human ingestion or inhalation (with stated seed exclusions) are subject to Chapter 17. Neither bill creates a retail licence or a retail fee — see Part 4. Sources: HB 772; HB 879. Accessed 1 November 2026.
  11. No Idaho response to the new federal hemp dates was found. No ISDA statement, statute or Attorney General statement responding specifically to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 or the 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates was found as of 1 November 2026 — Verify after those dates. The ODP zero-THC position for retail is already stricter than the incoming federal 0.4 mg-per-container cap, so the federal change would not liberalise Idaho retail on that agency position. The statute itself, §22-1705(4) as enacted by HB 879, states the 0.3% criteria in §§37-2701(u) and 37-2705(d)(29)(i) — see Part 1.5 for the three layers kept apart.

PART 1: LAWS

1.1 Legal status

Illegal — both recreational and medical. Confirmed directly from statute: marijuana is listed in Schedule I, and no chapter in Title 37 creates a medical cannabis licensing, patient, caregiver, dispensary or reciprocity scheme. The only cannabis-derived medicines recognised are the FDA finished-dosage exceptions in §37-2701(u) (cannabidiol with no more than 0.1% (w/w) residual tetrahydrocannabinols, and nabiximols) and the dronabinol/nabiximols/hemp exceptions in §37-2705(d)(29)(i). CBD is legal only as described in §1.5 below; it is not a medical marijuana programme.

1.2 Every statute and regulation — citation, summary, verbatim operative text, last amendment, link

History lines below are as printed on the legislature pages opened 1 November 2026. Where a full page was not opened, that is stated and the text is marked Verify rather than quoted as confirmed.

Idaho Code §37-2701 — Definitions (opened directly)

Link: §37-2701. History on the page: added 1971, ch. 215; amendments listed through am. 2021, ch. 11; am. 2021, ch. 35; am. 2021, ch. 242; am. 2022, ch. 22; am. 2024, ch. 68. The last-amended date for the current §37-2701(u) text beyond that history line is Verify from the session laws.

Verbatim, §37-2701(u) “Marijuana” or “marihuana” (operative part):

“‘Marijuana’ or ‘marihuana’ means all parts of the plant of the genus Cannabis, regardless of species, and whether growing or not; the seeds thereof; the resin extracted from any part of such plant; and every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of such plant, its seeds or resin. It does not include: (1) Industrial hemp or hemp possessed, grown, transported, farmed, produced, processed, or possessed by any other entity engaged in hauling, transporting, delivering, or otherwise moving hemp in interstate or intrastate commerce pursuant to a license granted under the provisions of the 2014 farm bill, the 2018 farm bill, 7 CFR 990.1 et seq., or the approved state plan for the state of Idaho. ‘Industrial hemp’ or ‘hemp’ means the plant species Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a measured total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight or volume basis that shall determine the total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) concentration, including both delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol and delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinolic acid (THCA) evaluated by decarboxylation during analysis, or by measuring each compound and calculating the total percentage of delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol if the THCA was decarboxylated, which must not exceed three-tenths of one percent (0.3%). (2) The mature stalks … fiber produced from the stalks, oil or cake made from the seeds or the achene … or the sterilized seed … incapable of germination.”
“Evidence that any plant material or the resin or any derivative thereof, regardless of form, that does not meet the definition of ‘industrial hemp’ or ‘hemp’ as provided in this section, or that is possessed without a license granted under [2014 farm bill, 2018 farm bill, 7 CFR 990.1 et seq., or the approved state plan for the state of Idaho], contains any of the chemical substances classified as tetrahydrocannabinols shall create a presumption that such material is ‘marijuana’ as defined and prohibited herein. ‘Marijuana’ does not include drug product in finished dosage formulation that has been approved by the United States food and drug administration that contains: (i) cannabidiol … derived from cannabis and no more than one-tenth of one percent (0.1%) (w/w) residual tetrahydrocannabinols; or (ii) nabiximols.”

Other definitions opened on the same page: §37-2701(e) “Controlled substance” means a drug, substance or immediate precursor in schedules I through VI of article II; §37-2701(ee) “Production” includes the manufacture, planting, cultivation, growing, or harvesting of a controlled substance; §37-2701(o) “Drug paraphernalia” covers equipment/products/materials used, intended or designed for planting, propagating, cultivating, growing, harvesting, manufacturing, compounding, converting, producing, processing, preparing, testing, analyzing, packaging, repackaging, storing, containing, concealing, injecting, ingesting, inhaling, or otherwise introducing a controlled substance into the human body in violation of the chapter.

Idaho Code §37-2702 — Board of Pharmacy authority; federal conformity is qualified, not automatic (opened directly)

Link: §37-2702. History: added 1971; am. 1972; am. 2017, ch. 4.

Plain-English summary: §37-2702(d) requires the Board, within 30 days of a Federal Register final order rescheduling or deleting a substance, to similarly control it by temporary rule or proposed statutory amendment — unless the Board objects within that 30 days, publishes its reasons, holds a hearing, and publishes a decision, which is final unless altered by statute. The schedules themselves are also in statute (§§37-2705 et seq.), so a Board rule alone does not rewrite the statute. This is the provision that prevents any claim that Idaho “automatically follows” the federal April 2026 split-scheduling order. Whether the Board has acted or objected on that order is Verify.

Idaho Code §37-2705 — Schedule I (opened directly)

Link: §37-2705. The tail/history line of this page failed to open on one attempt (rate-limit, not retried); the (d)(19)/(29) text below was opened directly, but the last-amendment/bill number for the current numbering is Verify.

§37-2705(a): “The controlled substances listed in this section are included in schedule I.” §37-2705(d) chapeau: “Hallucinogenic substances. Any material, compound, mixture or preparation that contains any quantity of the following hallucinogenic substances, their salts, isomers and salts of isomers, unless specifically excepted … (for purposes of this subsection only, the term ‘isomer’ includes the optical, position and geometric isomers):” §37-2705(d)(19): “Marihuana (marijuana);” §37-2705(d)(29): “Tetrahydrocannabinols or synthetic equivalents of the substances contained in the plant, or in the resinous extractives of Cannabis, sp. and/or synthetic substances, derivatives, and their isomers with similar chemical structure such as the following: i. Tetrahydrocannabinols, except for the permitted amount of tetrahydrocannabinol found in industrial hemp, or nabiximols in a drug product approved by the United States food and drug administration: a. ∆1 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and their optical isomers, excluding dronabinol in sesame oil and encapsulated in either a soft gelatin capsule or in an oral solution in a drug product approved by the U.S. Food and Drug Administration. b. ∆6 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and their optical isomers. c. ∆3,4 cis or trans tetrahydrocannabinol, and its optical isomers…”

Correction to secondary sources: several secondary pages claim marijuana is in “Schedule VI”. That is not what the opened §37-2705 shows. Do not repeat the Schedule VI claim; §37-2713A / Schedule VI contents were not opened — Verify.

Schedule I criteria, §37-2704, were not opened directly — verbatim text is Verify before quoting. The Idaho Supreme Court in State v. Heath (2021, Docket 47334, cited in search) summarised the test as high potential for abuse and no accepted medical use in treatment in the United States or lack of accepted safety for use under medical supervision.

Idaho Code Title 22, Chapter 17 — Industrial Hemp Research and Development Act, HB 126 (2021)

HB 126 legislature page: HB 126 (2021). House passed 44–26–0 (8 March 2021), Senate passed 30–5–0, signed by the Governor 16 April 2021, Session Law ch. 242, effective 16 April 2021. It created Title 22, Chapter 17 (§§22-1701 to 22-1707), amended §37-2701 and §37-2705, and added §67-2921.

§22-1701 short title; §22-1702 legislative intent (search result from legislature.idaho.gov, Verify full page): (1) assume primary regulatory authority over industrial hemp as allowed by federal law; (2) allow production, processing, transportation and research of industrial hemp in Idaho; (3) require the director to submit a state plan as expeditiously as possible. Link: §22-1702.

§22-1703, opened directly (link: §22-1703; history: added 2021, ch. 242):

“‘Hemp’ or ‘industrial hemp’ means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis, as defined in the 2018 farm bill.”
Definition conflict to keep visible, not harmonised away: §22-1703 says only “delta-9 … not more than 0.3% on a dry weight basis”, while §37-2701(u)(1) spells out total delta-9 including THCA evaluated by decarboxylation, and §67-2921(4) (below) also uses total delta-9 including THCA. Do not present the three definitions as identical.

§22-1705 (search result from legislature.idaho.gov; full page Verify): (1) production, processing, transportation and research of industrial hemp are subject to the rules under this chapter, the state plan, the 2018 farm bill and its rules. (4) — added by HB 879 (2026), ch. 292 — “Establishments offering hemp products intended for human ingestion or inhalation, excluding nonviable hulled hemp seed, hemp seed protein powder, or hemp seed oil, shall be subject to the provisions of this chapter. Such hemp products shall meet the criteria provided by sections 37-2701(u) and 37-2705(d)(29)(i), Idaho Code.” (5) the department may promulgate rules establishing fees and penalties for violations under this chapter. History: added 2021, ch. 242; am. 2026, ch. 292 (HB 879); am. 2026, ch. 293 (HB 772). Link: §22-1705. §§22-1706 (Industrial Hemp Administration Fund) and 22-1707 (Exceptions) were not opened — Verify. Chapter index: Title 22, Chapter 17.

USDA-approved state plan — yes. Idaho runs its own USDA-approved state plan, not the USDA direct programme. ISDA press release 1 November 2021: USDA approved the Idaho State Hemp Plan; ISDA would begin licensing producers and handlers under Idaho Code and the 2018 Farm Bill; online applications opened 8 November 2021. Source: ISDA, Approved State Hemp Plan (1 Nov 2021). Accessed 1 November 2026 (via search; open the release to confirm wording before republication).

ISDA rules — IDAPA 02.01.07, Rules Governing Hemp. Original temporary/proposed rule Docket 02-0107-2101 (2021). The current consolidated IDAPA 02.01.07 text was not opened directly in this run — all rule-level details (licence fees, sampling, testing, labelling, age) are Verify against the current Administrative Code before republication. The fee table in Part 4 is from the ISDA Applications page and a Cornell mirror of IDAPA 02.01.07.301, not from a direct opening of the current Administrative Code text.

Idaho Code §67-2921 — Transport (search result; full page Verify)

Link: §67-2921. Summary from search result, to be confirmed by opening the full page: same 0.3% delta-9 dry-weight hemp definition as §22-1703; (4) samples are analysed in a manner consistent with the 2018 farm bill and 7 CFR 990.1 et seq. to determine total delta-9 THC concentration, including all THCA, and non-compliant samples may subject the transporter to criminal penalties for marijuana under Title 37, Chapter 27; (5) it is unlawful to knowingly possess industrial hemp without a licence or in violation of the 2014/2018 farm bills or 7 CFR 990.1 et seq., except when lawfully transporting on behalf of and at the direction of a licensee; (6) first violation is a misdemeanor, fine ≤$150, with further tiers in secondary summaries (second doubles, third up to $1,000 + up to 6 months — Verify); (6)(d) hemp transported or possessed in violation of (5) is deemed contraband subject to seizure and destruction. Whether any port-of-entry requirement from the 2019 executive order survives under §67-2921 / current ISDA rules is Verify.

Constitutional / legalisation-limiting law

No constitutional amendment limiting legalisation is currently in force. The only such measure in play is PROPOSED HJR 4 (2025), on the 3 November 2026 ballot (see Part 0). No statute was found that, by itself, purports to bar the Legislature from legalising marijuana in future; the bar HJR 4 would create is a bar on citizen-initiated legalisation, reserving the power to the Legislature. If HJR 4 passes, that reservation becomes constitutional law; if it fails, it does not. Do not present HJR 4 as current law. A separate question — whether any Idaho law or constitutional provision prevents the state from following a federal schedule change — is answered by §37-2702(d) above: the Board may object, and the schedules are in statute, so following requires Board rule-making and/or statutory amendment; no separate “anti-following” constitutional bar was found — Verify by a full constitutional search before stating this as exhaustive.

1.3 Possession, trafficking, cultivation, paraphernalia, public consumption, product types

§37-2732 — Prohibited acts A (opened directly)

Link: §37-2732. History ends: am. 2021, ch. 35; am. 2025, ch. 7 (HB 7).

§37-2732(a): “Except as authorized by this chapter, it is unlawful for any person to manufacture or deliver, or possess with intent to manufacture or deliver, a controlled substance.” (a)(1)(B): manufacture/delivery/intent for any other nonnarcotic Schedule I drug, or a Schedule III drug, is a felony, up to 5 years, up to $15,000, or both. Because “production” includes planting/cultivation (§37-2701(ee)), this is the manufacture/delivery/intent tier covering marijuana cultivation and sale below trafficking thresholds. There is no separate personal-use cultivation allowance.
§37-2732(c)(3): possession of a nonnarcotic Schedule I drug (except LSD) or Schedules III–VI is a misdemeanor, up to 1 year, up to $1,000, or both — but the marijuana-specific §37-2732(e) below overrides this for marijuana weight tiers. §37-2732(d) — presence: it is unlawful to be present at or on premises where a person knows illegal controlled substances are being manufactured or cultivated, or held for distribution, transportation, delivery, administration, use, or to be given away; misdemeanor, fine ≤$300 and ≤90 days county jail, or both.
§37-2732(e) — marijuana possession, verbatim: “If any person is found to possess marijuana, which for the purposes of this subsection shall be restricted to all parts of the plants of the genus Cannabis, including the extract or any preparation of cannabis which contains tetrahydrocannabinol, in an amount greater than three (3) ounces net weight, it shall be a felony and upon conviction may be imprisoned for not more than five (5) years, or fined not more than ten thousand dollars ($10,000), or both, and for any amount less than or equal to three (3) ounces net weight, it shall be a misdemeanor and upon conviction shall be subject to a fine of not less than three hundred dollars ($300) when possessed by a person eighteen (18) years of age or older in addition to any penalties provided for in subsection (c)(3) of this section.”
Amount (net weight)ClassificationMaximumMandatory minimum
≤3 ozMisdemeanor (§37-2732(e) + (c)(3))Up to 1 year jail and/or up to $1,000$300 fine if 18+ (HB 7, eff. 1 July 2025). No mandatory jail minimum in (e) itself.
>3 oz, <1 lbFelony (§37-2732(e))Up to 5 years and/or up to $10,000None in (e) itself
≥1 lb or ≥25 plantsTrafficking — §37-2732B belowSee trafficking tableYes

Product types: because (e) expressly covers “extract or any preparation … which contains tetrahydrocannabinol”, flower, edibles, vapes/cartridges, concentrates, beverages and oils containing THC are all treated as marijuana for possession purposes unless they fall inside the licensed-hemp or FDA-drug exceptions. No product type is separately legalised for marijuana.

§37-2732B — Trafficking, mandatory sentences (opened directly)

Link: §37-2732B. History: added 1992, ch. 336; last amendment listed am. 2006, ch. 245 — whether later amendments changed the marijuana tiers is Verify.

§37-2732B(a)(1): “Any person who knowingly manufactures, delivers, or brings into this state, or who is knowingly in actual or constructive possession of, one (1) pound of marijuana or more, or twenty-five (25) marijuana plants or more, as defined in section 37-2701, Idaho Code, is guilty of a felony, which felony shall be known as ‘trafficking in marijuana.’”

1 lb to <5 lb, or 25 to <50 plants1 year$5,00015 years / $50,000

§37-2732B(a)(7): a second trafficking conviction results in a mandatory minimum fixed term twice that otherwise required. §37-2732B(a)(8): adjudication/sentence shall not be suspended, deferred or withheld, nor parole before serving the mandatory minimum; the court shall not retain jurisdiction. §37-2732B(a)(1)(E): weight is weight when seized or as determined as soon as practicable after seizure (under (c), represented weight controls if greater than actual weight).

Second/subsequent offences generally — §37-2739 (search-snippet only, Verify full page): link §37-2739. Snippet text: a person convicted of a second or subsequent offence under this act, who is not subject to a fixed minimum term under §37-2739B, may be imprisoned up to twice the term otherwise authorised, fined up to twice that otherwise authorised, or both; (b) counts a prior conviction under this act or under any US/state statute relating to narcotic drugs, marijuana, depressant, stimulant or hallucinogenic drugs. History: added 1971; am. 1990, ch. 268.

Paraphernalia and public use

§37-2734A — Prohibited acts D (opened directly). Link: §37-2734A. History: added 1980, ch. 388; am. 1990, ch. 311.

“(1) It is unlawful for any person to use, or to possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, conceal, inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body a controlled substance. (2) It is unlawful … to place in any newspaper, magazine, handbill, or other publication any advertisement, knowing, or under circumstances where one reasonably should know, that the purpose … is to promote the sale of objects designed or intended for use as drug paraphernalia. (3) Any person who is in violation … is guilty of a misdemeanor and upon conviction may be imprisoned for not more than one (1) year, fined not more than one thousand dollars ($1,000), or both.”

§37-2734B (delivery/manufacture of paraphernalia): secondary summaries claim a felony up to 9 years / $30,000 — not opened directly, Verify, do not use until opened.

§37-2732C — Using or being under the influence (search-snippet only, Verify full page): it is unlawful on a public roadway, on a public conveyance, on public property or on private property open to the public, to use or be under the influence of a controlled substance specified in §37-2705(b)–(f), except when administered by or under the direction of a person licensed to dispense, prescribe or administer controlled substances; misdemeanor, up to 6 months county jail, up to $1,000, or both; a further provision for an offence within 5 years of two or more separate violations and refusal to complete a licensed drug rehabilitation programme carries a mandatory minimum of 120 days to 1 year. History via snippet: added 1996, ch. 261; am. 2003; am. 2010, ch. 117. This is Idaho’s public-consumption / public-intoxication provision; no separate “public consumption of marijuana” section was found.

1.4 CBD

What CBD products are legal: only zero-THC products, plus the FDA-drug exception. There is no standalone “CBD Act” or CBD registry in Title 37, Chapter 27. CBD legality is derived negatively from §37-2701(u): (i) licensed industrial hemp, (ii) mature-stalk/seed exclusions, (iii) FDA finished-dosage exception (CBD ≤0.1% residual THC; nabiximols). Anything else containing any THC is presumed marijuana.

Official agency position — Idaho Office of Drug Policy, CBD page, updated February 2025, opened directly. Link: ODP CBD page. Accessed 1 November 2026. Verbatim:

“CBD oil is legal in Idaho, provided it is processed from industrial hemp and not from marijuana. It must also have no THC at all. Consequently, hemp-derived CBD products with a THC level of 0.3% are classified as marijuana.” “Licensed handlers may receive raw industrial hemp with up to 0.3% THC, however, throughout processing, licensed handlers in Idaho must dilute the final product to have zero percent THC. State law recognizes any THC (unless operating under a license issued by Idaho State Department of Agriculture, in compliance with Title 22, Chapter 17, IDAPA 02.01.07, and 7 CFR 990) as illegal. Any products found in retail or the marketplace in Idaho must contain zero percent THC as applicable to Idaho laws and subject to law enforcement.” “Epidiolex is only available with a prescription from a licensed healthcare provider.”

Attorney General opinion — LABELLED AS OPINION, NOT LAW: Idaho Attorney General, informal opinion in the 2015 Annual Report, pp. 132–134 (cited via secondary summaries; the AG PDF itself was not opened directly in this run — Verify by opening 2015 AG Annual Report (PDF) before quoting). Substance as consistently summarised: CBD is illegal in almost all cases unless (1) it contains no THC at all, and (2) it is excluded from “marijuana” under §37-2701 (then (t), now (u)) — i.e. derived from mature stalks, stalk fibre, seed oil/cake, other mature-stalk preparations, or sterilised seed incapable of germination. ODP stated in 2019 this 2015 position had not changed; ODP’s February 2025 page restates the zero-THC rule in the same terms. A 2019 statement attributed to AG senior attorney John McKinney (“even a trace amount … makes it illegal”) is a podcast/secondary report, not a formal opinion — Verify, do not cite as an AG opinion. Board of Pharmacy 2019 CBD advisory was not opened — Verify.

Epidiolex / SB 1017 (2021): ODP page (opened): DEA descheduled Epidiolex 6 April 2020; “in 2021, Idaho passed Senate Bill S1017, which removed Epidiolex from the list of Schedule V substances, aligning state regulations with federal law.” Legislature page: S1017 — Senate passed 30–3–2 on 8 February 2021, House passed 43–27–0 on 18 February 2021, signed 26 February 2021, Session Law ch. 11, effective 1 July 2021. Note: S1017’s legislature title is “CONTROLLED SUBSTANCES – Amends existing law to provide for certain synthetic drugs and controlled substances” — it is broader than Epidiolex alone; do not describe it as an “Epidiolex-only” bill without checking its text — Verify text.

Medical CBD provision / expanded-access programme: no current medical CBD registry or expanded-access registry in current statute was found. The 2015 expanded-access programme was by executive order (Governor Otter, for treatment-resistant epilepsy in children, using pharmaceutical-grade Epidiolex in an FDA-approved study; contemporaneous reporting: up to 25 children) — see Part 9 in Run 2 for full history. Its current status after FDA approval (June 2018) and descheduling (April 2020) is Verify.

Out-of-state medical cards: no reciprocity provision was found in Title 37, Chapter 27. Possession under §37-2732(e) has no out-of-state-card exception on its face. This is an absence-of-provision finding — mark Verify if a negative citation is required, and do not cite a non-existent “no reciprocity” section number. Confirm: no out-of-state medical card is recognised unless statute says otherwise, and no such statute was found.

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Hemp definition and THC measurement: see §22-1703 (delta-9, 0.3% dry weight) vs §37-2701(u)(1) (total delta-9 including THCA by decarboxylation, 0.3% dry weight or volume) vs §67-2921(4) (total delta-9 including all THCA). The cultivation/licence definition is the total-THC definition in §37-2701(u); the Title 22 short definition must not be quoted alone as the whole rule.

Delta-8, delta-9, THCA and other hemp-derived THC: there is no Idaho statute that names delta-8, delta-10 or HHC separately. They fall under §37-2705(d)(29) “tetrahydrocannabinols … derivatives, and their isomers” (isomer expressly includes optical, position and geometric isomers for §37-2705(d)). Delta-9 in a finished retail product is illegal if any detectable THC is present, per the ODP zero-THC rule + §37-2701(u) presumption, unless the holder is licensed and the material is licensed hemp in production/processing/transport. THCA is counted toward total delta-9 under §37-2701(u)(1) and §67-2921(4); THCA flower sold at retail is therefore not saved by a “THCA is not THC” argument in Idaho — if total delta-9 (THCA decarboxylated) exceeds 0.3% it is marijuana, and even at/below 0.3% a retail product must still meet the zero-THC retail rule. Synthetic/converted cannabinoids: no separate Idaho ban was found beyond the Schedule I “synthetic equivalents … derivatives, and their isomers” language — Verify whether ISDA retail rulemaking (Docket 02-0107-2601, October 2026, below) adds a synthetic-cannabinoid prohibition.

Attorney General / State Police position (dated, labelled): the AG position is the 2015 informal opinion summarised in §1.4 above (opinion, not law). No separate, dated State Police position statement on delta-8/THCA was opened in this run — Verify. Do not attribute the ODP zero-THC page to the State Police; it is the Office of Drug Policy.

Minimum age, testing, labelling: no minimum-age provision for hemp/CBD retail was found in Title 37, Chapter 27 or Title 22, Chapter 17 as opened. No packaging/labelling/advertising statute for hemp/CBD retail was found in those chapters. Any such rules would be in IDAPA 02.01.07 or in HB 879 retail rulemaking — Verify; do not invent an age (e.g. 21) for Idaho hemp/CBD. The tobacco/e-device age (21) in Part 3 is a different regime and does not apply to hemp/CBD by itself.

Transport rules: see §67-2921 in §1.2 above. Transport is authorised as part of production/processing/transport/research under §22-1705; interstate transport is governed by §67-2921. The 2019 stopgap executive order (EO 2019-13, Governor Little, 19 November 2019 — port-of-entry stop, documentation, consent to inspection/sampling, interstate-highway routing, effective for transport on/after 31 October 2019) predates HB 126 and the state plan; whether any port-of-entry requirement survives is Verify.

State response to the new federal hemp definition and its 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates: none found as of 1 November 2026 — Verify after those dates. Idaho’s retail zero-THC rule is already stricter than a 0.4 mg-per-container federal cap, so the federal change would not liberalise Idaho retail; its effect, if any, would be on licensed cultivation definitions, where Idaho already uses total delta-9 in §37-2701(u). Do not claim Idaho has adopted or rejected the federal definition.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer rules for hemp and CBD

None found in statute for hemp/CBD retail. No packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning or buffer-distance statute for hemp/CBD products was located in Title 37, Chapter 27 or Title 22, Chapter 17 as opened. Delivery of a hemp/CBD product that contains any THC would be delivery of marijuana under §37-2732(a) unless the licensed-hemp exception applies. Any future packaging/labelling/advertising rules would be in IDAPA 02.01.07 or HB 879 rulemaking — Verify. Local zoning/buffers are covered in Part 3; the only buffer located is a tobacco-retail buffer in Boise, not a hemp/CBD buffer (see Part 3).

1.7 Seed-to-sale / product tracking

None found. No seed-to-sale or product-tracking system for hemp or CBD was located in Idaho statute or in the ISDA programme summaries opened. ISDA tracks producers/handlers by licence, plot/lot registration and pre-harvest sampling (see Part 4 and, in Run 2, Part 11), which is not a retail seed-to-sale tracking system. Mark any claim of a seed-to-sale system as Verify before use.

1.8 Employment and drug testing protections

None found for cannabis/hemp in this run. No statute was located in Title 37, Chapter 27 or Title 22, Chapter 17 providing employment or drug-testing protections for marijuana, CBD or hemp use, and no such protection can be inferred from the absence of a medical programme. General Idaho employment/drug-testing law is outside Parts 0–6 and is not stated here; if it is needed, it must be researched separately and marked Verify until opened. Impaired-driving, expungement and related consumer/social rules are in Run 2, Part 12, not here.

Ballotpedia is secondary and is used here only for initiative-status pointers, not for law.

PART 2: TAXES

2.1 Sales tax — general rule, hemp/CBD treatment

Statewide rate 6%. Idaho Code §63-3619 (opened via legislature statute page): “An excise tax is hereby imposed upon each sale at retail at the rate of six percent (6%) of the sales price of all retail sales subject to taxation under this chapter.” History: added 2003, ch. 318; amended 2006, 1st Ex. Sess., ch. 1. Link: §63-3619. Accessed 1 November 2026.

No cannabis-specific sales/excise tax exists in Idaho — there is no legal marijuana sale to tax. The only marijuana-related excise tax is the illegal-drug stamp tax below.

Hemp/CBD: no Idaho State Tax Commission hemp- or CBD-specific sales-tax guidance, and no hemp/CBD-specific exemption, was located in the sources opened for this run. The general rule therefore is the only official basis available: retail sales of tangible personal property are taxable at 6% unless an exemption in Title 63, Chapter 36 applies. Verify: product-specific classification (e.g. whether a given CBD/hemp product is treated as food, a drug/supplement, or ordinary tangible property, and whether any food/drug exemption could apply) was not confirmed by a Tax Commission ruling or guidance opened here. Do not present “CBD is taxable at 6%” as a Tax Commission ruling — it is the general-rule inference only. Secondary, non-official summaries that list Idaho hemp at “6% sales & use tax” were seen but are not relied on for this point. A seller’s permit is obtained through the Idaho Business Registration system with the Tax Commission; no fee figure for the seller’s permit itself was confirmed from an official page opened here — Verify.

2.2 Illegal drug stamp tax — IN FORCE on the statute books

Idaho Code Title 63, Chapter 42, Illegal Drug Stamp Tax Act (§§63-4201 to 63-4211).

§63-4203, opened directly. Link: §63-4203. Accessed 1 November 2026. History: added 1989, ch. 144; amended 1990, ch. 179; amended 1993, ch. 9. No repeal was shown on the page opened.

“Every person who in violation of Idaho law possesses a controlled substance shall be liable for payment of an excise tax on all of the controlled substance possessed including the amount below any threshold provided in section 63-4202.” Rates, verbatim from the opened page: Marijuana: $3.50 per gram or portion of a gram; Other controlled substance sold by weight: $200 per gram or portion; Not sold by weight: $2,000 per 50 dosage units or portion; Growing marijuana plant: $775 per plant, with a $775 credit against tax on marijuana harvested from that plant if evidence of payment is permanently affixed to the harvested marijuana.

§63-4202 thresholds (search-result text only — Verify on direct open): “controlled substance” for this chapter means possession in violation of Idaho law of more than 42½ grams of marijuana, 1+ growing marijuana plants, 7+ grams of any other controlled substance sold by weight, or 10+ dosage units not sold by weight. Link: §63-4202. §63-4207 civil/criminal penalty (search-result text only — Verify): 100% civil penalty in addition to the tax, plus criminal liability to the same extent as possession under §37-2732 for distributing/possessing without stamps. §63-4204 (search-result text only — Verify): stamps/labels must be purchased from the Commission at 100% of face value. Link: §63-4204.

Status conclusion: the Act remains in the current published Idaho Code as of the 1 November 2026 check. Whether the Commission currently sells stamps in practice, and any revenue collected under it, was not found in an official source opened here — Unavailable / Verify. Do not assume stamps are routinely issued; the tax operates in practice mainly as a jeopardy assessment after seizure (see the Garcia v. State Tax Commission line of cases, secondary summary only).

2.3 Hemp-specific fees — licence/inspection fees, not a tax

See Part 4 for the full table. In brief, ISDA producer/handler fees under IDAPA 02.01.07.301: Producer $100 application + $500 annual licence + $250/lot pre-harvest inspection + $35/hr travel + shipping; Handler $100 application + $1,000 annual licence + $500/site annual inspection + $35/hr travel. No separate hemp excise tax, hemp sales tax, or hemp product tax was located in Idaho law.

2.4 Local taxes

No general county or city sales tax in Idaho. Local sales tax is confined to (a) resort-city local-option taxes and (b) auditorium-district taxes on lodging.

Resort-city local-option tax — official Tax Commission list, opened directly, updated 15 October 2026. Link: Tax Commission, City Sales Tax. Accessed 1 November 2026. The page states: “Some Idaho resort cities have a local sales tax in addition to the state sales tax… Resort cities have a choice in what’s taxed and can include everything that’s subject to the state sales tax. Some, but not all, choose to limit the local sales tax to lodging, alcohol by the drink, and restaurant food.” The page gives city names and phone numbers only, no rates and no tax-base table; rates must be confirmed city by city. Full list on that page (23 cities): Bellevue, Bonners Ferry, Cascade, Crouch, Donnelly, Driggs, Hailey, Harrison, Irwin, Kellogg, Ketchum, Lava Hot Springs, Mackay, McCall, Ponderay, Riggins, Salmon, Sandpoint, Stanley, Sun Valley, Swan Valley, Tetonia, Victor.

Consequence for the jurisdictions in Part 3: of Boise/Ada, Meridian, Nampa/Caldwell/Canyon, Idaho Falls/Bonneville, Pocatello/Bannock, Coeur d’Alene/Kootenai, Twin Falls, Lewiston/Nez Perce, Post Falls, Moscow/Latah, Sandpoint/Bonner, Rexburg/Madison, Fruitland/Payette — only Sandpoint appears on the Tax Commission resort-city list. The others have no local-option sales tax on that official list. Sandpoint’s rate (1%, for a 7% combined rate) appears only in secondary sales-tax compilations dated October 2026 — Verify with the City of Sandpoint before publishing a rate.

Auditorium-district tax and 2% travel & convention tax on lodging (≤30-day stays): widely described in secondary guides (Boise, Nampa — Nampa district said to come under Tax Commission administration 1 April 2026 — Idaho Falls, Pocatello-Chubbuck, McCall area districts; 5% district rate in Boise/Nampa in those guides). No Tax Commission auditorium-district page was opened in this run — Verify before using any district rate or boundary. These are lodging taxes, not hemp/CBD product taxes, and are included here only because they affect the local-tax picture for a retail premises.

No local cannabis/hemp tax was located in any jurisdiction checked. Idaho Code §39-5713 (tobacco/e-device context, see Part 3) expressly bars a local unit from imposing a tax or fee on tobacco products or electronic smoking devices — that is a tobacco provision, not a hemp provision, and must not be cited as a hemp tax pre-emption.

2.5 Income tax, 280E, and Idaho conformity — §63-3004

Idaho uses static (fixed-date) conformity. Idaho Code §63-3004 defines “Internal Revenue Code” as the IRC as amended and in effect on a specified 1 January date, updated annually.

2026 conformity bill — LAW, confirmed on the official 2026 legislation index opened directly: H0559 (2026), “Internal revenue code conformity” — listed as LAW (+) on 2026 legislation index. Bill text (search result): H0559 amends §63-3004 to read “the Internal Revenue Code as amended and in effect on the first day of January 2025 2026” (with the §85 exception as in effect 1 January 2020 and a §174 research-expenditure carve-out). Text PDF: H0559 (PDF). The earlier duplicate 2026 conformity bill H0519 is shown on the same index only as “H Rev/Tax” (not LAW) — H0559 is the enacted vehicle. Do not cite H0519 as law. Prior year for comparison: H0003 (2025) updated conformity to 1 January 2025 — H0003 SOP (PDF).

No Idaho-specific 280E decoupling (or coupling) statute was located. No Idaho statute or Tax Commission guidance that mentions IRC §280E by number was found in the sources opened here — Verify by a full-text search of Title 63, Chapter 30 before stating this as exhaustive.

Practical consequence for Idaho, on the federal baseline given: Idaho has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme, so there is no Idaho licensee to whom the federal Schedule III / 280E relief for state-licensed medical activity could apply. Marijuana in Idaho therefore remains Schedule I for this purpose, and any (illegal) adult-use/marijuana activity remains fully subject to federal §280E. Licensed Idaho hemp businesses are not “trafficking in Schedule I/II substances” by virtue of lawful hemp; §280E is not a hemp issue on the facts as stated. This is a legal inference from the federal baseline, not an Idaho ruling — Verify with a tax adviser / Tax Commission guidance if the dossier states it as advice.

Timing trap — flag as Verify, do not overstate: because Idaho conformity is static to 1 January 2026, and the federal split-scheduling order is effective 28 April 2026, whether a post-1-January federal IRC change flows into an Idaho return before the next annual conformity bill is a question for the 2027 conformity bill and Tax Commission guidance. It does not change the Idaho conclusion above (no Idaho medical licensees exist either way), but it must not be presented as “Idaho automatically follows the April 2026 federal change.” Idaho income-tax rate context (secondary, for scale only): flat 5.3% for 2025 and 2026 under §63-3024, per secondary summaries — Verify on the Tax Commission site before use.

2.6 Official revenue figures

None located for this run: no official figure for sales-tax revenue attributable to hemp/CBD products (the Tax Commission does not publish a hemp/CBD sales-tax line in any source opened here); no official figure for illegal-drug stamp-tax collections; no official figure for ISDA hemp fee revenue. All Unavailable. Do not use secondary “hemp tax by state” tables as revenue sources; they give rates, not collections. Do not estimate revenue from licence counts × fees — that would ignore inspections, late fees and non-renewals.

PART 3: LOCAL RULES

3.1 Authority and pre-emption — the framework

No statewide official list of local hemp/CBD rules exists. No ISDA, Tax Commission, or Association of Idaho Cities page listing hemp/CBD ordinances by city/county was located. This section therefore reports (a) the state-law pre-emption rules that do exist, (b) the general business-licence position, and (c) the jurisdictions checked, with the honest limit that absence of a located ordinance is not proof none exists in a given city’s code.

Cities/counties retain zoning and land-use authority. The clearest statutory statement is in the tobacco context and is routinely cited for the general principle: Idaho Code §39-5713 (opened via search result; legislature page §39-5713): “No local unit of government may adopt or enforce requirements for the regulation, marketing, or sale of tobacco products or electronic smoking devices that are more restrictive than or in addition to this chapter. No local unit of government may impose or enforce a tax or fee on tobacco products or electronic smoking devices… Nothing in this section shall preclude or preempt any local, county, or municipal government from exercising its lawful authority to regulate zoning or land use.” History: added 1998; amended 2012 and 2022 (ch. 191). Do not cite §39-5713 as a hemp pre-emption rule. It governs tobacco/e-devices. For hemp/CBD retail there is no equivalent hemp-specific local pre-emption statute located — a city could in principle use zoning/land-use power over a hemp/CBD retail use, and a hemp retail licence (if ever enacted — see HB 771 in Part 4) would be a state licence, not a local pre-emption.

General business licensing — Idaho has no statewide general business licence, and most large cities have none either. Secondary compilations (not official city pages) consistently report: Boise — no general business licence, only activity-specific licences; Meridian — no general business licence; Nampa — no general business licence, only activity-specific licences (Nampa in-home business registration, $100 one-time, is reported secondarily for home-based businesses — Verify with the City of Nampa). Idaho Falls, Coeur d’Alene, Pocatello and Twin Falls are likewise described secondarily as activity-specific only. All of these “no general licence” points are Verify against each city clerk’s current list before the dossier states them as fact, because they rest on secondary compilations, not on city pages opened here.

What does exist at local level for a retail shop in practice: (i) state seller’s permit (Tax Commission), (ii) Secretary of State entity registration, (iii) city/county zoning compliance and any building/fire permits for fit-out, (iv) health-district food-establishment permits if food is handled, (v) city alcohol licence only if alcohol is sold. None of these is hemp-specific.

Tobacco / vape-shop overlay (relevant because many CBD/hemp shops also sell vape products): Idaho Code Title 39, Chapter 57 (Prevention of Minors’ Access to Tobacco Products or Electronic Smoking Devices Act): retailers selling tobacco or e-devices must hold a state permit from the Department of Health and Welfare — described on the Department’s page, opened via search result: DHW Tobacco Permits / Electronic Smoking Device Information — permit required since 1 July 2020; sale to under-21s prohibited. That permit is the only retail tobacco/e-device permit required (§39-5704, search-result text). It is not a hemp/CBD licence and does not authorise sale of any THC product. Boise repealed its local tobacco ordinances in July 2022 after the 2022 state-law change barring more-restrictive local tobacco/e-device rules (Idaho Tobacco Project newsletter, August 2022, secondary) — consistent with §39-5713 above.

Boise zoning for “Retail Tobacco Business” (Boise City Code §11-06-05, via American Legal code library, search result): conditional-use approval requires the business not be within 300 feet of a building where public school activities are conducted, and not within 150 feet of a hospital or a room where an organised religion conducts regular religious activities (text truncated in the search result — Verify the full section before quoting). This is a tobacco-retail buffer, not a hemp/CBD buffer, and is included only so the dossier does not mistake it for one.

3.2 Zoning, moratoriums, caps, bans — findings

No hemp- or CBD-specific city/county moratorium, cap, or ban was located in any jurisdiction checked for this run. No hemp-specific zoning classification or buffer distance was located in any city/county code searched. Hemp cultivation/processing is regulated at state level by ISDA (plot/lot registration, inspections); a local jurisdiction would treat a hemp facility under its ordinary agricultural, industrial, or commercial zoning, subject to its ordinary use tables — Verify parcel-by-parcel with the relevant planning department; do not state a hemp zoning category for any city.

3.3 Jurisdictions checked (minimum list required, plus resort-tax cross-check)

Boise / Ada County; Meridian; Nampa and Caldwell / Canyon County; Idaho Falls / Bonneville County; Pocatello / Bannock County; Coeur d’Alene / Kootenai County; Twin Falls (city and county); Lewiston / Nez Perce County; Post Falls; Moscow / Latah County; Sandpoint / Bonner County; Rexburg / Madison County; Fruitland and Payette / Payette County. Method: checked via the official Tax Commission resort-city list (Part 2.4), ISDA licensee geography (Part 5), and web/code searches for hemp/CBD/smoke-shop ordinances. No individual city code was opened page-by-page for all 15+ jurisdictions in this run — the “none located” finding is therefore a search finding, marked Verify, not a certified code-by-code audit. A certified local audit needs each city/county code opened individually. Same-name discipline was applied throughout (see cover note).
