3.5 Business concentration (official sources only)

ISDA licensee geography is the only official concentration evidence in this run (Part 5 table). 2026 licensees are in eastern/south-eastern Idaho (Bonneville, Jefferson, Bingham, Jerome, Twin Falls counties) plus Ada (Garden City), Boundary (Naples), Custer/Lemhi (May), and a single Ada producer. That is a description of the ISDA table, not a ranking — no cluster ranking, heat map, or “hemp capital” claim is made, and no official source stating where hemp retail shops concentrate was located. Retail shops are not ISDA-licensed at present (see Part 4), so no official retail concentration data exists. Do not estimate or rank clusters yourself.

PART 4: LICENCE TYPES

4.1 State cannabis licences — NONE

Idaho issues NO cannabis (marijuana) licences of any kind. No medical programme, no adult-use programme, no dispensary, cultivation, processing, testing, or transport licence for marijuana exists under Idaho law. Any business claiming an Idaho cannabis licence is not describing a state-issued licence. This is consistent with the federal-baseline note: because Idaho has no state-licensed medical marijuana programme, marijuana remains Schedule I in Idaho under the split scheduling, and the “state-licensed” question for tribes is a separate Verify item for Part 10, not a state licence.

4.2 Hemp licences and permits — current (ISDA, USDA-approved state plan)

Idaho operates its own USDA-approved state plan — it does not use the USDA direct producer-licence programme. Evidence: USDA Agricultural Marketing Service “List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans” lists “Idaho — ID Approved Plan” (search-result page, updated 17 February 2026 per that page — Verify on direct open): USDA AMS state/tribal plan review. ISDA hemp programme hub (search result, not opened in full in this run — Verify): ISDA hemp hub.

Licence types in force — exactly two:

LicenceWhat it allows (per ISDA/IDAPA summaries)Fees — IDAPA 02.01.07.301 + ISDA Applications pageRequirements (summary)
Producer (Grower)Plant, propagate, produce hemp; market the crop including seed. Pre-harvest lot inspection required.Application $100; annual licence $500; late fee $250 if outside the application window; modification $250; pre-harvest/other inspections $250/lot + $35/hr travel + actual shipping costs. All fees non-refundable.Apply via ISDA online portal; application period 1 September–31 December for the next calendar year; licences valid 1 January (or date of issue) to 31 December, expiring 11:59 p.m. 31 December; fingerprint criminal-history background check (Idaho State Police/FBI) for applicant and key participants; felony drug conviction in the past 10 years is disqualifying (per programme summaries — Verify the current rule text); land/plot and lot registration; testing by DEA-registered lab rules (see Run 2, Part 11).
Handler (Processor)Handle/process raw hemp, including seed, into other materials; does not authorise growing. Handler annual site inspection required.Application $100; annual licence $1,000; late fee $250; modification $500; annual site/other inspections $500/site + $35/hr travel. All fees non-refundable.Same application window, validity, and background-check framework as Producer.
Both licences togetherA person seeking to produce and handle needs both licence types; the annual application fee is charged only for the first licence type (IDAPA 02.01.07.301(a)).Combined first-year ISDA fee floor, 1 lot / 1 site: $100 + $500 + $1,000 + $250 + $500 = $2,350, plus travel, shipping, background-check and testing costs paid directly to the provider.—

Official sources opened for the fees: ISDA Applications & Licenses page, opened directly (page last updated 25 February 2025 on the page itself; page text still refers to the 2025 licence year, so the fee table should be re-confirmed for the 2027 cycle before republication): ISDA Applications & Licenses. IDAPA 02.01.07.301, Fees (Cornell mirror of the rule, opened directly): IDAPA 02.01.07.301 (Cornell) — identical fee table, plus “Licensees pay the costs of background checks and required testing directly to the entity providing the service.” Tribal-land note on the ISDA page (verbatim): “If your property is within any tribal boundary within the state of Idaho, please do not apply for a hemp license with ISDA. Please contact ISDA with additional information regarding your property.”

Transport — NO separate ISDA transport permit or transport fee in the fee table. Transport is authorised as part of production/processing/transport/research under Idaho Code §22-1705, and interstate transport is governed by Idaho Code §67-2921 (search-result text only — Verify on direct open): §67-2921. The 2019 stopgap (Governor’s Executive Order 2019-13, port-of-entry declaration) predates the 2021 hemp law and the state plan; whether any port-of-entry requirement survives under §67-2921 / current ISDA rules was not confirmed from a current official page opened here — Verify. Do not state a current “transport permit” requirement or fee. A person transporting on behalf of a licensee, at the licensee’s direction, is excepted from the “knowing possession without a licence” offence in §67-2921(5) per the search-result text — Verify full text.

Retail — NO current retail licence. No licence is currently required for selling hemp or CBD products in Idaho (per ISDA programme summaries; the ISDA fee table contains no retail fee). This is the current position unless and until a retail bill is enacted — see the enacted-but-not-yet-effective / proposed items below, which must not be conflated with current law. There is no enacted-but-not-yet-effective retail licence in force: HB 879 (2026) creates no retail licence or fee; HB 771 (2026) and HB 478 (2025) are not law (below).

Enacted 2026 hemp changes that affect licensing scope: HB 772 (2026) — LAW, signed 2 April 2026, Session Law ch. 293, effective 1 July 2026 (official bill status page: HB 772) — amends §22-1705 on negligent violations: a producer growing solely for grain/fibre, who made reasonable efforts to stay within the acceptable THC level and whose hemp does not exceed 1% total THC dry weight, does not commit a negligent violation. Current §22-1705 text (search result): §22-1705. HB 879 (2026) — LAW, Session Law ch. 292 (official 2026 index: “H0879 — Industrial hemp — LAW +”; subject index: “Industrial hemp, human ingestion, inhalation — H0879 – Ch.292”: 2026 index) — amends §22-1705(4) so establishments offering hemp products intended for human ingestion or inhalation (excluding nonviable hulled hemp seed, hemp seed protein powder, and hemp seed oil) are subject to Chapter 17, and such products must meet §§37-2701(u) and 37-2705(d)(29)(i). Secondary summaries stress HB 879 creates no new retail licence and no new fee — Verify against the enrolled text before the dossier repeats that, but do not present HB 879 as a retail-licensing law.

HB 478 (2025) — DEAD, not law. Introduced 1 April 2025, status “Intro Sine Die” / “H Filed Office Chief Clerk” on the official 2025 index; its text would have added §22-1708 with a $600 retail licence and a ban on cannabinoid food/drink products, effective 1 January 2027 in the introduced text. Do not cite HB 478 as enacted or as “enacted but not yet effective.” Text PDF (for the dead-bill record only): HB 478 (PDF). HB 771 (2026) — NOT LAW. “Hemp products, retail,” referred to House Agricultural Affairs 26 February 2026; the official 2026 index lists it as “H Agric Aff” with no LAW marker. Its introduced text would have added §22-1708: no cannabinoids except naturally derived cannabidiol in consumable/personal-use products; every retail store selling hemp products to hold a 1-year ISDA licence at $600/year (+ $25 per additional store under one licence in secondary summaries of the text); civil penalties up to $10,000 per violation in secondary summaries. This $600 retail fee is PROPOSED ONLY. It must not appear in any “current fees” table. Text PDF: HB 771 (PDF); SOP: HB 771 SOP (PDF).

Research / other permits: no separate ISDA research licence type was confirmed in the fee table opened; research is listed in §22-1705(1) as an authorised activity subject to the chapter, the state plan and the 2018 farm bill, and University of Idaho entries appear on the ISDA licensee list as Producer/Handler (see Part 5). Whether a distinct research permit exists in current IDAPA 02.01.07 is Verify — it is not listed as a separate current licence type here. Full licence-type detail, including research, is in Run 2, Part 7.

4.3 Proposed medical cannabis initiative — licence types ONLY as PROPOSED

Measure: Idaho Medical Cannabis Act — citizen initiative, sponsor Natural Medicine Alliance of Idaho. Status: FAILED TO QUALIFY for the 3 November 2026 ballot. Official Secretary of State announcement, 14 July 2026 (press release, search result): “The Idaho Medical Cannabis Act initiative did not qualify for the November ballot after failing to submit the required number of valid petition signatures, both in total number of signatures and required legislative districts.” Required: 70,725 valid signatures including 6% of registered voters in at least 18 of 35 legislative districts. Campaign had claimed 150,000+ raw signatures submitted. Press release: Secretary of State 2026 ballot initiatives. The initiative therefore creates no licence, no programme, and no right in current law. Everything below is PROPOSED ONLY and must be labelled as such.

PROPOSED licence type (single, vertically integrated): “Medical Cannabis Production License” — per the sponsor’s own summary (Natural Medicine Alliance) and contemporaneous reporting (secondary): one licence type covering grow, process, distribute, and sell to cardholders; initially 3 production licences statewide, expandable to 6 total as population grows (sponsor: +1 licence per 650,000 population increase — Verify against the filed initiative text); each licensee: up to 2 cultivation/production facilities and up to 6 retail locations; online ordering for pickup and delivery proposed; proposed application prerequisites reported: an Idaho hemp licence in good standing, an operating plan, background checks, and a pharmacist engaged for oversight of dispensing, storing, distributing, and selling; proposed patient instrument: Medical Cannabis Card for a “substantial health condition”; proposed purchase limits reported as 113 g smokeable cannabis or 20 g THC extract for vaping per month, edibles 10 mg THC per serving — all PROPOSED, all Verify against the filed text; none is law. The proposal would also have reclassified cannabis to Schedule II under state law — PROPOSED, and distinct from the federal Schedule III split-scheduling baseline; do not merge them. Earlier initiative attempts (2020 Idaho Cannabis Coalition/Legalize Idaho; 2022 Kind Idaho) likewise failed to qualify — see Run 2, Part 9. No licence type from any earlier attempt is current law.

4.4 Social equity

No social-equity programme exists in Idaho for cannabis or hemp — no statute, rule, or ISDA programme providing equity licensing, fee waivers, priority, or restorative-justice licensing was located. No social-equity provision was located in the proposed Idaho Medical Cannabis Act summaries either — one secondary analysis states expressly that “it does not appear that there would be any equity-centered reforms” in the proposal. Verify against the filed initiative text before stating this as definitive, but there is no equity programme to describe as current or as proposed on the evidence found. The absence of equity provisions should be stated plainly (“None — current or proposed, on sources located”) rather than filled with another state’s model.

4.5 Estimated startup costs — hemp production and processing in Idaho (official floor vs. non-official estimates, kept apart)

Official, calculable floor (ISDA fees only — not a full startup cost): Producer, 1 lot, in-window: $100 + $500 + $250 = $850, plus $35/hr travel to/from site, actual sample-shipping costs, background-check cost, and testing cost (both paid directly to the provider; amounts not stated on the ISDA page — Verify current lab/background-check prices). Handler, 1 site, in-window: $100 + $1,000 + $500 = $1,600, plus travel. Producer + Handler, 1 lot / 1 site: $2,350 on the same basis (application fee charged once). Late application adds $250 per licence type (per the ISDA table’s late-fee row; whether the late fee is per licence or per application for a dual applicant is Verify).

Non-official estimates — clearly marked, dated, and NOT Idaho-official: Idaho Farm Bureau (November 2021, on programme launch): “For a farmer, the total application and program costs will run about $1,000 per year. Costs include $100 for the application, $500 for a license and $250 per lot for pre-harvest inspections.” Source: Idaho Farm Bureau, Nov 2021 — this is a 2021 programme-cost estimate (1 lot), not a whole-farm startup cost; it excludes seed, land, equipment, labour, water, and processing. Northwest Center for Alternatives to Pesticides, hemp page (2022 inaugural-season material): grower estimates quoted as planting “cheaper than corn, at only $60 an acre” and revenue “$500–600 per acre,” with the caveat that end use, equipment, and market are unsettled. Source: NCAP hemp page — grower anecdote, 2022, not an ISDA or University of Idaho budget, and not a startup total.

No Idaho-specific, current, published whole-business startup estimate for hemp production or for hemp processing was located (no University of Idaho Extension hemp enterprise budget, no ISDA startup guide, no processor capex figure). Unavailable — do not substitute a national or other-state figure without labelling it as such. Any processing-plant figure (extraction, decortication, building-materials manufacturing) would be an order of magnitude above the licence-fee floor and is entirely Verify / not estimated here.

PART 5: LICENCE DATA SOURCES

Do NOT try to list every licence holder here. This part inventories every source of licence-holder data found. The full 2026 ISDA licensee list is reproduced in the market table in Part 6 only to the extent needed to count and locate licensees; Part 5 itself is the source inventory. No other licence-holder list is reproduced in full.

5.1 ISDA hemp licence list — the primary source

  • URL (opened directly, 1 November 2026): ISDA Current & Past Idaho Hemp Licensees
  • Holder type: ISDA Producer, Handler, and Producer/Handler licensees.
  • Format: HTML table on a web page. No CSV, XLSX, or PDF download link was shown on the page opened, and no export/download button was identified. Search-only in the sense that the page itself is the list; there is no bulk-download file to cite. Exact download link / file name: NONE EXISTS on the evidence of the opened page.
  • Update frequency as stated: not stated on the page. The page is organised by licence year and carries “Approximate Acreage Approved to Date” for the current year, implying in-year updating, but no update schedule is published on the page — Verify with ISDA.
  • Latest data date on the page: licence year 1 January 2026 – 31 December 2026, “Approximate Acreage Approved to Date for 2026 Production: 233” (acres, as labelled “Acreage”).
  • Fields available: Business Name | License Type | City | County. No licence number, address, acreage per licensee, lot list, contact, or expiry date is shown. One 2026 row is shown as “Withheld at Requested Licensee” (Handler, Twin Falls) — the page withholds that name at the licensee’s request, a limitation to note.
  • Limitations: business name only (no DBA/trade-name split consistently); county/city as listed by ISDA; past years are retained on the same page (2022–2025 shown below the current year), so a reader must not add years together — a licensee appearing in multiple years is the same business renewing annually.
  • Counts derived by counting the opened table (not ISDA-stated totals — ISDA publishes no total on the page): 2026: 11 rows — Producer-only 7 (Burtenshaw Ag Inc, Idaho Falls/Bonneville; Double Springs Ranch [ISDA lists May under both Custer and Lemhi in the same table — Verify county with ISDA; reproduced as printed, not silently corrected] LLC, May/Custer; Garden City Greens LLC, Garden City/Ada; George Miller, May/Lemhi; Idaho Premium Hay Farms & IDA Best Hay, Roberts/Jefferson; Krown Farms, Aberdeen/Bingham; Seamons Farms LLC, Jerome/Jerome); Handler-only 3 (Hempitecture INC, Jerome/Jerome; Withheld at Requested Licensee, Twin Falls/Twin Falls; Idaho Hemp Processing LLC, Roberts/Jefferson); Producer/Handler 1 (Essential Oil Research Farm LLC, Naples/Boundary). Approximate approved acreage 233. 2025: 16 rows, approximate acreage 1,135. 2024: 23 rows, approximate acreage 1,860. 2023: 23 rows (including 3 University of Idaho entries), approximate acreage 1,248. 2022: 23 rows, approximate acreage 458. These year-on-year row counts were re-counted from the table as opened directly for this dossier on 1 November 2026; an earlier subagent count of 22 for 2022 was a miscount and is corrected here to 23. The sharp 2025→2026 fall (16→11 rows; 1,135→233 acres) is as printed on the page — the page does not state a reason; do not attribute it to demand, enforcement, or any single cause without a further ISDA source.

5.2 ISDA applications / portal — licence administration, not a public list

  • URL (opened directly): ISDA Applications & Licenses / portal
  • Holder type / function: new and renewing Producer/Handler applicants; licence management, inspection requests, payments.
  • Format: web portal (account required to apply/manage). No public download, no public search of applicants. The public list is the §5.1 page above; the portal itself is not a data source for third-party research.
  • Update frequency / latest date: page footer “last updated: February 25, 2025” — the fee table on it matches IDAPA 02.01.07.301, but the page text still refers to the 2025 licence year. Verify the portal/fee table for the 2027 cycle.

5.3 Idaho State Police / ISAC enforcement data — dashboard, not a licence list

  • URL: Idaho Statistical Analysis Center (ISAC), Idaho State Police (search-result page; dashboard/publication library not opened in full in this run — Verify export options)
  • What it holds: Idaho Incident-Based Reporting System (IIBRS) crime data, including the annual brief Drug & Alcohol Related Crime Trends (2025 edition released 10 September 2026, per ISP announcement ISP announcement — 2025 findings in that announcement: 25% of IIBRS offences were drug/alcohol offences; marijuana was seized in 58% of drug arrests; 44% physical-arrest rate for marijuana arrests).
  • Format: interactive dashboard (“Crime in Idaho Data Dashboard”) plus PDF briefs in a publication library, per ISP’s description. No bulk CSV download link was confirmed in this run, and no hemp-specific seizure or hemp-transport dataset was located. ISP data is enforcement data, not licence-holder data — it cannot be used to list hemp licensees, and marijuana seizure figures must not be presented as hemp figures.
  • Limitations: IIBRS is incident-based and agency-reported; county-level arrest rates by drug type are in the brief, but a hemp-vs-marijuana split is not a published IIBRS category on the evidence found.

5.4 Idaho Secretary of State — business entity search (SOSBiz)

  • URL (from secondary guides; NOT opened directly in this run — Verify): SOSBiz business search
  • Holder type: all Idaho-registered entities (LLCs, corporations, LPs, LLPs, assumed business names, foreign entities) — not a hemp or cannabis register. A hemp business appears here only if it is a registered entity, and its hemp licence status is not shown.
  • Format: interactive search page (by business name or file number; filters for “starts with/contains,” registered agent, active-only). No bulk download was confirmed. Individual records show form type, filing status, formation date, registered agent, principal address, and filing history including annual-report governors. Filed documents viewable; certified copies $10 (secondary).
  • Limitations: search-only, one entity at a time; no NAICS/hemp filter; a DBA/assumed name and a legal entity name can differ; an active SOSBiz record proves entity registration, not ISDA licensure, and vice versa.

5.5 USDA hemp records for Idaho

  • USDA AMS state-plan list (search result; NOT opened directly — Verify): USDA AMS state/tribal plan review — lists Idaho as “ID Approved Plan” with a linked state-plan PDF. No USDA-published Idaho licensee list was located; under an approved state plan, USDA does not issue Idaho producer licences, so the ISDA list (§5.1) is the operative register, not a USDA list.
  • USDA hemp acreage / production reports: USDA NASS hemp production reports cover states with sufficient data; no Idaho-specific USDA acreage figure was confirmed in this run — Verify against the latest NASS hemp report before using any federal acreage figure for Idaho. Do not substitute ISDA’s “approximate acreage approved” for USDA harvested-acreage data; they measure different things.

5.6 Local (county/city) business licence records

No central or statewide index exists. Records, where they exist, sit with each city clerk (activity-specific licences only in most large cities — see Part 3.1) and, for food handling, the seven public-health districts. What to request, city by city: city clerk licence/permit registers (e.g. Boise City Clerk activity licences; Nampa City Clerk activity licences; Idaho Falls City Clerk licences), plus county planning/zoning permit records for a cultivation/processing site. No published, downloadable local hemp-shop list was located for any of the checked jurisdictions. Practical limit: because Boise, Meridian, and Nampa have no general business licence (secondary — Verify), there is no general local business register to search for “hemp shops” in those cities. A shop would appear in local records only under an activity licence (e.g. tobacco/e-device at state level, food permit at health-district level, building permit) or in SOSBiz / Tax Commission seller-permit records, neither of which is public in bulk. Tribal records: no public tribal hemp/cannabis licence register was located in this run; ISDA expressly directs applicants on tribal land to contact ISDA rather than apply (Part 4.2). Tribal registers are for Run 2, Part 10, from official tribal sources only.

PART 6: MARKET DATA

6.1 Hemp acreage and licence counts by year — ISDA series (opened directly)

Source: ISDA Current & Past Idaho Hemp Licensees, opened directly 1 November 2026. The page heads each year “Approximate Acreage Approved to Date for [year] Production”. Counts below are row-counts from that page on 1 November 2026, split as Producer-only / Producer+Handler / Handler-only. “Approved acreage” is NOT the same measure as USDA planted/harvested acreage — see §6.2 conflicts. No 2021 production year exists: HB 126 was signed April 2021 and the first commercial production year was 2022. An early-2022 report (KISU, 25 January 2022, secondary) said only 8 licences had then been fully granted (4 handlers, 3 producers, 1 handler/producer) out of about 60 applications started — that is a mid-window snapshot, not a year-end total. Do not use it as the 2022 total. Source: KISU, 25 Jan 2022.

The same secondary page’s July 2025 snapshot (12 producers, 2 handlers, 1 producer/handler = 15, on about 1,782 acres) does NOT match the final ISDA 2025 list (16 licensees, 1,135 approved acres). 2026 collapse — secondary, dated: HempToday, “Idaho hemp growers slash acreage for 2026 as demand fails to catch up with supply”, published 26 June 2026, updated 13 July 2026, opened via browser (crawled 19 October 2026 copy): “planting just 233 acres in 2026, an 81% drop from last year”, attributing the figure to ISDA, and describing processors/growers “work[ing] through inventories” after a fibre-focused expansion. Its year series (458 in 2022; 1,248 in 2023; 1,860 in 2024; 1,242 in 2025; 233 in 2026) matches ISDA approved acreage for 2022–2024 and 2026 exactly, but gives 1,242 for 2025 vs ISDA’s 1,135 approved — i.e. HempToday appears to use a planted-acreage figure for 2025, not ISDA’s approved-to-date header. Flag the 2025 1,135 vs 1,242 difference in research notes; do not merge the two series without labelling. Source: HempToday, 26 June / 13 July 2026.

6.2 USDA hemp acreage — official series, but state table NOT opened directly

No Idaho-specific USDA production-value figure was found. The USDA National Hemp Report gives national totals and a state table, but the USDA PDF was not opened directly in this session, so all state-level USDA figures below are [search snippet only — Verify] and must not be presented as directly confirmed: USDA via secondary summary (cannabusinessplans.com, citing USDA National Hemp Report): Idaho planted 680 acres in 2022, 445 harvested; planted 1,270 in 2023, 1,260 harvested. USDA 2023 fibre table via NASS Executive Briefing 17 April 2024 (search snippet only): Idaho fibre harvested 1,240 acres, total production 4,154 (1,000 lb), with yield/price withheld as (D). Source: NASS Executive Briefing, 17 Apr 2024 (PDF). PNW Ag Network, 22 April 2025, summarising the 2024 National Hemp Report (released 17 April 2025): “2,300 acres were planted in Idaho last year with 2,100 harvested.” [secondary summary of official report — Verify against the report PDF]. Source: PNW Ag Network, 22 Apr 2025. National context (USDA NASS, 2024 report, 17 April 2025; via secondary summaries): US open-field planted 45,294 acres (+64% vs 2023), harvested 32,694 (+55%); total value $445m open + under protection ($417m open-field); fibre harvested 18,855 acres, 60.4m lb, value $11.2m. 2025 national value $739m (Marijuana Moment, April 2026, reporting USDA NASS 2025 report) — national only, not Idaho. Source: Marijuana Moment, Apr 2026.

CONFLICTING 2024 ACREAGE — must go in research notes, pick one: ISDA licensee page (opened directly) 1,860 = approved acreage to date; PNW Ag / USDA NASS summary (snippet) 2,300 planted / 2,100 harvested = survey planted/harvested; Idaho Farm Bureau (opened directly) 2,668 = stated as acres in 2024, measure not defined in article (series: 459 in 2022, 1,274 in 2023, 2,668 in 2024). Recommendation for this dossier: use ISDA approved acreage as the headline series (it is the only directly opened, licence-linked series), and list the USDA/Farm Bureau figures as conflicting/alternative measures in research notes. Do NOT average them.

Idaho hemp product market size: Unavailable from official sources. No ISDA, Idaho State Tax Commission, or USDA publication giving an Idaho-only hemp/CBD retail or farm-gate dollar total was found in this session. Do not publish an Idaho CBD market size. The only Idaho-linked dollar/volume facts found are the Hempitecture about 1m lb fibre purchase (2024, §6.3) and the national USDA values above.

Destruction / compliance — secondary, dated, Verify if used for law: Cannabis Law Report summary of an AgInfo report (search snippet only): about 44 acres (about 9.5% of 2022 acres) destroyed in 2022 for exceeding the THC threshold, all CBD varieties; no acres destroyed in 2023. Verify against ISDA before stating as fact. Source: Cannabis Law Report.

6.3 Concentration — opened directly, official-adjacent (industry body, not ISDA)

Idaho Farm Bureau Federation, Sean Ellis, “Idaho farmers are starting to figure out hemp” (article covering the 6 February House Agricultural Affairs Committee hemp presentation; opened directly 1 November 2026): “In 2024, Hempitecture purchased and converted almost a million pounds of hemp fiber. About 42 percent of that hemp was grown in Idaho.” In 2023 Hempitecture “sourced almost all of its hemp from Montana and Canada.” Idaho Hemp Processing owner Triston Sponseller “estimates that about 90 percent of the hemp acres grown in Idaho in 2024 were grown for Idaho Hemp Processing” (facility in the Rexburg/Roberts area). ISDA Director Chanel Tewalt quoted: once a product leaves a processing facility it can contain no THC at all; “strictest hemp standards in the entire country.” Source: Idaho Farm Bureau, “Idaho farmers are starting to figure out hemp”. Do NOT rank clusters yourself: the only concentration statement supportable from an opened source is the Sponseller 90%-for-one-processor claim above, plus the ISDA list’s geographic spread (Jerome/Twin Falls, Roberts/Jefferson, May/Custer, Aberdeen/Bingham, Idaho Falls/Bonneville). No official ISDA concentration-by-county table was found.

6.4 Official Idaho price data for hemp/CBD

None found. Unavailable. No Idaho official price series (ISDA, USDA AMS, Idaho Department of Agriculture market news) for hemp biomass, fibre, grain, or CBD retail was located. National proxy only (official, via search snippet — Verify against PDF before quoting as confirmed): USDA Economic Research Service, “U.S. Hemp Fiber Market Outlook”, approved 15 September 2026: US fibre hemp value $13.5m in 2025 (2% of $646m open-field total); average price received for fibre 2023–2025 was $0.24/lb, vs floral hemp $24.50/lb; 2024 fibre value $11.2m at about $0.19/lb implied. This is a NATIONAL price, not an Idaho price, and must be labelled as such. Source: USDA ERS, U.S. Hemp Fiber Market Outlook (PDF). Do not use commercial “hemp market size” forecast sites as Idaho prices — they are not Idaho data.

6.5 Idaho residents buying out of state — border data

6.5.1 Oregon — Ontario, OR (NOT Ontario, Canada) — strongest data, but Idaho-share is an estimate, not a registry

Distinguish: Ontario, OR is in Malheur County, OR, on the Snake River / I-84, about 50–55 miles west of Boise; Fruitland and Payette, ID are the Idaho-side neighbours. Ontario, Canada is a different province/country and has nothing to do with these figures.

Official source (found via search, NOT opened directly — Verify before quoting as confirmed): City of Ontario, OR, FY 2026–27 Budget Message (PDF): “Local 3% tax on the marijuana sold in Ontario continues to bring in a large amount of revenue… We are continuing to estimate a total of $2.5 million of revenues in this 26-27 budget. At this point, it looks like the tax revenue will come in higher than the budgeted $2.5 million in 25-26 fiscal year.” Marijuana tax is part of General Fund revenues. Source: City of Ontario, OR FY 2026–27 Budget Message (PDF) — open the PDF and confirm the $2.5m line before finalising.

Official testimony (Oregon Legislature, 2022 session — dated, not a sales registry): City of Ontario testimony in support of SB 1506 (Oregon Legislature, 2022): Ontario “is the second largest retailer of cannabis behind only Portland and accounts for 10% of all Oregon sales. Ontario cannabis retailers are grossing approximately $100 million in sales and 892,857 transactions per year. That’s nearly 2,500 sales daily with 90% of those sales being from citizens of Idaho.” Average transaction $112. SB 1506 would have let Ontario voters impose up to 10% local tax (vs then-current 3%). Source: Ontario testimony, SB 1506 (2022). Basis: city estimate/testimony, not OLCC purchaser-residency data. OLCC does not publish buyer state-of-residence. Label the 90% as a city/operator estimate.

Recent secondary, dated: Portland Monthly, “How a Tiny Town in Eastern Oregon Became a Destination for Weed” (search result last updated about 7 days before 1 November 2026; article text found via search): Ontario has “at least a dozen dispensaries”; “In 2023, sales across all dispensaries in Ontario hit about $100 million”; operator estimate “More than 90 percent of customers come from Idaho”. Also notes Ontario is “by far the highest per-capita sales amount of any county in Oregon” on a county basis, with only Multnomah and Washington Counties moving more overall. [secondary — Verify the 2023 $100m against OLCC county data; do not present as OLCC-published]. Source: Portland Monthly. Older secondary anchor: Idaho News 6, 23 December 2020 (via Wikipedia border-towns summary): Ontario sales topped $100m in under two years after dispensaries opened in 2019, “fueled in part by Idaho customers”. Ontario repealed its dispensary ban in November 2018; shops opened 2019. Huntington, OR was the earlier Idaho-shopper town until Ontario opened.

Oregon statewide context (secondary summary of OLCC data, Harris Sliwoski “State of the State 2025”): Oregon total retail sales January–November 2025 $848m vs $881m same period 2024; median usable-marijuana price $3.33/g in April/November 2025. Statewide only — not an Idaho-resident figure. Source: Harris Sliwoski, Oregon State of the State 2025.

Bottom line for this dossier: No official count of Idaho residents buying in Ontario, OR exists (no residency field in OLCC sales data found). The defensible statement is: Ontario, OR reports about $100m/year in cannabis sales (city testimony 2022; Portland Monthly for 2023) from a town of about 11,000, with the city and operators estimating about 90% of customers/transactions are Idahoans; the city budgeted $2.5m from its 3% local marijuana tax for FY 2026–27. Mark the 90% as estimate, Verify.

6.5.2 Washington — Spokane / Spokane Valley and Clarkston, WA (NOT Coeur d’Alene / Lewiston, ID)

Spokane, WA vs Coeur d’Alene, ID: separate cities about 30 miles apart on I-90, across the WA/ID line. Spokane Valley / Liberty Lake / Otis Orchards, WA are the WA-side border communities; Post Falls, Rathdrum and Coeur d’Alene, ID are the ID-side neighbours. No official Washington sales-by-Idaho-resident figure was found. Unavailable / Verify. Washington Liquor and Cannabis Board sales data found in this session is statewide/county only, with no Idaho-resident breakdown. Dispensary marketing (secondary, not sales data): Primo Cannabis, Spokane Valley (21630 E Gilbert Rd, Otis Orchards-East Farms, WA 99027) states it is “less than 10 minutes away from State Line, ID” and lists Post Falls, Rathdrum and Coeur d’Alene, ID among areas served. Source: Primo Cannabis, Spokane Valley. Clarkston, WA vs Lewiston, ID: separate cities across the Snake River. Clarkston, WA dispensaries found via search (marketing listings, not official sales data): Canna4Life, 721 6th St, Clarkston, WA (“across the river from Lewiston, ID”, “Clarkston’s first Cannabis store”); Sativa Sisters, 712 5th St, Clarkston, WA; Greenfield Company, 728 6th St, Clarkston, WA. PART 7: HEMP LICENCES

7.1 Headline: only two current licence types; handler = processor

Idaho has exactly two current hemp licence types: Producer and Handler. There is no separate “Processor” licence — processing is done under a Handler licence. There is no cannabis licence of any kind, no medical/adult-use licence, and no current retail licence.

ISDA hemp hub, opened directly 1 November 2026: “Employees of the ISDA Hemp Program operate under the approval of Idaho's State Hemp Plan with the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA). The Idaho Hemp Program operates under state authority to license and regulate production, processing, transportation, and research of industrial hemp.” Source: ISDA hemp hub. ISDA FAQ, opened directly 1 November 2026: “ISDA is not requiring licenses for retailers. Please refer to section 37-2701 of the Idaho Code for additional information. As a reminder, CBD must have zero percent (0%) THC in Idaho.” Source: ISDA Hemp FAQ. Current IDAPA 02.01.07, opened directly 1 November 2026 (current rule PDF, sections dated 3-15-22): §300.07 “License. A hemp producer or hemp handler license issued by the Department.” No retailer licence appears in the current rule. Source: IDAPA 02.01.07 (current rule PDF).

7.2 State plan vs USDA programme — Idaho runs its OWN USDA-approved state plan

Idaho does NOT use the USDA direct producer-licence programme for Idaho growers. USDA AMS List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans, updated 17 February 2026 (search result, not opened as a full page — Verify on direct open, but consistent with ISDA): “Idaho | ID Approved Plan (pdf) *” with footnote “Approved *: Plan approved under the final rule which took effect March 22, 2021.” Source: USDA AMS state/tribal plan review. ISDA press release, 1 November 2021 (search result): USDA approved the Idaho State Hemp Plan; ISDA would begin licensing producers and handlers under Idaho Code and the 2018 Farm Bill; online applications opened 8 November 2021. Source: ISDA, Approved State Hemp Plan (1 Nov 2021).

Statutory basis, opened directly 1 November 2026: Idaho Code §22-1705(1) and (3) — production, processing, transportation and research are subject to the rules under Chapter 17, the state plan, and the 2018 Farm Bill; once a state plan is accepted by the Secretary of Agriculture, production/processing/transportation/research “will also be subject to the state plan.” Source: §22-1705. Out-of-state recognition (ISDA FAQ, opened directly): an out-of-state operation does NOT need an Idaho licence to sell hemp in Idaho “as long as you are licensed through USDA or a state or tribe program, ISDA will recognize your license.” This does NOT make USDA direct licensing the Idaho programme — it is recognition of a foreign licence for sale only.

7.3 Producer licence

What it allows — IDAPA 02.01.07 §300.06, opened directly:

“A producer license authorizes a person or entity to obtain and possess hemp seed for planting; to cultivate and harvest hemp; to transport their own hemp crop; to dispose of or remediate their own hemp; as well as possess and market plant parts.”

Producer definition, current rule §010.10: a producer includes an owner, operator, landlord or tenant who shares in the risk of producing a crop and is entitled to share in the crop available for marketing, or would have shared had the crop been produced; a producer includes a grower of seed.

ItemProducer — current
Annual application$100
Application late fee (outside window)$250
Annual licence$500
Modification to application information$250 — current figure. The October 2026 proposed rule would reduce this to $100 (PROPOSED only, §7.7 — do not use $100 as current).
Pre-harvest / other inspections$250/lot + $35/hour travel to and from site + actual shipping costs
Background-check / testing costsPaid directly to the provider, not to ISDA (IDAPA §301.02)
First-year ISDA fee floor, 1 lot, in-window$100 + $500 + $250 = $850, plus travel, shipping, background-check and testing costs. All fees non-refundable.

Sources for fees: ISDA Applications page, opened directly (page footer 25 February 2025, text still referring to 2025 — re-confirm for the 2027 cycle): ISDA Applications & Licenses; IDAPA 02.01.07 §301 (Cornell mirror, opened directly): IDAPA 02.01.07.301 (Cornell).

Requirements — current rule §300, opened directly, plus ISDA FAQ opened directly: apply on the form prescribed by the Department; applicant and every key participant must include a fingerprint criminal-history report by Idaho State Police or FBI completed no more than 60 days before application submission. Key participant = person with direct or indirect financial interest, e.g. owner/partner, plus persons at executive levels in a corporate entity; other farm/facility employees need not be listed unless they meet that definition. Application period 1 September–31 December each year for the next calendar year; late fee $250 applies to each late application. Licences valid 1 January to 31 December (late licence valid from date of issue to 31 December); all expire 11:59 p.m. 31 December; not prorated. No cap: ISDA FAQ — “Currently ISDA does not have a cap on how many licenses will be issued.” No minimum/maximum acreage or square footage; no variety limitation, but varieties must be listed on the application. Map: FAQ — ISDA requires a map in the provided example format; a map in a different format “could result in your application being denied” — Verify the exact map specification in the portal before quoting it as a rule. Even a couple of plants / personal use / compost: FAQ — “Yes. Anyone intending to cultivate hemp in the state of Idaho must apply and possess a Producer License.” Tribal land: ISDA Applications page, verbatim — “If your property is within any tribal boundary within the state of Idaho, please do not apply for a hemp license with ISDA. Please contact ISDA with additional information regarding your property.” Ineligibility, current rule §300.08: no licence if (a) hemp licence revoked by Department/USDA/another state/Indian nation/U.S. territory — ineligible for 5 years from revocation; (b) felony relating to a controlled substance under federal or any state law — may not hold a licence, or be a key participant or governing person, before the 10th anniversary of conviction, unless lawfully growing hemp under the 2014 Farm Bill before 20 December 2018 and conviction also before 20 December 2018; (c) material falsification; (d) under 18 at application; (e) three negligent violations in a 5-year period — ineligible to obtain a licence for 5 years from the third violation. Licences are non-transferable — current rule §300.05: “All licenses are non-transferable.” Pre-harvest: producer must submit a sample request at least 35 days before expected harvest; ISDA collects samples of each lot not more than 30 days before harvest; hemp may be harvested only after the official sample is collected; no hemp may leave the producer's control until ISDA notifies the lot complies (full detail in Part 11).

7.4 Handler licence (= processor)

What it allows — IDAPA 02.01.07 §300.07, opened directly:

“A handler license authorizes a person or entity to obtain and possess hemp, including seed, for processing but not intended for the license holder's own cultivation.”

Handler duties, current rule §600 (Cornell mirror, search result — Verify on direct open of that section, but consistent with FAQ): a handler may not acquire/accept hemp except from a person licensed by the Department, USDA, or a state/tribe with a USDA-approved plan; licensed handlers shall not sell, offer or transfer within Idaho any hemp products not in compliance with §37-2701. Handler locations are inspected annually; a sample may be pulled at inspection.

ItemHandler — current
Annual application$100
Application late fee$250
Annual licence$1,000
Modification$500 — current figure. Proposed rule would reduce to $100 (PROPOSED only).
Annual site / other inspections$500/site + $35/hour travel to and from site
First-year ISDA fee floor, 1 site, in-window$100 + $1,000 + $500 = $1,600, plus travel, background-check and testing costs.
Dual Producer + Handler, 1 lot / 1 site$2,350, because the annual application fee is charged only for the first licence type (IDAPA §301.01(a), current rule).

Requirements: same application window (1 September–31 December), validity (1 January–31 December), fingerprint background check ≤60 days, key-participant, ineligibility and non-transferability rules as Producer. ISDA FAQ, opened directly, additional handler points: a manufacturer using a hemp ingredient such as an extract in finished products IS required to obtain a handler licence, even if it is not using hemp plant material and is buying extract from a processor. Handling facilities may obtain hemp from a producer with up to 0.3% THC, but “upon manufacturing, all hemp products must be at 0% THC at the point of distribution. Any manufactured hemp products that cannot reach 0% THC must be destroyed or be subject to law enforcement.” The same 0% rule applies to products manufactured in Idaho for out-of-state distribution: “all manufactured hemp products must be 0% THC in the state of Idaho, including products for out-of state distribution.” Food: other than plant material during production, THC is illegal in Idaho in all manufactured hemp products; the only products allowable for food manufacturing are hulled hemp seed, hemp seed protein powder and hemp seed oil. Hemp derivatives in animal feed are not recognised in Idaho (FDA/AAFCO position followed by ISDA). Lab as handler: FAQ — “If the lab is located in the state of Idaho, a lab must possess a handlers license from ISDA.” Labs testing hemp for THC must also be DEA-registered and ISO 17025-accredited. This is NOT a separate “lab licence” — it is a Handler licence plus federal accreditation.

Proposed handler definition (PROPOSED only, §7.7): the October 2026 proposed text would expand “handler” to include labs, processing/manufacturing facilities, seed facilities, mobile conditioners or mobile processing/manufacturing facilities; a licensed handler may accept hemp from a licensed (state, Tribe, USDA, or country of origin) producer/handler at ≤0.3% THC, may act as a broker, and may sell to licensed producers/handlers; all manufactured or end products must contain no detectable THC.

7.5 Transport — CONFIRMED: NO separate transport permit or fee

There is no transport permit, transport application, or transport fee in the ISDA fee table (IDAPA §301) or on the ISDA Applications page. Do not list one. Transport is an authorised activity, not a licence type: §22-1705(1), opened directly — production, processing, transportation, and research are subject to Chapter 17 rules, the state plan, and the 2018 Farm Bill; §22-1705(7)(b), opened directly — “The transportation of industrial hemp in interstate commerce may continue subject to the provisions of section 67-2921, Idaho Code.” Producer licence itself authorises the producer “to transport their own hemp crop” (IDAPA §300.06).

Idaho Code §67-2921, opened directly in full 1 November 2026 — history: added 2021, ch. 242, sec. 4; am. 2025, ch. 211, sec. 34. Source: §67-2921. This direct opening narrows Run 1's Verify flag on transport: the current text is now confirmed, and only the question whether any EO 2019-13 requirement survives remains Verify.

  • §67-2921(1)(c) hemp definition: Cannabis sativa L. and any part, derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, salts of isomers, with delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight, as defined in the 2018 Farm Bill.
  • §67-2921(1)(e) “Transporter” = any person/entity engaged in hauling, transporting, delivering, or otherwise moving hemp in interstate or intrastate commerce.
  • §67-2921(3): when a transporter/vehicle hauling hemp under a 2014 Farm Bill / 2018 Farm Bill / 7 CFR 990.1 et seq. licence is lawfully detained, the transporter must consent to inspection; a peace officer may randomly select reasonably sized samples not to exceed 20 grams per sampling event for each unique lot, package, or identified quantity and retain them for off-site testing, with no compensation for these de minimis samples; detention only as long as reasonably necessary for inspection, sampling and weighing.
  • §67-2921(4): samples analysed consistent with the 2018 Farm Bill and 7 CFR 990.1 et seq. to determine total delta-9 THC, including all THCA; non-compliant samples may subject the transporter to criminal penalties for marijuana under Title 37, Chapter 27.
  • §67-2921(5): it is unlawful to knowingly possess industrial hemp without a licence or in violation of the 2014/2018 Farm Bills or 7 CFR 990.1 et seq., except when lawfully transporting industrial hemp on behalf of and at the direction of the licensee.
  • §67-2921(6) penalties: 1st violation — misdemeanor, fine ≤$150; 2nd within 5 years of 1st — misdemeanor, fine ≤$300; 3rd or subsequent within 5 years of 1st — misdemeanor, fine ≤$1,000, or county jail ≤6 months, or both; hemp transported/possessed in violation is deemed contraband subject to seizure and destruction.
  • §67-2921(8): the section must not be construed to apply to material/product derived from industrial hemp that contains no quantity of delta-9 THC and is not derived from the prohibited parts of the marijuana plant under §37-2701(u). §67-2921(9): the section must not be interpreted to apply to hemp transported in/through Idaho prior to enactment of the section.
  • No port-of-entry stop, declaration form, or manifest requirement appears in the current §67-2921 text opened. The 2019 Executive Order 2019-13 stopgap (port-of-entry stop, documentation, consent to inspection/sampling, interstate-highway routing) predates HB 126 and the state plan. Do NOT present any EO 2019-13 requirement as current without a current ISDA/ISP source — Verify.

7.6 Research and “other” — NO separate research licence type

No distinct research licence, research permit, or research fee exists in the current fee table or licence list. Research is an activity covered by the Producer/Handler framework, not a third licence type. Current rule scope (§001, opened directly): “These rules govern the licensing, production, handling, and research of hemp.” ISDA FAQ, opened directly: “I am a member of research and extension, am I required to have a license? Yes. Research and extension are required to possess a valid license. Please contact ISDA for additional information.” Registry evidence (opened directly, Run 1 and re-opened 1 November 2026): University of Idaho entries appear on the ISDA licensee list as Producer or Handler, not as a separate research class — e.g. 2023: University of Idaho, Aberdeen R&E Center (Producer, Aberdeen/Bingham); University of Idaho, Boise (Producer, Boise/Ada); University of Idaho, Twin Falls (Handler, Twin Falls/Twin Falls). 2022: University of Idaho, Aberdeen R&E Center (Producer); University of Idaho, Boise (Handler/Producer). Related, non-hemp licences that are sometimes confused with hemp types — NOT hemp licence types, do not list them as such: seed dealer licence (the proposed producer definition notes a producer selling seed “may require a seed dealers license from the Department” — PROPOSED text, Verify in current rule/seed law before stating as current); nursery / Plants Division licence (FAQ — wholesale nurseries selling plants must possess both Producer and Handler licences, undergo regulatory sampling/inspections before distribution, and “are also required to be licensed with the ISDA Plants Division”); tobacco/e-device permit (DHW, Title 39 Chapter 57) — a retail permit for tobacco/vape sales, NOT a hemp licence (Run 1 Part 3).

7.7 HB 772 and HB 879 — what they actually changed (current law, opened directly)

HB 772 (2026), ch. 293, effective 1 July 2026 (Run 1 status, confirmed by §22-1705 history line: “am. 2026, ch. 292, sec. 1, p. 1213; am. 2026, ch. 293, sec. 1, p. 1214”). Idaho Code §22-1705, subsection shown as [(8)](7), verbatim, opened directly:

“Hemp producers do not commit a negligent violation if: (a) The hemp is produced for the sole purpose of producing grain or fiber; (b) The hemp producer has made reasonable efforts to grow hemp within the acceptable hemp THC level, as defined by rule, and in accordance with Idaho statutes, rules, and the state hemp plan; and (c) The hemp does not have a total THC, as defined by rule, concentration of more than one percent (1%) on a dry weight basis.”

Critical limit — HB 772 does NOT raise the acceptable THC level to 1% for movement or commerce. It only shields negligent-violation status when all three conditions are met. Senate Agricultural Affairs Committee minutes, 24 March 2026 (search result): Chairman Nichols confirmed the bill “did not change circumstances under which hemp could leave the field.” HempToday, 26 June / 13 July 2026 (secondary, opened in Run 1): crops testing 0.3–1.0% “may still be subject to destruction or other state requirements.” The October 2026 proposed rule restates this as §900 negligent acts: growing >0.3% is negligent excluding grain/fibre grown to the federal 1% limit where the producer made reasonable effort, and hemp grown with THC in excess of 1% is itself listed as a negligent act — all PROPOSED text, but consistent with the statute.

HB 879 (2026), ch. 292, effective 1 July 2026 (same history line). Idaho Code §22-1705(4), verbatim, opened directly:

“Establishments offering hemp products intended for human ingestion or inhalation, excluding nonviable hulled hemp seed, hemp seed protein powder, or hemp seed oil, shall be subject to the provisions of this chapter. Such hemp products shall meet the criteria provided by sections 37-2701(u) and 37-2705(d)(29)(i), Idaho Code.”

Other §22-1705 provisions opened directly, relevant to licensing: (5) “The department is authorized to promulgate rules establishing fees and penalties for violations associated with the provisions of this chapter.” — this is the rulemaking authority for Docket 02-0107-2601; it does NOT itself create a retail licence or fee. (6) No penalty unless notice and opportunity for hearing under the Idaho APA (Title 67, Chapter 52); appeal to district court of the county of alleged violation within 30 days of final agency action. (7)(a) Rules under this chapter must be adopted through the negotiated rulemaking process. (7)(b) Interstate hemp transport may continue subject to §67-2921 (see §7.5). HB 879 creates no retail licence, no retail fee, and no $600 fee. The $600 concept is HB 771 (2026, NOT law) and HB 478 (2025, DEAD) — see §7.8. Do not attribute the $600 figure to HB 879 in any table.

7.8 PROPOSED ONLY — Retail licence, Docket 02-0107-2601; HB 771 / HB 478

Status as of 1 November 2026: PROPOSED ONLY. No final adoption, no pending-rule publication, and no effective date was found. Do not present any retail fee or retail licence requirement as current law.

Chronology, all opened directly except where noted: Negotiated rulemaking — Notice of Intent published in Idaho Administrative Bulletin Vol. 26-7, 1 July 2026, pp. 37–38 (stated in the October proposal). Proposed (Fee) Rulemaking: Idaho Administrative Bulletin Vol. 26-10, 7 October 2026, pp. 13–25, Docket No. 02-0107-2601, dated 27 August 2026, opened directly 1 November 2026: Administrative Bulletin Vol. 26-10 (PDF). Descriptive summary (p. 13): scope is “to make the rule consistent with changes approved by the Idaho Legislature in H.772 and H.879 in the 2026 Session,” including H.879 retail sale of hemp-derived products for human ingestion/inhalation and H.772 non-negligent violations; “The expansion of regulatory authority into the retail space will necessitate fees for retailers of hemp derived products.” Written comments due 28 October 2026; public-hearing request deadline 21 October 2026 (public notice, search result — Verify hearing date from the Bulletin notice itself before quoting a hearing date; the comment deadline is in the Bulletin text itself). ISDA Current Year's Rulemaking page, opened directly 1 November 2026, still describes 02.01.07 only as: “The scope of the rulemaking will be to make the rule consistent with changes approved by the Idaho Legislature in H.772 and H.879 in the 2026 Session.” No final rule, pending rule, or effective date is stated there. Source: ISDA Rulemaking. No November 2026 Bulletin final rule for 02-0107-2601 was found in searches on 1 November 2026. The November Bulletin would in any event not yet have been published on 1 November. Final adoption / effective date: Verify — re-check the November/December 2026 Bulletin and the Legislature's 2026 interim administrative-rules index. Whether a fee rule additionally requires legislative review/approval before effectiveness is also Verify — do not assert it either way.

Proposed retail licence — proposed text only (Bulletin Vol. 26-10, pp. 14–25): Proposed §010 definitions: “Hemp Products. All inhalable and/or ingestible hemp products intended for human ingestion or inhalation, excluding nonviable hulled hemp seed, hemp protein powder, or hemp seed oil.” “Retailer. A retailer includes an owner, operator, landlord, or tenant who shares in the risk of selling or distributing hemp products… A retailer or distributor may also include, but is not limited to: physical locations, booths, farmers markets, wholesale, online, digital, or mobile facilities.” “In-State Retailer” = physical, mobile or online establishment within Idaho; “Out-of-State Retailer” = the same outside Idaho. “Prohibited Cannabis Products. Any product containing THC, with the exception of biomass pursuant to a producer license as defined by the 2018 farm bill.” Proposed §300.08 “Retail License. A retail license authorizes a person or entity to obtain and possess (not manufacture) hemp products for the purpose of selling or distributing and not intended for the license holder's own consumption or use. All physical, online, digital, or distribution entities must possess an individual license for each location or site.” Proposed fees, §301 and Fee Summary (p. 13): Retailer annual application $100; Retailer annual licence $1,000/site; Retailer modification $100; Retailer annual site inspection and other inspections included in licence cost. Fiscal note: agency estimates 100 entities will apply as licensed hemp retailers, generating about $110,000, with about $95,000 programme expenses (net about $15,000). These are PROPOSED fees, not current fees, and must not appear in a current-fees table. Proposed application: handler and retailer application period 1 September–31 December for the next calendar year, late fee $250 — same window as current handler, but the proposal would split the producer window (see below). Proposed §300.02(a): “Excluding retail applications, [an] applicant and any key participants must include… a fingerprint background check…” — i.e. the proposal would EXEMPT retail applications from the fingerprint background-check requirement. That exemption is PROPOSED only. Proposed retailer duties, §600: Department will inspect all licensed retailers annually, plus random inspections during reasonable business hours; may collect samples; law enforcement may be notified of prohibited cannabis products; “License retailers shall not sell, offer, trade, distribute, or transfer within Idaho any prohibited cannabis products”; records of all sales, acquisitions and transfers, plus disposal/return of prohibited products, retained 3 years. Proposed testing, §700.07: a handler/retail test showing total THC above a non-detectable THC level is conclusive evidence the product lot/batch is a prohibited cannabis product. This is the proposed-rule restatement of the zero-THC retail rule — consistent with, but not a replacement for, the current ODP/FAQ zero-THC position. Proposed civil penalty, §900.08(a) (new text): up to $10,000 for each offense plus reasonable attorney's fees. PROPOSED only — do not cite as a current penalty. (The $10,000 figure in some secondary summaries of HB 771 is a different, dead-bill figure — do not conflate the two.) Other proposed changes that must NOT be mistaken for current law: (i) Producer application period would change to 1 November–31 March for the next/current calendar year (proposed §300.03(b)), vs current 1 September–31 December for all types; (ii) Producer/Handler modification fees would fall to $100 each (vs current $250/$500); (iii) Rule scope would add “and the retailing of hemp based products”; (iv) Licence definition would add “or hemp retailer license.” All PROPOSED.

HB 771 (2026) and HB 478 (2025) — separate from Docket 02-0107-2601, and NOT law: HB 771, introduced 26 February 2026, referred to House Agricultural Affairs, no LAW marker on the official 2026 index (Run 1): would have added §22-1708 — no cannabinoids except naturally derived cannabidiol in consumable/personal-use products; every retail store selling hemp products to hold a 1-year ISDA licence at $600/year (+ $25 per additional store under one licence in secondary summaries); civil penalties up to $10,000 per violation in secondary summaries. Text: HB 771 text (PDF) — PROPOSED ONLY, NOT LAW. HB 478 (2025), Intro Sine Die: same $600 concept, effective 1 January 2027 in its introduced text. DEAD, NOT LAW. Text: HB 478 text (PDF). Do not merge these: the Docket retail fee is $100 + $1,000/site (proposed rule under HB 879 authority); the HB 771/HB 478 retail fee is $600/year (dead/proposed bills). They are different instruments, different amounts, and neither is current law.

7.9 Registries — locate, format, download status

Primary registry — ISDA Current & Past Idaho Hemp Licensees, opened directly (crawled 1 November 2026, 16:55 UTC; re-opened in Run 2): URL: ISDA Current & Past Licensees. Format: HTML table on a web page. No CSV, XLSX or PDF download link shown, no export/download button identified. Exact download link / file name: NONE EXISTS on the evidence of the opened page. Update frequency as stated: not stated on the page. Organised by licence year with “Approximate Acreage Approved to Date” for the current year. Latest data date on the page: licence year 1 January 2026–31 December 2026, Approximate Acreage Approved to Date for 2026 Production: 233. Fields: Business Name | License Type | City | County. No licence number, address, per-licensee acreage, lot list, contact or expiry date. One 2026 row withheld: “Withheld at Requested Licensee” (Handler, Twin Falls/Twin Falls). Counts counted from the opened table (not ISDA-stated totals): 2026: 11 rows — 7 Producer-only, 3 Handler-only, 1 Producer/Handler (Burtenshaw Ag Inc, Producer, Idaho Falls/Bonneville; Double Springs Ranch LLC, Producer, May/Custer; Essential Oil Research Farm LLC, Producer/Handler, Naples/Boundary; Garden City Greens LLC, Producer, Garden City/Ada; George Miller, Producer, May/Lemhi; Hempitecture INC, Handler, Jerome/Jerome; Withheld at Requested Licensee, Handler, Twin Falls/Twin Falls; Idaho Hemp Processing LLC, Handler, Roberts/Jefferson; Idaho Premium Hay Farms & IDA Best Hay, Producer, Roberts/Jefferson; Krown Farms, Producer, Aberdeen/Bingham; Seamons Farms LLC, Producer, Jerome/Jerome). 2025: 16 rows / 1,135 acres; 2024: 23 rows / 1,860 acres; 2023: 23 rows / 1,248 acres; 2022: 23 rows / 458 acres. Do not add years together — repeat licensees are renewals. Limitation: ISDA FAQ, opened directly — “Under Idaho law, [ISDA] cannot release mailing or telephone lists” (Idaho Code §74-120); information not on the website requires a public records request. Per-licensee acreage is therefore not published on the registry.

Other registry-adjacent sources (no separate hemp registry): ISDA Applications & Licenses / portal: ISDA Applications — web portal (login required to apply/manage/pay); no public download, no public search of applicants; footer 25 February 2025, text still referring to 2025 — re-confirm for 2027 cycle. ISDA Hemp FAQ: ISDA FAQ — guidance only, not a registry. USDA AMS plan list: USDA AMS — plan-approval status only, no Idaho licensee list under a state plan. No retail registry exists, because no retail licence exists. If Docket 02-0107-2601 is adopted, a retail registry would be a future, separate item — Verify.

PART 8: CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.1 Headline result — STRICT VERIFIED COUNT IN THE SEARCHES TRIED: 0 — no publicly listed Idaho property meeting the strict standard was found as of 1 November 2026. This is not a certified statement that no such property exists

Under the strict verified standard used in prior dossiers — a property counts ONLY if the listing itself explicitly zones it for, or markets it for, hemp cultivation, hemp processing, or hemp-product retail — the verified count for Idaho is ZERO.

No property meeting that standard was found in any search below. This is a finding of “none found on verified evidence,” not a claim that no Idaho farmland could ever be used for hemp. In particular: ordinary agricultural zoning (e.g. Ag, Ag-40) does NOT count. Idaho has no hemp-specific zoning category located in Run 1, and hemp cultivation/processing is regulated at state level by ISDA licence (plot/lot registration), not by a local “hemp zone.” A farm listed only as agricultural, irrigated, or “suitable for” general agriculture is therefore excluded, however suitable it might be in practice. A listing that merely mentions “hemp” among a long list of possible crops/uses, without an Idaho address, is excluded (see the BusinessesForSale near-miss below). Operating hemp facilities that are NOT listed for sale or lease are excluded (Hempitecture, Idaho Hemp Processing). Out-of-state properties are excluded even when the search engine returned them for an “Idaho hemp” query (see §8.5 traps). Listing status for the table below: every entry is Verify / NOT counted, because no entry meets the strict standard. No property is presented as active and recent on verified evidence.

8.2 Searches tried (all 1 November 2026)

  1. LoopNet — “Idaho hemp farm for sale” and “LoopNet Idaho hemp farm”: returned generic Idaho property records (farm ground, farm addresses, tax-record pages) with no hemp marketing, plus out-of-state hemp farms. No Idaho hemp-marketed listing was returned. LoopNet's own hemp-farm category page returned for Nevada (“There is 1 Hemp Farm For Sale in Nevada — Highway 160 & Ohio Road, The Ohio Ranch, Pahrump, NV 89060, $995,000, 254.90 AC”) — excluded: Nevada, not Idaho. Source: LoopNet hemp farms, NV.
  2. LoopNet site-restricted “Idaho hemp for sale”: single returned listing was 47315 Elk Chattaroy, Elk, WA 99009 — Washington I-502 cultivation/processor, $899,000, 16 acres — excluded: Washington, not Idaho; also a marijuana licence type that does not exist in Idaho. This is the classic Spokane/Coeur d'Alene border trap in reverse: Elk, WA is not an Idaho property. Source: LoopNet, Elk, WA.
  3. 420Property — Idaho index and city pages (cannabis/hemp specialist): Idaho index page states Idaho “remains one of the few states with no medical or adult-use cannabis program” and then: “We couldn't find a listing for your search criteria.” City pages repeat this: Lewiston, ID — “We couldn't find a listing for your search criteria”; Salmon, ID — same; St. Maries, ID — same; Spirit Lake, ID — descriptive text about “hemp-suited processing space” but no actual Idaho listing shown (the only concrete listing on that page is I-502 in Elk, WA). Result: zero Idaho listings on 420Property. Sources: 420Property Idaho; 420Property Lewiston, ID; 420Property Salmon, ID; 420Property Spirit Lake, ID; 420Property St. Maries, ID.
  4. LandWatch — site:landwatch.com Idaho hemp: returned generic Idaho land (Emmett, Mountain Home, Orofino, Parma, Sagle, Athol, Fremont County, Rigby, Preston) with no hemp marketing, plus Sacramento County, California industrial-hemp zoning ordinance (PDF) and Hemp, GA (town name, not a crop) — both excluded: wrong state / wrong meaning of “Hemp.”
  5. Crexi — Idaho agricultural / industrial land for sale: search snippets returned general Idaho farm/industrial listings; snippet counts were not verified as listing counts and are not published here as inventory figures (e.g. River Run Ranch, Bliss, ID — certified organic farm/ranch, 530 acres, Snake River; Gilbert Grade, Orofino, ID — 379± acres dry-land farm; Nampa commercial development land, 15.65 AC, C2 zoning, $2,726,856). None of the snippets reviewed mentions hemp. Crexi has no hemp filter in the results returned. General farm/industrial listings are not counted under the strict standard. Sources: Crexi agricultural, ID; Crexi industrial, ID.
  6. Land & Farm — Idaho farms for sale: 456 current listings in one snippet (2,595 in another, by different filter) — irrigated farms, ranches, feedlots, dryland grain farms. No hemp-marketed Idaho listing was surfaced in the snippets reviewed. General farm listings are not counted.
  7. Targeted web searches: “‘hemp farm’ Idaho ‘for sale’ acres price”; “‘hemp processing’ Idaho facility for sale OR lease property”; “‘CBD’ store for sale Idaho Boise business” — no Idaho hemp-zoned/marketed property for sale was returned. The CBD-store search returned only (a) a historic Welcomed Science CBD opening story (Garden City/Boise, pre-2021, not a sale listing), (b) Happy Life CBD, Boise (see §8.3), and (c) historic Boise Towne Square CBD-store proposals — none is a property for sale.

8.5 Same-name / border traps avoided in Part 8

Moscow, ID ≠ Moscow, Russia; Lewiston, ID ≠ Lewiston, ME (420Property Lewiston, ID page returned zero listings — do not substitute Lewiston, ME listings); Coeur d'Alene, ID ≠ Spokane, WA; Fruitland/Payette, ID ≠ Ontario, OR; Ontario, OR ≠ Ontario, Canada; Elk, WA (I-502 listing) ≠ Elk City, ID (timber/recreational land, no hemp marketing); Pahrump, NV ≠ any Idaho town; Arvada, CO (former hemp farm, LoopNet) ≠ any Idaho town; Immokalee, FL (hemp-licence farm) ≠ Idaho; Hemp, GA is a town name, not a hemp property; Sacramento County, CA hemp ordinance ≠ Idaho zoning. The BusinessesForSale facility (§8.3) is the one candidate that could not be placed at all — it is excluded rather than assigned to Idaho on the basis of I-80/airport clues. If the parent wishes to pursue it, the follow-up is to contact the broker for a verified address/state before any inclusion.

Part 8 follow-ups: re-run LoopNet/Crexi/Land & Farm with a live-browser / logged-in search if required — the text-search route used here cannot see paywalled or login-gated LoopNet/CoStar detail, and LoopNet property-record pages are not the same as active listings (mark any live-browser result as a separate verification step); check Idaho MLS / Intermountain MLS for “hemp” keyword listings — not accessible via the public searches used here; a broker/MLS search is the remaining route to a non-zero result, if one exists; check county assessor / zoning verification for any candidate farm before counting it — agricultural zoning alone will not meet the strict standard; the listing must itself say hemp; broker follow-up for the BusinessesForSale $1,150,000 facility — obtain verified state/county/address before any inclusion; until then it stays excluded; re-check 420Property Idaho pages after 1 November 2026 — they returned zero listings on 1 November, but a new listing could appear at any time; listing status for any future entry must still be marked Verify unless the listing itself shows it active and recent.

Office phone (208) 854-3040; mailing address PO Box 83720, Boise, ID 83720-0198. A separate ODP grant address, 304 N 8th St, Ste #455, Boise, ID 83702, appears in grant documents — do not conflate it with the main mailing address without checking. CBD page opened in Run 1, updated Feb 2025: ODP CBD page — zero-THC rule, Epidiolex prescription-only, SB 1146a veto history, 2015 Expanded Access Program history.
Idaho Department of Health and Welfare (DHW)Tobacco/electronic smoking device permits for vape shops; 2015 Expanded Access Program authorisation route.Tobacco Permits & Electronic Smoking Device Information, found by search: DHW tobacco permits. From that page: retailers including vape shops selling nicotine, tobacco, or electronic smoking device products must obtain a permit; Prevention of Minors' Access to Tobacco Products and Electronic Smoking Devices Act requirements took effect 1 Jul 2020; permits issued free of charge and must be secured before beginning sale; permits expire 31 Dec 2025 in the secondary summary and must be renewed annually — Verify current expiry wording on the DHW page itself. A DHW tobacco/e-device permit is not a hemp/CBD licence and does not authorise sale of any THC product. DHW has no medical cannabis programme to administer. No DHW cannabis-specific contact page was found — Verify if one is needed.

Other secondary roles (note, do not overstate): Idaho Legislature / Legislative Services Office (bill pages, HJR 4 text, proposed-amendment page proposed amendment); Idaho Secretary of State / VoteIdaho (ballot measures, initiatives, results — no official HJR 4 result exists as of 1 November 2026); Idaho Transportation Department / ports of entry (historical EO 2019-13 regime only; current survival under §67-2921 is Verify); USDA Agricultural Marketing Service (federal hemp-plan approval: USDA AMS plan review — list updated 17 February 2026 per search result).

It did not decriminalise CBD generally; it created an affirmative defence. Do not describe it as legalisation. Veto-message fragments corroborated across multiple secondary sources, including Spokesman-Review reporting 16 Apr 2015: “It ignores ongoing scientific testing on alternative treatments … It asks us to trust but not to verify. It asks us to legalize the limited use of cannabidiol oil, contrary to federal law. And it asks us to look past the potential for misuse and abuse with criminal intent.” Also: “Of course I sympathize with the heartbreaking dilemma facing some families trying to cope with the debilitating impacts of disease.” Full veto text was not opened from the Governor's archive in Run 1 or Run 2 — Verify verbatim before quoting the full message.

Contemporaneous reporting: up to 25 children. ODP page opened in Run 1 confirms the EAP route. Idaho did not thereby legalise hemp production; Idaho law still treated any THC-containing Cannabis as marijuana until HB 126.

Amended in the Senate; died in the House. … The Senate rewrote the entire bill, adding heavy law enforcement controls that gutted the intent of the original House bill. The House did not take up the Senate's amended version.” Accurate short form: “passed the House, passed the Senate as amended, then died in the House on concurrence.” Do not state simply “died in committee” as the full history. 2021 (Senate only)Constitutional amendment attempt that did NOT reach the ballot — clarification, not a passed measureBallotpedia HJR 4 page, found by search: Ballotpedia HJR 4 — Background: “In 2022, the Idaho legislature attempted to place a constitutional amendment on the ballot that proposed banning the use, manufacture, and possession of psychoactive drugs in the state of Idaho unless regulated by law and the federal Food and Drug Administration. The state Senate approved the bill, but the state House never vot[ed].” Marijuana Moment, found by search: Marijuana Moment — Senate approved the resolution 24–11 (committee 6–2 along party lines, sponsor Sen. C. Scott Grow); if it had also passed the House by two-thirds it would have gone to the 2022 ballot. Proposed new §30 language: “the production, manufacture, transportation, sale, delivery, dispensing, distribution, possession, or use of a psychoactive drug shall not be permitted in the state of Idaho,” except substances (a) approved by the FDA and permitted by the state, (b) lawfully prescribed, and (c) lawfully dispensed. Rep. Outcome on current evidence: Senate passed, House did not pass, never reached the ballot, never became law. Do not conflate it with 2025 HJR 4, and do not conflate SJR 102 (2021, legislature calling itself into special session — passed both houses, approved by voters 2022, 287,194 to 267,623) with the drug amendment. The gaanjaheal claim that “in 2021, the Republican-dominated Idaho legislature passed a proposed constitutional amendment to ban any future marijuana legalization laws” is therefore incorrect as a passed measure on this evidence; what happened in 2021 was a Senate-only passage, not a passed referral.
