It must not be conflated with the federal 0.4 mg cap effective after 11 Dec 2026.

9.2 Big Sky Scientific — 2019 hemp truck seizure — outcome (full detail)

Seizure: 24 January 2019, East Boise port of entry / weigh station, Interstate 84, Ada County. Idaho State Police stopped a semi carrying hemp purchased by Big Sky Scientific LLC from Boones Ferry Berry Farms, an Oregon-licensed hemp grower, en route to Colorado for processing into CBD isolate. Cargo: 6,701 pounds of hemp plant material (precise figure, multiple reports) vs “about 7,000 lb” / “about 13,000 lb load” in other summaries — use 6,701 lb as the precise figure, with “about 7,000 lb” noted as a rounded secondary figure. Value reported about $1.3 million. Truck and cargo seized; driver arrested. Driver: Denis Palamarchuk, Portland, Oregon, working for IYI Trucking (not Big Sky, per later reporting). Initially charged with felony marijuana trafficking; at that quantity framing, a mandatory minimum of 5 years was reported as the exposure. Corporate domicile conflict: sources describe Big Sky as Colorado-based, Montana-based, and Boise-based in different reports. Verify domicile; it is not material to the legal outcome and should not be stated as a single confirmed fact.

Federal civil case — Big Sky Scientific LLC v. Idaho State Police / Bennetts, D. Idaho No. 1:19-cv-00040-REB — emergency stage, not a final merits decision. District court denied emergency TRO and, on 19 February 2019, denied preliminary injunction. Reasoning reported consistently: cargo grown before any USDA/state plan existed could not have been “produced in accordance with” subtitle G of the 2018 Farm Bill, so §10114(b) interstate-commerce protection did not apply. An OFW Law summary also cites an Order dated 2 February 2019 denying the motion — Verify whether 2 February 2019 was the TRO order and 19 February 2019 the preliminary-injunction order, before assigning both dates in the final file. That was an emergency-stage ruling, expressly not a final merits determination.

Driver plea, September 2019, and Ninth Circuit abstention. Driver plea, September 2019: Palamarchuk pleaded guilty to a misdemeanor described as “hauling an improperly permitted load including faulty bill of lading” / “hauling an improperly permitted load.” Felony trafficking charges dropped. Per Transport Topics, 24 September 2019: he was required to serve 5 days already served of an earlier judgment calling for 180 days in Ada County Jail, had his earlier fine cut in half to $500, and was required to pay $1,860 in restitution. AP report, 11 September 2019, confirms the plea-deal structure (misdemeanor, felony dropped, most jail time to be suspended). Exact plea-charge wording differs slightly across AP summaries — the Transport Topics phrasing above is the most precise found; Verify from the Ada County record before quoting the charge title as a court caption. Two other drivers, Andrew D'Addario and Erich Eisenhart, arrested in April 2018 for transporting more than 900 hemp plants through Idaho, also pleaded guilty to misdemeanors in September 2019 per Transport Topics: each pleaded guilty to misdemeanor marijuana possession, served 2 days of 180 originally sentenced, and each paid $4,138.79 restitution. Secondary only — Verify before using, and do not conflate their charges with Palamarchuk's. Ninth Circuit, unpublished decision, Big Sky Scientific v. Bennetts, 776 Fed. Appx.: date conflict — Vanderbilt summary and Lexology/Mondaq summaries give 4 September 2019; OFW Law summary gives a memorandum decision issued 9 September 2019. Use 4 September 2019 as the primary date, flag the 9 September 2019 variant in research notes, and Verify from the slip opinion. Holding, consistently reported: the Ninth Circuit did NOT decide the merits. It held the district court should have abstained under Younger because the Idaho state in rem forfeiture action was pending, reversing the district court's refusal to abstain, on the state's representation it would lift the stay and the assumption the state court would proceed expeditiously. Federal litigation effectively ended there.

Executive Order 2019-13, 19 November 2019, Governor Brad Little, interstate hemp transport. Governor's press release, found by search (gov.idaho.gov press-release title in Run 1): “issued an executive order today to resolve conflicts between state and federal law related to the interstate transportation of hemp across Idaho … serves as a stopgap measure until the Idaho Legislature enacts a more permanent solution … does not authorize or legalize the production of hemp, its byproducts, oils, or any other derivative prohibited by Idaho law.” EO text, found by search as a PDF copy on a local-news mirror (not a direct gov.idaho.gov open in this pass — Verify against the Governor's site before quoting as a primary source): EO 2019-13 (mirror PDF). Operative provisions from that text and contemporaneous summaries: Permit, on and after 31 October 2019, interstate transportation of hemp produced in accordance with the 2014 Farm Bill or the 2018 Farm Bill and rules thereunder. Transporter must stop at the first port of entry encountered in Idaho to declare hemp; present documentation including producer licence verification, a DEA-registered laboratory report confirming Farm Bill compliance for each lot, a bill of lading with contents, origination including lot number, destination, weight, and vehicle type; affirm the vehicle contains no illicit drugs or variations of hemp not authorised by the 2014/2018 Farm Bills; label each container with producer name/address, quantity, and lot number; consent to inspection and ISP sampling for off-site testing; receive an inspection report. Transport only on interstate highways or in the immediate vicinity of an interstate highway, save for an Idaho Transportation Department-authorised detour; avoid unnecessary delay. “Nothing within this Executive Order … shall authorize or be interpreted to legalize hemp, its byproducts, oils, or any other derivative prohibited by Idaho law. This Executive Order only permits the interstate transportation of hemp consistent with the 2018 Farm Bill and implementing regulations, this Executive Order, and the State of Idaho rules regarding the interstate transportation of hemp.” “Nothing … shall authorize or be interpreted to apply to hemp transported in the State of Idaho prior to October 31, 2019.” The EO therefore did not save the January 2019 Big Sky shipment, which predated 31 October 2019. Whether any port-of-entry requirement survives under §67-2921 / current ISDA rules after HB 126 is Verify — see Part 10.

State forfeiture judgment, 21 January 2020. Idaho State Police v. One White 2013 Freightliner Commercial Vehicle et al., 4th District, Ada County, Judge Jonathan Medema. Summary judgment for Idaho State Police; Big Sky's motion for summary judgment denied; hemp forfeited to ISP and not returned to Big Sky, notwithstanding that ISP implicitly conceded the material was low-THC Cannabis sativa and that it later tested at or below the federal THC limit. Reasoning as summarised in Vanderbilt (February 2020), Lexology, Mondaq (4 February 2020), and OFW Law summaries: Oregon's 2014 pilot programme was non-compliant and the material was cultivated before the 2018 Farm Bill, so it was not shown to be within the federal hemp definition / produced in accordance with subtitle G; and there was then no roadside means to distinguish hemp from marijuana. Under Idaho's Uniform Controlled Substances Act at that time, all plants within the Cannabis genus were controlled substances other than mature stalks, and possession of THC-containing plant material was presumptively contraband subject to forfeiture. Date conflict: most summaries give 21 January 2020; one Run 1 secondary report dated the judgment 24 January 2020. Use 21 January 2020, flag the 24 January 2020 variant in research notes, Verify from the judgment. Appeal status: NOT FOUND. A Ganjapreneur report published 30 January 2020 states Big Sky's attorney was evaluating the decision before deciding whether to file an appeal or seek Idaho Supreme Court review. No appellate outcome was found in Run 1 or Run 2. Do not state the forfeiture is “final and unappealed” without that check. Policy consequence, correctly stated: the seizure + EO 2019-13 + HB 126 sequence is the direct line to Idaho's current transport regime (§67-2921). Do not present Big Sky as authority that current licensed hemp transport through Idaho is illegal — the 2019 facts predate HB 126, the USDA-approved state plan, and §67-2921.

9.3 Ballot history — qualified vs failed, with official results or signature status

No Idaho medical cannabis measure has been voted on and passed or failed at the ballot in the sources found; all citizen attempts below failed to qualify, except HJR 4, which is a legislative referral, not a citizen initiative, and has not yet been voted on as of 1 November 2026. Keep “failed to qualify” distinct from “voted down.”

YearMeasureSponsorOutcome
2012, 2016, 2018Medical attempts—Fell short. Secondary LocalYocal Idaho report, April 2026, citing KTVB reporting October 2025, states the 2026 effort was the seventh attempt since 2012 and that past initiatives in 2012, 2016, 2018, and 2022 all fell short. No signature counts for 2012, 2016, or 2018 were located — Verify against Secretary of State initiative lists; do not invent counts. A separate secondary source also mentions failed attempts in 2012 and 2014 before a 2016 effort — Verify the 2014 attempt against the Secretary of State list before adding it to the table.
2020Medical Marijuana InitiativeIdaho Cannabis Coalition / Legalize IdahoDid not qualify — signature drive cancelled due to COVID-19; attempts to file for deadline extensions and requests to permit electronic signatures were unsuccessful; did not qualify for the 2020 ballot. Wording identical to the 2022 initiative, per Ballotpedia HJR 4 page found by search. No official signature total was located — Verify if a total is needed; do not use a campaign estimate as an official result.
2022Medical Marijuana InitiativeKind IdahoDid not qualify — sponsor announced before the deadline that it had failed to achieve sufficient signatures. Same wording as 2020, per Ballotpedia. Threshold cited for 2022: 64,945 valid signatures, per MjLink (2022) and Star News Network (23 Jan 2022). No official final valid-signature count was located — Verify.
2024Idaho Medical Marijuana ActKind IdahoDid not qualify. Kind Idaho began collecting signatures for the 2024 ballot; MjLink (2022) states it must collect at least 74,000 signatures by April 2024. That figure differs from the generic “roughly 70,000” in later Marijuana Moment reporting — flag the threshold difference; the operative threshold is set by registered-voter counts at the time, Verify on the Secretary of State page. Proposed terms reported secondarily (Cannabis Business Times via MjLink): patients could possess up to 4 ounces; 4% excise tax on gross receipts for dispensary products; dispensaries 1,000 feet from private or public schools. All PROPOSED, all Verify against the filed text; none is law. Marijuana Moment, 2024, quoting Kind Idaho treasurer Joe Evans: Kind Idaho collected about 20,000 signatures; roughly 70,000 validated signatures were needed; the campaign cited resource limitations and community response, and pivoted to a slimmed-down noncommercial / decriminalisation proposal for 2026. A separate 2021 proposal, reported in the same Marijuana Moment article, would have allowed adults to possess up to 3 ounces on private property, prohibited home cultivation, and contemplated consumers buying in neighbouring states and bringing product home for private consumption. It did not make the ballot. Do not conflate it with the 2024 medical initiative.
2026Idaho Medical Cannabis ActNatural Medicine Alliance of Idaho (NMAI)Did NOT qualify — official determination, Secretary of State announcement 14 Jul 2026, via Ballotpedia and Run 1: 58,024 valid signatures, 12,701 short of 70,725, and the 6% in 18 of 35 legislative districts requirement also not met. Campaign stated 17 Jul 2026 it would not pursue a legal challenge. Campaign claims at different stages, all secondary and superseded by the official determination: over 45,000 signatures (Marijuana Moment, early 2026); over 73,000 total, exceeding the 70,725 statewide requirement on a raw-count basis (Marijuana Moment, 2026); over 100,000 raw signatures (LocalYocal, April 2026); over 150,000 raw signatures submitted (Run 1 summary, from Secretary of State announcement context). Raw counts are not valid counts. Filed August 2025; AG Certificate of Review 29 Sep 2025 (see §9.1). Proposed terms, from Run 1, all PROPOSED ONLY: single vertically integrated “Medical Cannabis Production License”; initially 3 licences statewide, expandable to 6 (+1 per 650,000 population increase, per sponsor — Verify against filed text); each licensee up to 2 cultivation/production facilities and up to 6 retail locations; online ordering for pickup and delivery proposed; proposed prerequisites included an Idaho hemp licence in good standing, an operating plan, background checks, and a pharmacist engaged for oversight; proposed patient instrument: Medical Cannabis Card for a “substantial health condition”; proposed purchase limits reported as 113 g smokeable cannabis or 20 g THC extract for vaping per month, edibles 10 mg THC per serving — all Verify against filed text; proposal would have reclassified cannabis to Schedule II under state law — PROPOSED, distinct from the federal Schedule III baseline; do not merge them. Marijuana Moment reporting on the campaign's fiscal analysis (Younger Associates, using Utah's programme as a model) claimed over $100 million in annual medical sales and up to $28 million in new yearly state revenue. That is a campaign-commissioned projection, not an official revenue figure — label it as such if used, and do not use it as a market total. Secondary analysis in Marijuana Moment (2026) also states the proposal had no equity-centred reforms and no home-grow option. Verify against filed text before stating definitively.
2026Decriminalisation initiativeKind IdahoDid not qualify per Ballotpedia and IdahoVoters (records as of 3 Oct 2026) listing the Idaho Medical Cannabis Act and the Decriminalize Cannabis Now initiative as “Did Not Qualify” on the Secretary of State's Initiatives and Amendments page. Names in sources vary: Personal Adult Marijuana Decriminalization Act (PAMDA), “Decriminalize Cannabis Now” initiative, and a slimmed-down noncommercial proposal. Detail and signature figures for the decriminalisation initiative were not opened in Run 1 or Run 2 — Verify; do not conflate its figures with the medical initiative's 58,024 / 70,725. Kind Idaho's 2024 pivot statement, per Marijuana Moment 2024, was to a noncommercial proposal after the medical effort failed; whether that pivot produced the specific 2026 decriminalisation filing, and its exact title, is Verify on the Secretary of State filing list.
2026HJR 4, legislatively referred constitutional amendmentLegislature (2025)ON BALLOT 3 Nov 2026 — NOT YET VOTED ON as of 1 Nov 2026. Ballot question, from the Legislature's proposed-amendment page and the 2026 Voter Pamphlet p. 4 as quoted in secondary sources: “Shall Section 26, Article III of the Constitution of the State of Idaho be amended to provide that only the Idaho Legislature shall have power and authority to legalize the growing, producing, manufacturing, transporting, selling, delivering, dispensing, administering, prescribing, distributing, possessing, or using of marijuana, narcotics, or other psychoactive substances?” Proposed new §26(2), effective immediately upon adoption by voters, in the same sources: “only the legislature of the state of Idaho shall have power and authority to legalize the growing, producing, manufacturing, transporting, selling, delivering, dispensing, administering, prescribing, distributing, possessing, or using of marijuana, narcotics, or other psychoactive substances.” Meaning statement, per §67-453 and the Voter Pamphlet: a YES vote would give the Legislature exclusive authority to legalise marijuana, narcotics, or other psychoactive substances; a NO vote would make no change to the Constitution, which currently allows changes to controlled-substances laws using the initiative process. Simple majority approves, per Idaho Capital Sun reporting cited in secondary sources — Verify the majority rule on the Secretary of State page if stated as law. Fiscal note, per Rep. Bruce Skaug as quoted on Ballotpedia: no changes to existing statutes, hence no fiscal impact except the cost of placing the measure on the ballot; Secretary of State approximate cost of any ballot measure not more than $200,000; biennial appropriation $300,000 historically to cover ballot measures. Secondary quotation — Verify against the HJR 4 Statement of Purpose before quoting as official. If approved, HJR 4 would not affect any initiatives on the ballot in the 2026 election itself, because its text is effective immediately upon adoption and no marijuana initiative is on the 2026 ballot in any event, per Ballotpedia. Election result: NONE as of 1 Nov 2026. The election date is 3 Nov 2026, in the future relative to all research in this file. Ballotpedia's results table showing “Yes 0.00% / 0” and “No 0.00% / 0” is a placeholder before voting, not a result. ACLU of Idaho (2026 ballot explainer, found by search) confirms three 2026 ballot measures: citizen-led Proposition 1, HJR 4, and HJR 6, plus a non-binding advisory State Gun Question. The Arbiter (Boise State student paper, 30 Oct 2026) confirms in-person voting 8 a.m.–8 p.m. on Tuesday 3 Nov 2026 and that neither the medical nor a separate decriminalisation initiative qualified. Required follow-up: after 3 Nov 2026, check the official Secretary of State results (VoteIdaho / Secretary of State elections results) for HJR 4 and report the result with source and date. Until then, mark the result Verify / not yet available, do NOT invent a result.

9.4 Public opinion polling — dated secondary only, do not invent

No official polling was found. No poll was located on an Idaho government site. No polling on HJR 4 itself, on adult-use legalisation as a standalone question beyond the 2022 SurveyUSA recreational item below, or on hemp was located — Unavailable for HJR 4, not zero support.

Boise State University Public Policy Survey — reported February 2011 (older, usable with caveats). Source, found by search: Toke of the Town, Feb 2011 — reporting Betsy Z. Russell, Spokesman-Review. Corroborated: Jack Herer, Nov 2011 — “According to a 2011 Boise State University Public Policy Survey, 74 percent of Idahoans support the purchase of medicinal marijuana for terminally and seriously ill patients.” Field: November 18 to January 8 (the survey's 20th iteration; the prior survey was in 2007, budget cuts having nixed the intervening years). Sample: 525 randomly selected Idaho households, cell phone and landline respondents. Margin of error: ±5 percentage points. Results: 74% support allowing “terminally and seriously ill patients to use and purchase marijuana for medical purposes”; 23% said no; 3% didn't know. On sale/manufacture: “should allow the sale and manufacture of marijuana for medical purposes” — 46% agreed, 46% disagreed (remainder not stated in the snippet — Verify full crosstabs before publishing a complete breakdown). Note the question wording is narrower than general medical legalisation (terminally/seriously ill patients only); do not present 74% as support for a general medical programme without that qualifier.

SurveyUSA for the Idaho Statesman — 2022 (dated, usable). Poll page opened directly 1 November 2026: SurveyUSA, Idaho 2022 — headline: “2 of 3 in Idaho say Legalize Medical Marijuana; 48% Want Recreational Use Made Legal; Just 1 in 10 Say Jail Time Right for Marijuana Possession.” Field/sample, from secondary reporting of the same poll: conducted October 17–20, 2022, online and by phone, 550 adults; released November 5, 2022. Sources: Ganjapreneur, Nov 2022; Kind Idaho, 20 Nov 2022; MPP polling summary: MPP polling summary (PDF). Results, from the opened SurveyUSA page: medical use should be made legal 68%; should remain against the law 18%; not sure 14%. Recreational legal 48%; should remain against the law 41%; not sure 10% (48+41+10 = 99, rounding). Possession of small amounts: jail time 11%; fine 43%; no penalty at all 42% (11+43+42 = 96; remainder presumably unsure — Verify full crosstabs before publishing a complete breakdown). Medical legalisation supported by significant majorities of every demographic subgroup except those identifying as very conservative, who still support by a 13-point margin, 47% to 34%. A Kind Idaho editorial (20 November 2022) cites a similar 2019 survey finding 72% in favour of medical marijuana — Verify that 2019 poll's pollster, dates, and sample before using it as a separate data point; do not merge it with the 2022 SurveyUSA figures.

GS Strategy Group for Natural Medicine Alliance of Idaho — October 2025 (the only usable recent dated poll; campaign-commissioned, not independent). Pollster now identified from the deck itself (resolves the earlier “pollster not identified” gap): GS Strategy Group, presented by Robert Jones. Deck footer in the search-returned deck: “505 W. BANNOCK STREET | BOISE, ID 83702 | 208.342.1545 | www.gsstrategygroup.com”. Deck: “Idaho Medical Marijuana Research – October 2025,” via NMAI Medical Cannabis Survey (PDF, search-returned) — the deck itself was found by search, not opened as a full PDF in this pass; figures below are corroborated by Marijuana Moment (February 2026) and Marijuana Herald (19 December 2025) reporting of the same poll, and by the deck text returned in search. Field dates: 23–26 October 2025. Sample: N = 400 likely voters in Idaho. Margin of error: ±4.9 percentage points. Sponsor/commissioner: Natural Medicine Alliance of Idaho, the 2026 medical initiative sponsor — label the poll as campaign-commissioned, not independent. Results: Medical use overall: 83% support allowing marijuana for medical use (45% medical use only + 38% medical and recreational use); 15% say it should not be legal at all. Note: 45 + 38 + 15 = 98, not 100; the deck notes some numbers may be off ±1% due to rounding, and 2% is unaccounted for in the snippet — Verify the full deck for “don't know / refused” before publishing a complete breakdown. Ballot question (“Next November, there could be a ballot in Idaho that would legalize the use of cannabis for medical purposes. If that election were today, would you vote yes or no?”): Yes 76% (Definitely 50%), No 21%, Undecided 4%. Note: 76 + 21 + 4 = 101, again a rounding artefact in the deck — Verify before publishing. By party, from the deck table returned in search: medical support = Dem 95% (29 medical-only + 66 medical-and-recreational), Indy 92% (49 + 43), GOP 74% (48 + 26); not legal = Dem 3%, Indy 4%, GOP 23%. Ballot Yes = Dem 97%, Indy 88%, GOP 63%; No = Dem 1%, Indy 11%, GOP 32%; Undecided = Dem 1%, Indy 1%, GOP 5%. Other item reported by Marijuana Herald (19 December 2025): only 13% of respondents said people suffering from serious conditions like cancer or PTSD have all the pain treatment options they need. Reporting dates: Marijuana Moment signature/poll article (early 2026, reporting the October 2025 poll); Marijuana Herald article 19 December 2025, “Poll: Over 80% of Idaho Voters Support Legalizing Medical Marijuana.”

Older, imprecise poll reference — do not use as a precise figure. Marijuana Moment, 2024, quoting Kind Idaho treasurer Joe Evans: “nearly 2 in 3 Idahoans in support of medical legalization,” citing polling data from about two years earlier, and “about 8 in 10 voters in favor” of decriminalisation in the same two-year-old poll. No pollster, field dates, sample size, or margin of error were given in that article — Verify; do not convert “nearly 2 in 3” or “about 8 in 10” into percentages with a pollster attribution. (The “about two years earlier” poll may be the 2022 SurveyUSA poll above, but that linkage was not established in the article — do not assume it.)

PART 10: TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10.1 Legal framework with the state — general principles

Federal hemp framework: 2018 Farm Bill, Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018, Subtitle G, “Hemp Production,” added to the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (7 U.S.C. §1621 et seq.); USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program, 7 CFR Part 990. A state, tribe, or U.S. territory may submit a hemp production plan to USDA for approval; the plan must contain at minimum the practices and procedures in 7 CFR 990.3(a). Only a governing body, such as a State Department of Agriculture or Tribal Government, may submit a plan. Plans may be updated at any time; major operational changes require a revised plan submission. Source: USDA AMS, “Information for States and Tribes with USDA-approved Hemp Plans,” found by search: USDA AMS, Information for States and Tribes — note the URL as returned contains “fors-tates”; copy it verbatim from the search result and Verify the canonical AMS URL before publishing. Approved-plan list, found by search, updated 17 February 2026: USDA AMS plan review — lists Idaho as “ID Approved Plan” and, for tribes, Nez Perce Tribe as “Approved Plan (pdf)*” and Shoshone-Bannock as “Approved Plan (pdf)*,” where “*” means approved under the final rule which took effect 22 March 2021.

Tribal sovereignty / jurisdiction principle in the two approved Idaho tribal codes: each code retains tribal rulemaking, regulatory, and (for Shoshone-Bannock, expressly) taxing authority over hemp commerce within its territorial boundaries, preserves sovereign immunity, exempts compliant licensed activity from Tribal Court prosecution, and states that nothing in the code authorises violation of federal law. Neither code creates a state licence, and neither code is an Idaho state statute.

No Idaho–tribal cannabis compact or intergovernmental agreement (IGA) on marijuana was found in this pass. Contrast: Nevada law (June 2017) allows Nevada tribes to negotiate medical marijuana compacts with the state, and Nevada's governor signed compacts with the Ely Shoshone Tribe and Yerington Paiute Tribe — those are Nevada tribes and Nevada law, not Idaho, and must not be attributed to Idaho tribes.

ISDA vs tribal jurisdiction: ISDA's Applications page directs applicants on tribal land not to apply to ISDA (see §9.1). That is an ISDA administrative direction, not a tribal ordinance and not a state–tribal agreement. Whether ISDA has a separate memorandum or agreement with any tribe on hemp was not found — Verify with ISDA and with each tribe before stating that none exists as an exhaustive negative.

State law off-reservation: Idaho's marijuana prohibition, possession/trafficking penalties, and zero-THC retail rule apply in Idaho outside tribal jurisdiction as described in Run 1. A tribal hemp licence does not, on the evidence found, authorise sale of a THC-containing product in the Idaho marketplace off-reservation, and a tribal hemp product that contains any THC would be treated as marijuana under Idaho law off-reservation unless it falls within a licensed-hemp or FDA-drug exception. That last sentence is a legal inference from Run 1 statutes, not a quoted tribal or state provision — label it as such if used, and Verify with the relevant tribal code's transport provisions and with ISDA before stating it as a settled cross-jurisdiction rule.

Official tribal website directory (for verification, not as evidence of a programme): Idaho Supreme Court tribal links page, found by search: ISC tribal links — lists Coeur d'Alene Tribe (now cdatribe-nsn.gov), Kootenai Tribe (www.kootenai.org), Nez Perce Tribe (nezperce.org/government/lawandjustice/), Shoshone-Bannock Tribe (www.sbtribes.com), Shoshone-Paiute Tribe (shopaitribes.org/spcourt/). Some of these URLs may have migrated — Verify current canonical URLs before publishing. Northwest Portland Area Indian Health Board member list, updated 22 September 2026, corroborates Coeur d'Alene www.cdatribe-nsn.gov, Kootenai www.kootenai.org, Shoshone-Bannock www.sbtribes.com: NPAIHB member tribes.

10.2 Tribe-by-tribe — official tribal/government sources only for legal points

Nez Perce Tribe — USDA-approved hemp plan; NO marijuana programme found

Status: Federally recognised tribe, headquarters Lapwai, Idaho. Nez Perce Reservation.

Hemp ordinance / code, official tribal source: Comment period notice, official tribal site, found by search: Nez Perce hemp ordinance comment period (10 Sep 2020) — notice of a proposed amendment to the Nez Perce Tribal Code regarding regulation of cultivation, processing, and distribution of industrial hemp on the Nez Perce Reservation; comment deadline 23 October 2020. The notice states approval must be completed in coordination with USDA, which must approve plans submitted by states and Indian tribes, and that the Tribe will regulate for “Acceptable Hemp THC Level” as prescribed by federal law. USDA approval, official tribal announcement found by search: Nez Perce USDA approval announcement — “On February 12, 2021, the U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA) approved the Nez Perce Tribe's Hemp Regulatory Code (Code). The Code was submitted to the USDA on December 16, 2020 for review pursuant to the U.S. Domestic Hemp Production Program.” With approval, the Tribal Executive Committee would proceed with final adoption and implementation. Date conflict: a secondary summary (500nations) headlines “USDA Approves Nez Perce Tribe Hemp Plan February 20, 2021” and states the Tribe was the 41st tribe to receive approval. Use the Tribe's own 12 February 2021 approval date as primary; flag the 20 February 2021 reporting date as the date of the news report, not necessarily the approval date, Verify from USDA. Code as adopted: Nez Perce Tribal Code, Title 6, Chapter 13, Hemp Regulatory Code, as adopted through Resolution NP 21-186 (9 March 2021). The version opened directly in this pass is a March 2022 public-comment version with track changes to reflect the USDA Final Rule, not a clean final codified version: Nez Perce Hemp Code, March 2022 public-comment version (PDF) — Verify the current clean codified version with the Tribe before quoting any section as the current law. Operative text below is from that opened version, and must be labelled accordingly.

Verbatim operative text — Nez Perce Hemp Regulatory Code, opened version:

§6.13.05 Jurisdiction, A: “Territories of the Nez Perce Tribe. For purposes of the Hemp Regulatory Code, the Territories of the Nez Perce Tribe includes all lands within the exterior boundaries of the Nez Perce Reservation; all Nez Perce Tribe allotments, located both on and off the Nez Perce Reservation that still possess an Indian title; and, any lands, both on and off the Nez Perce Reservation, title to which is either held in trust by the United States for the benefit of the Nez Perce Tribe, or its members, which remain subject to restriction against alienation and over which the Nez Perce Tribe exercises jurisdiction. The Tribe shall also have jurisdiction over E-commerce transactions emanating from or to the jurisdiction of the Nez Perce Tribe.” §6.13.05 Jurisdiction, B: any person who uses land anywhere within the exterior boundaries of the Tribe, and any person who enters into agreements or understandings with the Tribe or its members and residents by commercial dealings, contracts, leases, licenses, permits, intergovernmental agreements, or other arrangements, commercial or otherwise, shall be deemed to have entered into a consensual relationship with the Nez Perce Tribe or its members subject to the regulatory and adjudicatory jurisdiction of the Nez Perce Tribe. §6.13.06 Sovereign Immunity: “Nothing in this Code shall be construed to waive, alter, or otherwise diminish the Nez Perce Tribe's Sovereign Immunity, whether expressed or implied, by virtue of this Code for any and all administrative or legal action which may arise directly or indirectly from the same; nor does the Nez Perce Tribe waive, alter, or otherwise diminish its rights, privileges, remedies, or services guaranteed by the Treaty of 1855.” §6.13.07 Exemption from Prosecution for Certain Acts: “No employee or Key Participant of a Licensed Hemp Business or Licensed Producer or Processor shall be subject to prosecution or civil penalty in the Tribal Court for the cultivation, production, or distribution of Hemp when acting in accordance with the requirements of this Code and applicable Tribal and federal law.” §6.13.08 Compliance with Federal Law: “Nothing in this Code authorizes any Person to violate any Federal law or regulation.”
§6.13.11 Definitions, A, “Acceptable Hemp THC Level”: when a laboratory tests a sample, it must report the Delta-9 Tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) content concentration level on a dry weight basis and the measurement of uncertainty. The acceptable level is when application of the measurement of uncertainty to the reported delta-9 content on a dry weight basis produces a distribution or range that includes 0.3% or less. Example given in the code: reported 0.35% with measurement of uncertainty ±0.06% gives a range 0.29%–0.41%; because 0.3% is within the distribution or range, the sample is within the acceptable hemp THC level. “This definition of 'Acceptable Hemp THC Level' affects neither the federal statutory definition of hemp, 7 U.S.C. §1639o(1), in the 2018 Farm Bill nor the definition of 'marihuana,' 21 U.S.C. §802(16), in the Controlled Substances Act.” §6.13.11 Definitions, S, “Hemp”: “the plant Cannabis sativa L., and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not with a THC concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent (0.3%) on a dry weight basis, or as otherwise defined in Federal law.” §6.13.11 Definitions, J, “Decarboxylated”: the completion of the chemical reaction that converts THC-acid into Delta-9 Tetrahydrocannabinol; the decarboxylated value is also calculated using a conversion formula that sums Delta-9 Tetrahydrocannabinol and eighty-seven and seven-tenths (87.7) percent of THC-acid. §6.13.11 Definitions, X, “Hemp Product”: a finished product with an Acceptable Hemp THC Level, derived from or made by processing a Hemp Crop, prepared in a form available for commercial sale; includes, but is not limited to, cosmetics, personal care products, Consumable Products, cloth, cordage, fiber, fuel, paint, paper, particleboard, plastics, and any product containing one or more Hemp Ingredients such as cannabidiol.

Licensing structure in the code's table of contents: Part III Hemp Licensing — annual hemp licence required (§6.13.15), licence applications (§6.13.16), grow-site registration (§6.13.17), terms and conditions acknowledgment (§6.13.18), licence term (§6.13.19), annual renewal (§6.13.20), ineligibility (§6.13.21), revocation (§6.13.23), appeal of denial (§6.13.24), fees (§6.13.25), compliance and enforcement (§6.13.26); Part V Transportation (§6.13.35); Part VII Inspections, Sampling, and Testing (§§6.13.50–6.13.57), including lab accreditation, pre-harvest compliance sampling, and remediation/disposal of non-compliant plants and products. Fee amounts and licence-term lengths were not extracted from the opened version in this pass — Verify from the code text before publishing any Nez Perce fee or term. Operations / plans: Regulation under the code is described in the Tribe's USDA-approval announcement as extensive, including licensing requirements for growers, inspection and sampling, pre- and post-harvest testing, regulations on use of pesticides, and use of only certified testing labs. The Tribe's 2021 announcement, as summarised secondarily, stated: “Although we still have a lot of work to do before we license the first crops, we are excited for what the future holds in this area and to bring back an element of our culture that's been missing for some time.” Current licensed production volume, current licensee list, and current retail operations on the Nez Perce Reservation were not found in this pass — Unavailable / Verify. Do not claim a current crop, acreage, or dispensary on the basis of the 2021 plan approval. No Nez Perce marijuana ordinance, medical cannabis programme, or dispensary was found in official tribal/government sources in this pass. A Honeysuckle Magazine feature on a Nez Perce elder and historical marijuana cultivation on allotments, including 1980s enforcement, is not a tribal ordinance or current programme and must not be cited as one. The same feature states the Nez Perce people organised to legalise cannabis cultivation on the reservation in 2017 but the Tribal Executive Committee had yet to revise the tribal law and order code — secondary, undated in the snippet, Verify before using as history.

Shoshone-Bannock Tribes (Fort Hall Reservation) — USDA-approved hemp plan; hemp operations reported; NO marijuana programme found

Status: Federally recognised tribes, Fort Hall Reservation, southeastern Idaho. Governing body: Fort Hall Business Council (FHBC).

Hemp ordinance / code, official government source: USDA AMS plan PDF, opened directly in this pass: Shoshone-Bannock Hemp Plan (PDF) — Title 26, Agriculture, Livestock and Animals, Chapter 4, Hemp Regulatory Code, 23 pages in the PDF as crawled. USDA approved-plan list, found by search, updated 17 February 2026: USDA AMS plan review — Shoshone-Bannock listed as “Approved Plan (pdf)*.” The exact USDA approval date for the Shoshone-Bannock plan was not stated in the opened PDF's first pages or in the list snippet — Verify the approval date from USDA before publishing it; do not borrow the Nez Perce 12 February 2021 date. The “*” notation means approved under the final rule which took effect 22 March 2021, so the approval post-dates that rule, but that is not an approval date.

Verbatim operative text — Shoshone-Bannock Hemp Regulatory Code, opened PDF:

§26-4-01 Short Title: “This document may be cited as the 'Hemp Regulatory Code.'” §26-4-02 Findings and Purpose, A: “Article VI, Section 1, Subsection f of the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes Constitution authorizes the Fort Hall Business Council (FHBC), as the governing body of the Tribes, to engage in business activities which promote the economic well-being and advancement of the Tribes and its members.” §26-4-02, B: “Industrial Hemp is a valuable agricultural crop and commodity with significant value for Tribal economic development. Hemp is a strain of Cannabis that is grown as an agricultural product throughout North America. Hemp is utilized for a variety of products and functions including fiber, oil, and biomass based products. Hemp production has been evaluated as an important material to maintain the traditional practices of the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes.” §26-4-02, C–E: cultivation of hemp was a staple of American agriculture until it was outlawed through state law, the Marijuana Tax Act in the 1930's, and the Controlled Substances Act of 1970, 21 U.S.C. §801 et seq., because hemp contains naturally occurring compounds found in marijuana crops; prior to the 2018 Farm Bill, the CSA classified hemp as a Schedule I drug and prohibited possession or use except in federally approved research; the 2018 Farm Bill amended the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (7 U.S.C. §1621 et seq.) by adding Subtitle G, “Hemp Production,” which now allows the Tribes' controlled cultivation of hemp in accordance with that Act and a Tribal Hemp Plan approved by the Secretary of Agriculture.
§26-4-03 Scope and Authority, A: “The Shoshone-Bannock Tribes will retain full rulemaking, regulatory and taxing authority over the commerce of, including but not limited to: the growth, cultivation, processing, marketing, production, and sale, of hemp and hemp products within its territorial boundaries by any persons.” §26-4-03, C: “The regulations and penalties imposed by this Code extend to any person within the Tribe's Jurisdiction, whether Licensed or not.” §26-4-04 Industrial Hemp Authorized as an Agriculture Crop: “Hemp will be considered an authorized agricultural crop on the Fort Hall Reservation if it contains no more than 0.3 percent total delta-9-tetrahydrocannibinol (THC). The Tribes hereby authorizes the possession, cultivation, transportation, production and use of Hemp products within the Fort Hall Reservation, when those activities are licensed by the Tribes and conducted in full compliance with the requirements of this Code and applicable law.” Note the PDF spelling “tetrahydrocannibinol” — quote it as printed or note the spelling; do not silently correct it in a verbatim block without a sic note. §26-4-05 Jurisdiction, A: “For purposes of the Hemp Regulatory Code, the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes includes all lands within the exterior boundaries of the Fort Hall Reservation, and Indian country as defined in 18 U.S.C. §1151 (a), (b), & (c). The Tribes shall also have jurisdiction over Ecommerce transactions emanating from or to the jurisdiction of the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes.” §26-4-05, B: consensual relations — any person who uses land anywhere within the exterior boundaries of the Tribes, and any person who enters into agreements or understandings with the Tribes or its members and residents by commercial dealings, contracts, leases, licenses, permits, intergovernmental agreements, or other arrangements, commercial or otherwise, shall be deemed to have entered into a consensual relationship with the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes or its members subject to the jurisdiction of the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes. §26-4-06 Sovereign Immunity: “Nothing in this Code shall be construed to limit the jurisdiction of the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes, the Tribal Court, or the Tribal Police, and nothing herein shall limit or constitute a waiver of the sovereign immunity of the Tribes or its officers, instrumentalities, employees, elected officials, and agents, or authorize any form a prospective waiver of such sovereign immunity.” §26-4-07 Exemption from Prosecution for Certain Acts: “No employee or Key Participant of a Licensed Hemp Business or Licensed Producer or Processor shall be subject to prosecution or civil penalty in the Tribal Court for the cultivation, production, or distribution of Hemp when acting in accordance with the requirements of this Code and applicable Tribal and federal law.” §26-4-08 Compliance With Federal Law: “Nothing in this Code authorizes any Person to violate any Federal law or regulation. Any subsequent changes to this Ordinance shall require submission and approval to the USDA to review for compliance.”

§26-4-11 Definitions, A, “Acceptable Hemp THC Level,” as opened (first lines): when a laboratory tests a sample, it must report the delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol content concentration level on a dry weight basis and the measurement of uncertainty. The acceptable level is when application of the measurement of uncertainty to the reported total delta-9 content produces a range that includes 0.3% — the full definition was truncated in the opened crawl at line 273; Verify the complete definition, including the worked example if any, before quoting it in full. Code structure: Part III Licensing (§§26-4-20 annual licence requirements, 26-4-22 ineligibility, 26-4-23 institution of higher education / research institution requirements, 26-4-24 revocation, 26-4-25 appeal, 26-4-26 fees, 26-4-27 compliance and enforcement); Part IV Registered Land Area Controls (§§26-4-30, 26-4-31); Part V Transportation (§26-4-35); Part VI Records and Reports (§§26-4-40–26-4-44); Part VII Inspections, Sampling, and Testing (§§26-4-50–26-4-57, including disposal of non-compliant plants and hemp products); Part VIII Violations (§§26-4-60 negligent violations, 26-4-61 corrective action plan, 26-4-62 repeat negligent violations, 26-4-63 other violations). Fee amounts, licence terms, and negligent-violation thresholds were not extracted in this pass — Verify from the code text. Repeat negligent violations, §26-4-62, A, from the search-returned text: “A Hemp Producer that negligently violates this Hemp Regulatory Code three (3) times in a five (5)-year period shall be ineligible to produce Hemp for a period of five (5) years beginning on the date of the third violation.” Immediate revocation trigger in the search snippet for the code: “The License of a Hemp Producer shall be immediately revoked in the event that a Licensee (Hemp Producer): A. Pleads guilty to, or is convicted of, any felony related to a controlled substance” and “Is found to be growing Cannabis exceeding the Acceptable Hemp THC Level with a …” — snippet truncated; Verify the full revocation list before quoting.

Operations / plans, tribal news sources (not state law): Shoshone-Bannock Tribal Agri-Business Corporation pilot project, reported in ShoBan News (tribal newspaper site, found by search, date not stated in the snippet — Verify date before publishing): planted 140 acres of industrial hemp in the Michaud Flats area, south end of the Fort Hall Reservation, in a field where wheat was initially planted; seed purchased from Dun Agro for $17,000; hemp about 2 feet tall at reporting, expected 12–16 feet by late-August harvest; Tribal Agri-Business licensed as a grower under Shoshone-Bannock Hemp regulations and USDA; Tribal Enterprises licensed as a seller of hemp products; Fort Hall Business Council initially approved a resolution for a Dun Agro agreement but later rescinded it; Agri-Business was developing a harvest and purchase agreement with Dun Agro, and until approved the hemp could be stored up to 2 years or sold elsewhere; land owned by the Tribes through a tribal lease. First tribal-member-owned hemp selling business, reported in ShoBan News (found by search, date not stated in the snippet — Verify date): Zah Not-Soo Boohee (Good Medicine Plant), owner Patazikivaa Perry, Fort Hall — described as the first tribal member owned hemp selling business on the Fort Hall Reservation, offering CBD oil extracts, lotions, tea bags, and pre-rolled cigarettes. Perry stated he grows all the hemp on his land and makes the CBD products himself, makes a full-spectrum oil including CBDA, CBDC, CCBG, THC, THCA and everything in the plant, and “can't go over 0.3 percent THC because of the Farm Bill Act.” Tribal business licence issued under agriculture/farming for hemp production and hemp product sales on June 8 (year not stated in the snippet — Verify year). Licence stipulation quoted in the article: “All products grown, bought or sold under this license must meet the 'Acceptable Hemp THC Level' as required by LWOR-2022-S2. Further, all hemp products grown, bought, or sold under this license must meet compliance and safety standards as approved by the USDA with testing conducted in accordance with LWOR-2022-S2 and final test results must be certified by the lab that was used, test results must be made available to the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes Tax Department, and testing must be completed prior to products entering the stream of commerce. All conduct of business must be done in accordance with the Tribal Law & Order Code…” Do not present the Perry business or the Agri-Business pilot as current operations without a date check. Both reports are undated in the snippets retrieved; the pilot report's context (first season, wheat field, Dun Agro) suggests an early pilot, not a current production total. The Tribes also have a licence to grow hemp with plans for industrial use, per the Perry article — secondary, Verify. No Shoshone-Bannock marijuana ordinance, medical cannabis programme, or dispensary was found in official tribal/government sources in this pass.
