Tribal “state-licensed” question — remains Verify

Question: If any tribe operates a medical programme, does it count as “state-licensed” for purposes of the federal split-scheduling carve-out (state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026)? Answer on current evidence: Verify, no authority found. No Idaho tribe was found operating a medical cannabis programme in this pass. No Idaho statute, federal regulation, DEA guidance, or court decision stating that a tribal medical programme counts (or does not count) as “state-licensed” was found in Run 1 or in Run 2. A USDA-approved tribal hemp plan is not a state licence and must not be equated with “state-licensed” medical marijuana. Hemp is federally distinct from marijuana; tribal hemp approval does not answer the medical-marijuana question. A tribal medical programme, if one were created under tribal sovereignty, would raise separate questions of tribal jurisdiction, federal Controlled Substances Act status, and any state–tribal compact — none of which can be answered from the sources found. Do not state that a hypothetical tribal programme would or would not qualify. Related framework note, for context only, not as an answer: The 2014 DOJ Cole Memorandum on tribal marijuana policy and subsequent DOJ guidance are frequently cited in secondary tribal-cannabis literature, but no Idaho-specific DOJ policy, compact, or enforcement agreement was located in this pass — Verify before citing any DOJ tribal policy as applicable to Idaho tribes.

10.3 Interstate — hemp shipments, out-of-state buying, enforcement, what can legally be brought into Idaho

What can legally be brought into Idaho — short answer, matching Run 1: No marijuana in any amount or product type may lawfully be brought into Idaho for personal use, medical use, or sale, except FDA finished-dosage products (Epidiolex with a prescription; nabiximols in an FDA-approved drug product) as excepted in §§37-2701(u) and 37-2705(d)(29)(i). Flower, edibles, vapes/cartridges, concentrates, beverages, and oils containing THC are all treated as marijuana under §37-2732(e) (“extract or any preparation … which contains tetrahydrocannabinol”) unless they fall inside the licensed-hemp or FDA-drug exceptions. Licensed hemp may be transported through and within Idaho only as authorised by Title 22, Chapter 17, the USDA-approved state plan (or an approved tribal plan for tribal land), and §67-2921. Zero-THC hemp/CBD products are the only hemp products that may be sold at retail / in the Idaho marketplace, per ODP February 2025. An out-of-state medical card, including an Oregon medical marijuana card, is not recognised in Idaho; no reciprocity provision was found in Title 37, Chapter 27. Possession in Idaho is governed by Idaho law on return, regardless of lawful purchase elsewhere. Importing 1 lb or more, or possessing 25 plants or more, is trafficking under §37-2732B, with mandatory minimums in Run 1. Possession ≤3 oz is a misdemeanor with a $300 minimum fine if 18+ (HB 7); >3 oz to <1 lb is a felony up to 5 years / $10,000.

Hemp shipments through Idaho — current law vs historical stopgap: Current: §22-1705(1) — production, processing, transportation, and research of industrial hemp are subject to the rules under Title 22, Chapter 17, the state plan, the 2018 Farm Bill, and its rules. §67-2921 — transport, total delta-9 including all THCA analysis, knowing possession without a licence unlawful except when lawfully transporting on behalf of and at the direction of a licensee, first violation misdemeanor fine ≤$150, non-compliant hemp deemed contraband subject to seizure and destruction per §67-2921(6)(d). Full §67-2921 text was opened directly in Run 2, Part 7 — see Part 7 for the confirmed text; Run 1's Verify flag on §67-2921 is narrowed accordingly. Historical: EO 2019-13 (see §9.2) was a stopgap for interstate transport on/after 31 October 2019, with first-port-of-entry declaration, DEA-lab reports, bills of lading, container labelling, interstate-highway routing, and ISP sampling. It did not legalise hemp production or retail in Idaho, and it did not apply before 31 October 2019. Whether any EO 2019-13 port-of-entry, documentation, or routing requirement survives as a current requirement after HB 126 and §67-2921 is Verify — do not present EO 2019-13 requirements as current law without that check. Federal interstate-transport protection: 2018 Farm Bill §10114(b) prevents states from prohibiting transportation or shipment of hemp produced in accordance with subtitle G. Big Sky shows that protection did not save a pre-plan, pre-2018-Farm-Bill crop in 2019 Idaho litigation. It does not authorise transport of marijuana, and it does not override Idaho's zero-THC retail rule for products sold in Idaho.

No interstate cannabis agreement found. No Idaho–Oregon, Idaho–Washington, Idaho–Nevada, or Idaho–Montana cannabis compact, memorandum, or mutual-recognition agreement was found in this pass. Mark as Unavailable / Verify if an exhaustive negative is needed; do not state “no agreement exists” as a certified exhaustive finding without a state-agency check. No Idaho law or agreement allows Idaho residents to bring home marijuana bought in a neighbouring legal state, and no neighbouring state's law purports to authorise import into Idaho.

Idaho residents buying in Oregon — Ontario, OR. Ontario, OR is in Malheur County, OR, on the Snake River / I-84, about 50–55 miles west of Boise. Fruitland and Payette, ID are the Idaho-side neighbours. Ontario, OR ≠ Ontario, Canada. Sales: about $100 million per year — City of Ontario testimony to the Oregon Legislature, 2022 (SB 1506 testimony: second-largest retailer behind Portland, 10% of Oregon sales, about $100 million gross, 892,857 transactions per year, nearly 2,500 sales daily, 90% from Idaho citizens, average transaction $112) and Portland Monthly for 2023 (about $100 million, at least a dozen dispensaries, operator estimate more than 90% of customers from Idaho). The 90% figure is a city/operator estimate, not OLCC purchaser-residency data; OLCC does not publish buyer state of residence on the evidence found. Local tax: City of Ontario FY 2026–27 Budget Message (PDF, found by search, not opened directly — Verify by opening Ontario FY 2026–27 Budget Message (PDF)) states the city continues to estimate $2.5 million in revenues from its 3% local marijuana tax in the 2026–27 budget, with 2025–26 revenue expected higher than budgeted. History: Ontario repealed its dispensary ban in November 2018; shops opened 2019; Huntington, OR was the earlier Idaho-shopper town until Ontario opened. Idaho News 6, 23 December 2020, via secondary summary: Ontario sales topped $100 million in under two years, fueled in part by Idaho customers. Enforcement context: older AP-era reporting (about 2012–2013) claimed 40% of pot seizures in Idaho came from Oregon medical marijuana, 53% in the border sector. That figure is dated, secondary, and sector-specific — do not present it as a current statewide figure.

Idaho residents buying in Washington — Spokane / Spokane Valley and Clarkston, WA. Spokane, WA ≠ Coeur d'Alene, ID. Separate cities about 30 miles apart on I-90, across the WA/ID line. Spokane Valley / Liberty Lake / Otis Orchards, WA are WA-side border communities; Post Falls, Rathdrum, and Coeur d'Alene, ID are ID-side neighbours. Clarkston, WA ≠ Lewiston, ID. Separate cities across the Snake River. No official Washington sales-by-Idaho-resident figure was found — Unavailable. Washington Liquor and Cannabis Board data found in Run 1 is statewide/county only, with no Idaho-resident breakdown. Marketing only, secondary: Primo Cannabis, Spokane Valley (21630 E Gilbert Rd, Otis Orchards-East Farms, WA 99027), states it is less than 10 minutes from State Line, ID and lists Post Falls, Rathdrum, and Coeur d'Alene, ID among areas served. Clarkston, WA dispensary listings found by search: Canna4Life, 721 6th St, Clarkston, WA (“across the river from Lewiston, ID”); Sativa Sisters, 712 5th St, Clarkston, WA; Greenfield Company, 728 6th St, Clarkston, WA. No Idaho-customer share or sales figure for Clarkston was found — Unavailable. Washington tax context, secondary 2026: 37% cannabis excise tax plus sales tax; Spokane combined sales tax 9.1% cited in a 2026 cost-comparison article. Tax-rate context only, not a measure of Idaho buying.

Idaho residents buying in Nevada — Jackpot, NV. Jackpot, NV is directly south of Twin Falls, ID on US-93. Jackpot, NV ≠ Twin Falls, ID. No official Nevada sales-by-Idaho-resident figure was found — Unavailable. Marketing only, secondary: Thrive Cannabis Marketplace, Jackpot, NV, markets itself as “the closest legal marijuana dispensary to Idaho,” serving Twin Falls (about 45-minute drive), Pocatello (about 2.5 hours), Idaho Falls (about 3 hours), and Boise (about 4 hours). No sales or Idaho-share figure is given on that page, per Run 1. Do not conflate Jackpot with Las Vegas/Reno statewide Nevada sales.

Idaho residents buying in Montana — West Yellowstone / Gardiner, MT. West Yellowstone, MT is nearest to the eastern Idaho / Island Park / Rexburg corridor. West Yellowstone, MT ≠ Island Park or Rexburg, ID. No official Montana sales-by-Idaho-resident figure was found — Unavailable. Dispensary presence only, secondary listings: Lone Peak Cannabis Company, 4420 Targhee Pass Hwy, West Yellowstone, MT (states anyone 21+ with government photo ID may purchase adult-use cannabis, “Montana residents and out-of-state visitors are welcome”), plus Yellowstoned and Northstar in West Yellowstone, and Gardiner, MT shops (The Smoking Bear, Paradise Organics). No Idaho-specific volume is stated. Montana adult-use is 21+. Idaho residents may lawfully purchase in Montana, Washington, Oregon, or Nevada, but may not lawfully bring marijuana back into Idaho.

Enforcement at the borders — what the evidence actually supports. ISP patrol and interdiction, including I-84 in western Idaho bordering Oregon, and the East Boise port of entry / weigh station in the Big Sky case, are documented. ISP Domestic Highway Enforcement (ISP DHE) is listed as an Oregon-Idaho HIDTA initiative. Oregon-Idaho HIDTA, Idaho State Trend Report 2026 (April 2026, found by search as a PDF: HIDTA Idaho State Trend Report 2026 (PDF) — not opened directly in this pass, Verify before quoting as confirmed): Idaho is the 14th largest landmass state, 83,642 square miles, population just over 2 million per U.S. Census Bureau estimates reported in the document, 2025. About 54% of residents live within HIDTA-designated counties Ada, Bannock, Canyon, and Kootenai. During 2025, HIDTA-designated task forces in Ada, Bannock, Canyon, and Kootenai Counties seized 44.8 kg methamphetamine, 6.9 kg powder fentanyl, 50,738 fentanyl pills, 18.3 kg cocaine, 215.3 kg and 771 dosage units of illicit marijuana and marijuana edibles; conducted 204 drug-related arrests; seized $551,602.96 in cash and assets; seized 92 firearms. “According to task force officers surveyed, most of the marijuana trafficked in the state is imported from the neighboring production states Oregon, California, and Washington.” Fentanyl and methamphetamine remain the principal drug threats by availability and demand, per law-enforcement survey data; illicit marijuana continues to impact communities but is not listed as a principal threat in that summary. Task forces named: DEA Boise Task Force (Ada and Canyon), CCNU in Canyon County, BADGES in Bannock County (DEA Boise, Pocatello Police, ISP, Bannock County Sheriff, Chubbuck Police), NINE in Kootenai County (state/local agencies, Bureau of Indian Affairs, U.S. Customs and Border Protection, and others per the report). Kootenai County Trend Report 2026 (April 2026, found by search: HIDTA Kootenai County Trend Report 2026 (PDF) — not opened directly, Verify): In 2025, task-force officers seized 13.7 kg methamphetamine, 41,538 fentanyl pills, 6.0 kg powder fentanyl, 3.7 kg cocaine, 5.5 kg of raw marijuana and marijuana edibles; NINE conducted 14 drug-related arrests, seized 21 firearms and $189,450.97 in cash and assets; NINE disrupted or dismantled 6 drug trafficking organisations. Washington identified as a local supply area for many HIDTA task forces operating in Kootenai County, from active investigations and survey responses. HIDTA Times, 4th Quarter 2025 (found by search: HIDTA Times 4th Quarter 2025 (PDF) — not opened directly, Verify): Marijuana seizures listed as 168,719.7 kg in 2024 and 55,135.1 kg year-to-date 2025 in the Oregon-Idaho HIDTA activity table. Scope is Oregon-Idaho HIDTA-wide, not Idaho-only — do not present these as Idaho seizure totals without confirming the table's geographic scope. ISP ISAC 2025 brief, cited in Run 1: 25% of IIBRS offences were drug/alcohol offences; marijuana was seized in 58% of drug arrests; 44% physical-arrest rate for marijuana arrests. That is an ISP announcement summary, not a border-specific count. No official border-specific seizure count, border-crossing count, or return-traffic count for 2025–2026 was found that isolates Idaho residents returning from Oregon, Washington, Nevada, or Montana — Unavailable, not zero enforcement.

PART 11: OPERATIONS RULES

Cover for hemp businesses. Where Idaho has no rule, that is stated explicitly. No other state's rule has been imported. Governing rule — IDAPA 02.01.07, §§010, 500, 700, opened directly 1 November 2026: IDAPA 02.01.07 (current rule PDF). Rule dates in the opened text are (3-15-22). Caution: this opened version predates HB 772 (2026) — see the negligent-violation note in §11.2/§11.5. Whether IDAPA has been re-promulgated to reflect HB 772 is Verify.

11.1 Testing: required tests, sampling, failed tests, accredited labs

What test is required: Only a total-THC regulatory test is specified in IDAPA §700. No pesticide, heavy-metal, microbial, solvent or mycotoxin panel is required by §700 as opened — no such required panel found. ISDA FAQ, opened directly, says ISDA does not require soil testing before licensing, and buyers may require soil tests for pesticide residues or heavy metals: ISDA Hemp FAQ. §010(02) “Acceptable Hemp THC Level”: total delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis that, when reported with the measurement of uncertainty, produces a range that includes 0.3% total THC or less, as defined in the 2018 Farm Bill. §010(11) “Total THC”: the sum of THCA and delta-9 THC. §700(02): an accepted laboratory must use appropriate validated methods, evaluate measurement of uncertainty, and test using post-decarboxylation or a similarly reliable method by which the total THC reported accounts for conversion of THCA into THC.

Sampling rules — IDAPA §500, opened directly: Producer must submit a sample request form at least 35 days before the expected harvest date (§500(03)). ISDA conducts inspection and collects samples of each lot not more than 30 days before harvest. Hemp may be harvested only after the official sample is collected. Producer licensee or a key participant must be present at inspection (§500(05)). Harvest no more than 30 days after sample collection; if harvest is delayed beyond 30 days, secondary samples of each lot must be collected and tested, on a further request form, subject to the §301 inspection fee (§500(06)). Harvested lots may not be commingled with other lots or material (§500(07)). No hemp may leave the control of the producer until ISDA notifies the licensee the lot complies (§500(08); §700(04) same rule for entering the stream of commerce, with measurement of uncertainty applied). Transplanting: §501 — transplant information with the application or by lot-change request; a lot may not be divided into more than one transplantation area for on-farm production; selling transplants wholesale or retail requires a producer and handler licence. Handler inspections: §600 — ISDA inspects all handler locations annually, licensee or key participant must be present; random inspections during normal business hours; a sample may be pulled at an inspection.

Lab procedure — IDAPA §700, opened directly: Sampling per ISDA Sampling Protocol; ISDA sends samples to an accepted laboratory selected by the license holder at time of sampling; licensee bears full lab cost (§700(01)). Lab must: maintain chain of custody; retain the sample at least 30 business days from submission; not commingle lots; send official results to ISDA, license holder and USDA no later than the 15th business day from submission; report total delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis with measurement of uncertainty (§700(02)). Failed test / conclusive evidence: §700(03) — a test result showing, with acceptable quality control passing, total THC exceeding the acceptable level is conclusive evidence the lot exceeds the allowed concentration. Lab must promptly notify producer, ISDA and USDA. Retest: §700(05) — licensee may request retest of the original sample within 5 days of receiving first results; retest must be by the same laboratory, on the original sample; retest results are final. No second retest is provided for in §700.

Accredited / DEA-registered labs: §010(01) “Accepted Laboratory”, opened directly: a state department of agriculture laboratory or a laboratory accredited to ISO/IEC 17025:2017 and capable of THC quantitation by GC-MS or HPLC. The Idaho State Police Forensic Laboratory is expressly excluded. After 31 December 2022, accepted labs also must be registered with DEA under the Controlled Substances Act, 21 CFR part 1301.13. ISDA FAQ, opened directly: “Any laboratory testing hemp for THC concentration must be registered with DEA… Additionally, labs are required to maintain an ISO 17025 accreditation. Idaho labs must also have a handlers license issued by ISDA.” ISDA: “does not endorse any labs”; labs on its website list are “accredited labs strictly for producers and handlers reference”, list derived from USDA, labs on it possess DEA and ISO 17025. FAQ also says labs conducting official analysis “will be required to be DEA and ISO 17025 accredited as of December 31st, 2024” — date discrepancy vs IDAPA's 31 December 2022: Verify which date ISDA currently enforces. No definitive named lab list is given here, because the ISDA hemp-resources lab list itself was not opened directly in this run — Verify at ISDA's hemp resources page before naming labs in the dossier. Provider claims found by search only (not ISDA-confirmed, Verify): SC Labs claims an “Idaho State Department of Agriculture Registered Hemp Testing Laboratory” credential — SC Labs accreditations; IndigoBridge Laboratory claims DEA registration, USDA hemp analytical approval and ISO 17025 for cannabinoids — IndigoBridge hemp testing. Neither claim is presented as ISDA endorsement. Do not conflate with the federal baseline: Run 1's federal baseline says USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing is delayed to 31 December 2026. That is a federal enforcement delay; it does not rewrite Idaho's IDAPA §010(01) DEA + ISO 17025 accepted-lab definition as opened. How ISDA applies the federal delay in practice is Verify.

11.2 Licence rules: transfers, ownership changes, renewals, relocation, revocation, background checks, insurance/bonding

All from IDAPA 02.01.07 §§300–301, 900, opened directly (same PDF as above), plus ISDA FAQ opened directly. Fees are Run 1 figures and are repeated only for context: producer $100 application / $500 licence; handler $100 / $1,000; modification $250 producer / $500 handler; producer pre-harvest inspection $250/lot + $35/hr travel + shipping; handler inspection $500/site + $35/hr travel.

  • Transfers: NO. §300(05): “All licenses are non-transferable.” There is no transfer procedure in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. A change of licensee requires a new application/licence — this is the necessary inference from non-transferability, not a separate transfer rule; Verify with ISDA if the dossier needs a change-of-control procedure spelled out.
  • Ownership / key-participant changes: no freestanding “ownership change” section was found. The mechanisms that exist are: (a) §300(10) License Amendment — any change to required information on an approved licence requires a licensee to submit an amendment on ISDA's form within 10 business days of the change, subject to §301 modification fees; (b) the key-participant definition (§010(06): person with direct/indirect financial interest, e.g. owner/partner, plus corporate executive-level persons) and the background-check rule below — a new key participant would have to be added and background-checked, but the rule does not state a separate approval timeline for key-participant substitution — Verify.
  • Renewals: annual only. §300(03): application period 1 September–31 December each year for the next calendar year; $250 late fee per late application. §300(09): licence valid 1 January–31 December, except a late-application licence valid from issuance to 31 December. Fees are non-refundable and, per ISDA's licence timeline (search result, Verify by opening), not pro-rated. ISDA FAQ opened directly confirms: licence valid Jan 1–Dec 31, late licences from issuance to Dec 31, not pro-rated.
  • Relocation / new site: no separate “relocation” section was found. Relocation is governed in practice by: licence amendment within 10 business days (§300(10)); §500(01) — any growing site not conforming to the licence must be destroyed or the licence amended; lot/facility information and maps are part of the application (ISDA FAQ: unclear/illegible maps can result in denial). A move to an unlicensed site without amendment would fall under §800(05)(a) disposal for plants in an unlicensed area — Verify the amendment-vs-new-application treatment for a full relocation with ISDA.
  • Suspension: §300(12) — a licence may be suspended when a licensee has engaged in conduct violating hemp law/rule, or failed to comply with a written order related to a negligent violation. A suspended licence may be restored after a waiting period of 1 year, and the person/entity may be required to comply with a corrective action plan to fully restore it.
  • Revocation: §300(13) — a licence will be revoked if the licensee: (a) pleads guilty to / is convicted of any felony related to a controlled substance; (b) made a materially false statement to ISDA with a culpable mental state greater than negligence; (c) was found growing cannabis exceeding the acceptable THC level with a culpable mental state greater than negligence; or (d) negligently violated law/rule 3 times in 5 years.
  • Ineligibility (separate from revocation): §300(08) — no licence to an ineligible person/entity: (a) person whose hemp licence was revoked by ISDA, USDA, another state, tribe or territory is ineligible for 5 years from revocation; (b) felony controlled-substance conviction (federal or any state) bars holding a licence, or being a key participant or governing person, before the 10th anniversary of conviction, unless lawfully growing hemp under the 2014 Farm Bill before 20 Dec 2018 and conviction also before that date; (c) material falsification in any application to ISDA/USDA/another state/tribe/territory; (d) under 18 at application; (e) 3 negligent violations in 5 years bars producing hemp for 5 years from the third violation.
  • Background checks: §300(02)(a) — applicant and any key participants must include a criminal history report from a fingerprint background check by Idaho State Police or FBI completed no more than 60 days before application submission. ISDA FAQ opened directly confirms the 60-day rule for applicant and key participants. Licensees pay background-check costs directly to the provider (§301(02)). No drug-test requirement for applicants/key participants is in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened — see §11.8.
  • Insurance / bonding minimums: NONE FOUND. No insurance, surety-bond, or financial-assurance minimum appears in IDAPA §§300–301 or 900 as opened, and no such minimum was found in Title 22, Chapter 17 summaries. Say so explicitly: Idaho imposes no hemp-licence insurance or bonding minimum on the sources opened. Verify against the current ISDA application form before stating this as exhaustive, as a form-level requirement would not appear in the rule text.
  • Other licence-holder duties in the rule: §300(11) — notify ISDA of any theft of hemp materials, growing or not, within 48 hours of discovery. §400 — producer records kept 3 years, available during normal business hours at the growing location (application documents; seed source; acreage planted/harvested/disposed; handlers sold to + quantities; variety/strain per lot; disposal/remediation records; copies of records submitted to ISDA/USDA/law enforcement). §600(03) — handler records kept 3 years at the facility (acquisitions with producer name/address/licence no./quantity/date; unprocessed sales with buyer name/address/licence no./quantity/date; products made with description/quantity/date of distribution; disposal records for product exceeding acceptable THC). §400(02) — all licence holders report to their local USDA FSA office per USDA requirements. Department employees and law enforcement need no licence for handling hemp in official duties (§300(14)).
  • Negligent violations — IDAPA vs 2026 statute, flag for harmonisation: IDAPA §900(01) lists negligent acts as: (a) failure to provide accurate legal description of land; (b) failure to obtain a licence; (c) production exceeding acceptable THC. §900(02): no more than 1 negligent violation per calendar year. §900(03): corrective action plan with a reasonable correction date + periodic reporting for not less than 2 consecutive calendar years; §900(04) inspections to check the plan; §900(05): negligent violation is not subject to criminal enforcement. §900(06): non-negligent violations (culpable mental state greater than negligence, including knowingly growing >0.3% delta-9 THC dry weight) are reported to Idaho State Police and the U.S. Attorney General. §900(07): penalties/procedure governed by §22-1705(5) and the Idaho Administrative Procedure Act, Title 67, Chapter 52. But Run 1's HB 772 (2026, eff. 1 July 2026) provides grain/fibre-only producers acting in good faith do not commit a negligent violation if total THC ≤1% dry weight. The opened IDAPA text (3-15-22) does not contain that 1% carve-out. Verify whether IDAPA §900 has been amended post-HB 772 before quoting §900(01)(c) as the complete current negligent-violation rule. Crops 0.3–1.0% may still face destruction/other requirements even without a negligent violation (per HempToday summary of HB 772, secondary, 26 June 2026 — Verify against enrolled HB 772 text).

11.3 Cultivation rules

Licensing: any person/entity must hold an active ISDA licence for planting, propagating, producing, handling or processing hemp in Idaho (§300(01)). ISDA FAQ opened directly: yes, even a couple of plants or for personal use/compost — anyone intending to cultivate hemp must hold a Producer Licence. No minimum or maximum square footage/acreage: any amount approved by ISDA on the licence (FAQ, opened directly). No cap on number of producer/handler licences issued (FAQ, opened directly). No variety restriction: varieties must be listed on the producer application; once approved, producer may grow the listed variety (FAQ, opened directly). ISDA publishes no certified-seed or seed-supplier list; growers source their own propagative material (FAQ, opened directly). Out-of-state seed dealers may sell seed in Idaho if licensed by their state/tribe/USDA programme, plus check with ISDA seed lab on seed-dealer licence need (FAQ, opened directly).

Plot / lot registration: “Lot” (§010(08)) = a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse or indoor growing structure containing the same variety/strain throughout. Application requires lot/facility information and maps in ISDA's example format; illegible/non-conforming maps can result in denial (FAQ, opened directly). Producer records must include variety/strain per lot and acreage planted/harvested/disposed (§400).

Pre-harvest sampling: see §11.1 in full (35-day request, ≤30-day sampling window, presence of licensee/key participant, 30-day harvest window, no movement before compliance notice, no commingling).

Indoor / outdoor rules: NO separate indoor vs outdoor regime found. The lot definition expressly includes field, greenhouse, or indoor growing structure, and the same §§500/700 sampling, harvest-window, no-commingling and no-movement rules apply to all three. No indoor-specific lighting, security, or setback rule was found in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. Do not import another state's indoor-cultivation rules. General building/fire/zoning codes would apply as ordinary law, but no hemp-specific indoor rule was located — Verify with ISDA if the dossier needs a definitive indoor statement.

Non-conforming sites: §500(01) — growing sites not conforming to the licence must be destroyed or the licence amended.

11.4 Security and transport (including transport permits — confirm: none separate)

Security: NO hemp-specific security rule found in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. No fencing, locked-storage, camera, alarm, guard, signage or inventory-control security standard appears in §§300–900 as opened. The only security-adjacent duties are: ISDA access to hemp sites for inspection/sample/testing/investigation (§500(02)); theft notification within 48 hours (§300(11)); 3-year records (§§400, 600); lot non-commingling (§500(07)); and no movement before compliance notice (§500(08)). A search for an Idaho hemp fencing/locked-storage requirement returned no Idaho hemp rule (only unrelated LPG, wastewater and pharmacy rules) — no such rule found; Verify against the ISDA Sampling/Disposal Protocols, which are incorporated by reference in the rule but were not opened directly. Do not import cannabis-licence security standards (cameras, vaults, etc.) from other states or from Idaho pharmacy rules (IDAPA 27.01.03) — those are not hemp rules.

Transport — NO separate ISDA transport permit, and NO transport fee in the §301 fee table. Confirmed. Producer licence authorises the holder “to transport their own hemp crop” (§300(06)). Handler licence authorises obtaining/possessing hemp, including seed, for processing but not for the holder's own cultivation (§300(07)). ISDA FAQ opened directly: carrying the licence when moving/distributing/manufacturing hemp is not required, but strongly encouraged.

Idaho Code §67-2921, opened directly 1 November 2026: §67-2921. History: added 2021, ch. 242, sec. 4; am. 2025, ch. 211, sec. 34. (1)(e) “Transporter” = any person/entity engaged in hauling/transporting/delivering/moving hemp in interstate or intrastate commerce. (2) ISP/ISDA transport rules broader/more stringent than federal law must go through negotiated rulemaking with explicit delineation — a procedural limit on stricter state transport rules. (3) When a transporter/vehicle hauling hemp under a 2014/2018 farm-bill or 7 CFR 990 licence is lawfully detained, the transporter must consent to inspection for farm-bill compliance; the peace officer may randomly select samples not to exceed 20 grams per sampling event per unique lot/package/quantity, retained for off-site testing, no compensation for de minimis samples; detention only as long as reasonably necessary for inspection, sampling and weighing. (4) Samples analysed per 2018 farm bill / 7 CFR 990 to determine total delta-9 THC including all THCA; non-compliant samples may subject the transporter to marijuana criminal penalties under Title 37, Chapter 27. (5) It is unlawful to knowingly possess industrial hemp without a licence or in violation of the 2014/2018 farm bills or 7 CFR 990, except when lawfully transporting on behalf of and at the direction of the licensee. (6) Penalties, opened directly: 1st violation misdemeanor, fine ≤$150; 2nd within 5 years of first misdemeanor, fine ≤$300; 3rd or subsequent within 5 years of first misdemeanor, fine ≤$1,000 and/or county jail ≤6 months; (6)(d) hemp transported/possessed in violation is deemed contraband, subject to seizure and destruction. (7) If transported/tested substance fails the §67-2921 hemp definition (>0.3% delta-9 dry weight), nothing in the section inhibits a peace officer enforcing Title 37, Chapter 27. (8) Section does not apply to material/product derived from hemp containing no quantity of delta-9 THC and not derived from prohibited marijuana-plant parts per §37-2701(u). (9) Does not apply to hemp transported before enactment. No transport manifest in the opened statute. A secondary site's claim of a “hemp transport manifest” under §§22-1706/22-1707 is not supported by the §67-2921 text opened directly and must not be cited — no manifest, bill-of-lading, advance-notice or port-of-entry requirement appears in §67-2921 as opened. The 2019 executive-order port-of-entry regime predates HB 126 and does not appear in the current statute — whether any port-of-entry requirement survives is Verify (carried from Run 1). ISDA's Sampling/Disposal Protocols were not opened — Verify whether they impose any transport documentation beyond §67-2921.

11.5 Waste disposal, including non-compliant hemp destruction

IDAPA §800, opened directly (same PDF), plus ISDA licence timeline (search result, Verify by opening): ISDA Hemp Program License Timeline (PDF).

  • Non-compliant lot — decision deadline: §800(01) — no more than 5 calendar days after notification that a lot tested above the acceptable THC level, the licensee must notify ISDA of the decision to destroy or remediate the entire non-compliant lot, and by which method, per ISDA's Disposal and Remediation Protocol.
  • Disposal method: §800(02) — dispose with chemical or mechanical destruction to render the material non-retrievable, non-ingestible, and unfit to enter the stream of commerce.
  • Remediation: §800(03) — lots may be remediated per the Protocol; remediated hemp is resampled and retested per §500(04) and §700; remediated hemp that fails retest must be destroyed and is not eligible for additional remediation. Timeline summary (search result): producers choose dispose or remediate; ISDA verifies disposal; retesting only on the original retained sample for a lab retest — note this is distinct from remediation resampling, which is a new sample after remediation.
  • Verification: §800(04) — ISDA must inspect and document disposal or remediation.
  • Other mandatory disposal: §800(05) — disposal required for: (a) hemp plants in an unlicensed area; (b) hemp plants not accounted for in required reporting; (c) lots destroyed due to pests, weeds, disease, poor stand, natural disaster, or weather event such as flood or hail.
  • Reporting: §800(06) — all hemp disposed of, for any reason, must be reported to and verified by ISDA, and may be subject to §301 fees.
  • Costs: §800(07) — all disposal/remediation costs paid by license holder or land owner.
  • USDA notification: §800(08) — ISDA provides USDA information about non-compliant plants, sites and related test results. §200(01) — ISDA provides USDA a hemp producer report, disposal report and annual state report per USDA deadlines.
  • Handler product over 0% THC: ISDA FAQ opened directly — handling facilities may obtain hemp up to 0.3% THC, but on manufacturing all hemp products must be at 0% THC at point of distribution; products that cannot reach 0% THC must be destroyed or be subject to law enforcement. Same for out-of-state distribution: manufactured products must be 0% THC in Idaho, including for out-of-state distribution; any level above 0% is subject to law enforcement.
  • General waste: no hemp-specific solid-waste, composting or wastewater rule was found in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. Ordinary Idaho solid-waste / DEQ rules would apply to non-hemp waste, but no hemp-waste classification was located — Verify with DEQ/ISDA if the dossier needs a general-waste statement. Do not state that destroyed hemp may be composted, landfilled or used as animal feed — §800(02) requires it be rendered non-retrievable/non-ingestible/unfit for commerce, and animal-feed use is separately prohibited (see Part 12, pets/feed).
  • HB 772 interaction: see §11.2 — whether a 0.3–1.0% grain/fibre crop that is not a negligent violation must still be destroyed/remediated is Verify against enrolled HB 772 and any updated IDAPA §800 / Protocol.

11.6 Environmental rules: water, pesticide limits, energy

No hemp-specific environmental chapter exists in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. Each sub-item is therefore “no hemp-specific rule found”, with the general-law position stated only where an official source was opened.

  • Water: NO hemp-specific water rule found. No irrigation, water-right, groundwater or water-use quantity rule for hemp appears in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened, and no ISDA hemp water rule was located. Idaho water rights generally operate under prior appropriation administered by the Idaho Department of Water Resources, but that general regime was not researched as a hemp rule in this run and must not be presented as one — Verify with IDWR if the dossier needs a water-rights statement. Do not state a hemp water allocation, metering or reporting requirement.
  • Pesticides: NO Idaho hemp pesticide list, and NO hemp-specific pesticide limit found in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. ISDA FAQ, opened directly: “ISDA will not be generating or publishing a list of pesticides approved for use on hemp in Idaho. ISDA does not generate nor publish a list of registered products for any commodity in Idaho.” ISDA FAQ, opened directly: ISDA does not require soil testing before licensing; buyers may require pesticide-residue or heavy-metal soil tests. General position (not a hemp rule): pesticide use in Idaho is governed by FIFRA label law and Idaho pesticide registration — a product may only be used consistently with its EPA-approved label, and at the time of the first Idaho season (2022, secondary: Idaho Farm Bureau / AgInfo) no herbicides were labelled for Idaho hemp, with University of Idaho trials (see §11.9) testing potato-labelled herbicides (Prowl H2O, Linex) through the IR-4 Project for a future hemp label. No current EPA hemp-labelled product list for Idaho was opened — Verify against EPA / ISDA pesticide registration before naming any product as lawful for hemp in Idaho. Do not import another state's hemp pesticide list.
  • Energy: NO hemp-specific energy rule found. No energy-use, renewable-energy, lighting, or carbon rule for hemp cultivation/processing appears in IDAPA 02.01.07 or Title 22, Chapter 17 as opened. Ordinary building/electrical codes and utility rules apply as general law, but no hemp energy standard was located — Verify if the dossier needs an energy statement.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts, promotions

NO hemp-specific sampling/giveaway/discount/promotion rule found in IDAPA 02.01.07 or Title 22, Chapter 17 as opened. State this explicitly; do not import cannabis-retail promotion bans from other states. Regulatory sampling is covered in §11.1 (§500/§700) — that is the only “sampling” regime in the hemp rule. Consumer sampling / giveaways: no rule was found that authorises, prohibits, or sets conditions (age, quantity, location, labelling) for giving away hemp/CBD samples, or for discounts/promotions, by a hemp licensee or by a retailer. Because there is no retail hemp licence (Run 1) and no retail promotion rule, a promotion is governed only by general law: giving away a product containing any THC would be delivery/possession of marijuana under §37-2732(a)/(e) unless the licensed-hemp exception applies — i.e. a “free THC sample” is not saved by being free. Giving away a zero-THC CBD/hemp product is not prohibited by any hemp rule found, but no rule affirmatively authorises it either, and general consumer-protection, food, and (for inhalable products) tobacco/e-device rules may apply depending on product type — Verify product-by-product before stating a promotion is lawful. Handler duty (§600(02)(b)): licensed handlers shall not sell, offer or transfer within Idaho any hemp products not in compliance with §37-2701 — that compliance duty (zero-THC for manufactured product) would apply to a giveaway/transfer as much as to a sale, on the rule's wording, but the rule does not use the words sample, giveaway, discount or promotion — Verify ISDA's interpretation before quoting §600(02)(b) as a promotion rule. Discounts / advertising: no hemp advertising, discount, loyalty, or price-promotion rule was found — consistent with Run 1 §1.6 (no packaging/labelling/advertising statute for hemp/CBD retail found). Do not state a ban on discounts, happy hours, or advertising.

11.8 Drug testing and background-check rules for licence holders and staff

Licence holders / key participants — background check, NOT drug test: IDAPA §300(02)(a) + ISDA FAQ (both opened directly, see §11.2): fingerprint criminal-history check by ISP/FBI within 60 days of application, for applicant and key participants. 10-year felony controlled-substance bar and 5-year post-revocation bar (§300(08)). No drug-test requirement for applicants, key participants or staff appears in IDAPA 02.01.07 as opened. Other staff — no hemp background-check or drug-test rule found: ISDA FAQ, opened directly: “You are free to employ individuals within your business/operation in accordance with the Idaho Labor Laws. Individuals that have been convicted of a felony related to a controlled substance… may not, before the tenth anniversary… hold a license, be a key participant, or be a governing person” — i.e. the felony bar applies to licence/key-participant/governing-person status, not to ordinary employment. Ordinary farm/facility employees do not need to be on the application unless they meet the key-participant definition (FAQ, opened directly). General employment drug testing (context, not a hemp rule): Idaho Code §§72-1701 et seq., the Idaho Employer Alcohol and Drug-Free Workplace Act, is a voluntary framework for employers generally (the 2003 amendment, S1049, removed private-only language and extended the Act to all employers / state subdivisions testing all employees where not constitutionally prohibited) (search summaries only — Verify full text before quoting). Secondary summaries state: employers who adopt a compliant written policy gain protections including a presumption of misconduct for unemployment purposes and workers'-compensation premium benefits; testing occasions in a policy may include pre-employment, reasonable-suspicion, post-accident and random testing; and, because marijuana is fully illegal in Idaho with no medical programme, there is no medical-marijuana defence to a positive test and no out-of-state medical card protection — consistent with Run 1's “no employment protections found”. Federal DOT testing (49 CFR Part 40) applies separately to safety-sensitive transport workers and is not modified by Idaho hemp law (secondary summary — Verify). Do not present §§72-1701 et seq. as a hemp-licence drug-testing rule; it is general employment law.

11.9 Research and university cannabis/hemp work (University of Idaho)

No separate research licence type in IDAPA §§300–301 as opened. IDAPA §001 scope says the rules govern “licensing, production, handling, and research of hemp”, and §300(01) requires an active licence for planting/propagating/producing/handling/processing — research is therefore licensed through the Producer/Handler licences, not through a distinct research permit on the sources opened. ISDA FAQ, opened directly: “Research and extension are required to possess a valid license. Please contact ISDA for additional information.”

University of Idaho on the ISDA licensee list (Run 1 list, opened directly 1 November 2026): ISDA licensees. 2023 list: University of Idaho, Aberdeen R&E Center — Producer, Aberdeen/Bingham; University of Idaho, Boise — Producer, Boise/Ada; University of Idaho, Twin Falls — Handler, Twin Falls/Twin Falls. 2022 list: University of Idaho, Aberdeen R&E Center — Producer, Aberdeen/Bingham; University of Idaho, Boise — Handler/Producer, Boise/Ada. 2024, 2025 and 2026 lists: NO University of Idaho entries appear on the ISDA page as opened. Do not present UI as a current (2026) ISDA licensee. Whether UI research continued under another entity, a different licence name, or outside ISDA licensing after 2023 is Verify with UI/ISDA.

What UI actually did (secondary, dated — not official UI/ISDA research reports): Idaho Farm Bureau / AgInfo, first Idaho season (2022): UI Extension weed scientist Pamela Hutchinson (Aberdeen Research and Extension Center) and operations manager Chad Jackson were among the first licensed growers; Hutchinson planted small irrigated industrial-hemp plots with seed from IND Hemp (Fort Benton, MT) to evaluate herbicides, because no herbicides were then available/labelled for Idaho hemp; promising leads were potato-labelled Prowl H2O and Linex, to be pursued through the IR-4 Project for a hemp label; Xi Liang (cropping systems agronomist) to assist future production trials; UI planned an Extension programme with the Shoshone-Bannock Tribes teaching ISDA registration and hemp growing. Sources (secondary): Idaho Farm Bureau, UI hemp trials; AgInfo, U of I and Hemp. Later interruption (secondary, 2025 committee reporting — Verify): a House Agricultural Affairs Committee briefing summary (secondary, Citizen Portal) reported that Idaho Global Entrepreneurial Mission (iGEM) support for hemp-related research was cut in late summer 2024, interrupting UI PhD student work and leaving some projects unfinished. Verify with UI before stating current UI hemp-research status or funding. No university medical-cannabis research programme was found. There is no Idaho medical programme to research under, and no UI cannabis (marijuana) research licence or programme was located — do not present UI hemp trials as cannabis/marijuana research. Any human-subject or controlled-substance marijuana research would require federal DEA/FDA authorisation and Idaho Schedule I compliance, which was not researched as a UI programme in this run — Verify if needed, do not assume it exists.

PART 12: CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke/vape shop rules

Matches Run 1 exactly; no new cannabis rule found. Paraphernalia — §37-2734A, opened directly in Run 1: unlawful to use, or possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant/propagate/cultivate/grow/harvest/manufacture/compound/convert/produce/process/prepare/test/analyze/pack/repack/store/contain/conceal/inject/ingest/inhale/introduce a controlled substance; unlawful to advertise paraphernalia in print knowing/reasonably should know its purpose is to promote sale of paraphernalia objects; misdemeanor, up to 1 year, up to $1,000, or both. Link: §37-2734A. §37-2734B (delivery/manufacture of paraphernalia): secondary summaries claim felony up to 9 years / $30,000 — still not opened directly, Verify, do not use until opened (carried from Run 1). Smoke/vape shops — NO hemp/CBD shop licence, and NO cannabis shop licence, exists. A shop selling tobacco/electronic smoking devices is governed by Title 39, Chapter 57 (below), not by hemp law. A shop selling only zero-THC CBD/hemp needs no ISDA retail licence (ISDA FAQ opened directly: “ISDA is not requiring licenses for retailers… CBD must have zero percent (0%) THC”). A shop selling any THC product, or paraphernalia for marijuana use, is exposed to §§37-2732, 37-2734A and 37-2701(u) presumption — there is no “smoke shop” safe harbour in Idaho law found.

Tobacco / e-device shop rules — Title 39, Chapter 57 (separate regime, do not conflate with hemp): §39-5704, search result from legislature.idaho.gov (full page Verify before quoting verbatim; text via Justia/FindLaw mirrors): unlawful to sell/distribute/offer tobacco products or electronic smoking devices at retail, or possess them with intent to sell at retail, without first obtaining a tobacco/e-device permit from the Department of Health and Welfare, which is the only retail tobacco/e-device permit required; permits issued annually for each permanent business location, separate permit per place of business, nontransferable to another person/business/location; permits expire annually at midnight 31 December, renewal notice mailed at least 90 days prior; permit closed when business closes, stops selling, moves, or is sold. Search-result links: §39-5704; Justia §39-5704. §39-5704(6) sign (search result): permittee may display a sign stating state law prohibits sale to under-21s, proof of age required, sellers/minors subject to fines/penalties. IDAPA 16.07.25.021 (Cornell mirror, search result — Verify current text) adds permittee responsibilities: permit available on request, sign displayed, employee training (including $100 per offence for selling to under-21, photo-ID verification, random inspections, vendor-assisted sales, original sealed packaging). §39-5704(8): minors (under 21 for this chapter) may possess but not sell/distribute in employment for stocking/carrying duties; employees 18–20 may sell/distribute in course/scope of employment. §39-5713 (Run 1): no local unit may adopt/enforce tobacco/e-device requirements more restrictive than Chapter 57 or impose a tobacco/e-device tax/fee, but nothing precludes local zoning/land-use authority. This is a tobacco pre-emption, not a hemp pre-emption. Fee for the tobacco/e-device permit was not confirmed from an official page opened in this run — Verify (secondary summaries claim free; do not publish a fee without DHW confirmation).

12.2 Minimum age for hemp and CBD products

NO statewide minimum age for hemp/CBD purchase was found in Idaho statute — this confirms Run 1, and the dossier must say so explicitly rather than adopting a secondary “18+” or “21+” figure as law. No age provision appears in Title 37, Chapter 27 or Title 22, Chapter 17 as opened in Run 1, and no age provision appears in IDAPA 02.01.07 §§300–900 as opened in this run, except: §300(08)(d) — a person under 18 at application is ineligible for a hemp licence (i.e. to produce/handle, not to buy CBD retail). Secondary CBD guides conflict and are not law: one 2026 guide says “Idaho does not set a statewide minimum age… most sellers require 18, many 21” (ATL Rx, 2026); another says “Idaho law does not specify any particular age” (CoBo CBD); others list Idaho as “18+” without citing a statute. None cites an Idaho statute, and none is adopted here. Retailer/store policies (18 or 21) are private policies, not Idaho law, and local ordinances were not found to set a CBD age — Verify city-by-city if the dossier needs a local-age statement. Distinct ages that must NOT be confused with a CBD age: Tobacco/e-devices: 21 (§39-5702/39-5704, Chapter 57) — applies to tobacco/e-device products, not to zero-THC CBD oil/cream as such. A CBD vape product sold as an electronic smoking device would fall under Chapter 57's 21 rule on its device/product definition — Verify DHW's application to CBD vape devices before stating this categorically. Alcohol: 21 (separate regime, not hemp). Hemp licence: 18 (see above). Marijuana possession $300 minimum fine: applies when possessed by a person 18 or older (§37-2732(e), Run 1) — that is a penalty trigger, not a purchase age, and there is no lawful marijuana purchase age in Idaho because there is no lawful purchase. Out-of-state purchase ages (Oregon/Washington/Nevada/Montana adult-use 21+) do not create an Idaho CBD age.

12.3 Impaired driving law and testing — including per se metabolite rule

No per se THC limit, and no per se metabolite rule, was found. The Idaho position is impairment-based for drugs, with a metabolite-only test held insufficient for administrative suspension. Do not assume a per se rule.

Idaho Code §18-8004, opened directly 1 November 2026: §18-8004. History: added 1984, ch. 22; last amendment listed am. 2002, ch. 253. (1)(a) It is unlawful for any person under the influence of alcohol, drugs or any other intoxicating substances, or any combination, or with alcohol concentration 0.08 or more as shown by blood/urine/breath analysis, to drive or be in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, on highway/street/bridge or public/private property open to the public. (1)(b)–(c) Commercial vehicles: under-influence standard, plus alcohol 0.04–<0.08 (b) and 0.08+ (c) tiers. (1)(d) Under-21: alcohol 0.02–<0.08 is unlawful (penalties in §18-8004A). These numeric thresholds are alcohol only. (2)–(3) A test showing alcohol <0.08 does not bar prosecution for under-influence on other competent evidence, and a <0.08 alcohol result may be considered with other competent evidence of drug use. (4) Evidentiary alcohol test formula (g/100cc blood, /210L breath, /67mL urine); analysis by ISP laboratory or ISP-approved laboratory/method; results admissible without a witness to establish reliability. (5) “Actual physical control” = in driver's position with motor running or vehicle moving. (7) The fact a person charged with DUI involving a drug is or has been entitled to use that drug under Idaho or any other jurisdiction's law is NOT a defence. This matters for out-of-state medical/recreational use: lawful use elsewhere is no defence to an Idaho DUI charge. No THC concentration, no nanogram limit, and no metabolite threshold appears in §18-8004 as opened. Penalties are in §§18-8004B/18-8004C/18-8005 (not opened directly in this run — Verify penalty tiers before quoting; do not import them from secondary summaries). Excessive DUI (§18-8004C) is listed in §67-3004(11)(b) as an assaultive/violent misdemeanour for shielding purposes (see §12.4) — that classification is Verify against current §18-8004C text.

Public-use / under-influence offence — §37-2732C (Run 1, search-snippet only, Verify full page): unlawful on a public roadway/conveyance, public property, or private property open to the public, to use or be under the influence of a §37-2705(b)–(f) controlled substance except as administered by/under direction of a licensed dispenser/prescriber/administrator; misdemeanor up to 6 months / $1,000; repeat provision with 120-day–1-year mandatory minimum on refusal of rehabilitation programme after 2+ violations within 5 years (snippet — Verify).

Metabolite case law — the key authority is AGAINST a metabolite-only per se rule: Reisenauer v. State, Idaho Supreme Court (2008), summarised via secondary case summary (Verify against the official opinion before quoting holdings verbatim): a positive urinalysis for carboxy-THC (inactive marijuana metabolite) was held not substantial evidence of presence of drugs/intoxicating substances for administrative licence suspension, because carboxy-THC indicates past use, not current intoxication; the statute requires evidence of an intoxicating substance, not merely a metabolite. Secondary summary: Studicata, Reisenauer summary. Idaho Dept. of Transportation v. Van Camp, Idaho Supreme Court, 14 Nov 2012, Docket 38958: this was a cyclobenzaprine case, not a THC case. The Court reversed and remanded, holding the licensee bore the burden under §18-8002A(7)(c) to prove the drug was not intoxicating and that Reisenauer’s reasoning was limited to that subsection. Do not cite Van Camp as a THC/metabolite holding. The Court quoted Reisenauer — “This substance is merely a metabolite of marijuana… Consequently, we held that Reisenauer met his burden… to show that the test did not reveal the presence of a drug or other intoxicating substance.” Source: Justia, Van Camp — Verify the full opinion for the precise holding and its limits (administrative suspension vs criminal DUI). State v. Morin, 158 Idaho 622, 349 P.3d 1213 (Idaho App. 2015, review denied 22 June 2015) — criminal DUI conviction: the Court of Appeals held carboxy-THC evidence was relevant and properly admitted when combined with other evidence of present impairment; it held the discovery response was deficient as a matter of law but did not warrant reversal because other evidence (officer observations, slurred speech, strange responses, failed field sobriety tests, lack of convergence, dilated pupils and abnormal eye tremors, dilated pupils, other substances including venlafaxine metabolite and amphetamine) supported impairment; carboxy-THC is described in the opinion as an inactive metabolite with no pharmacological effect, detectable 10 days to a month after use, long after intoxication ends, and “relevant when the State also presents evidence of present marijuana impairment” — i.e. not a standalone per se proof. Source (opinion PDF via search): Morin opinion (PDF) — Verify against the official opinion before quoting, and do not present Morin as creating a metabolite per se rule; it does the opposite in its reasoning, even though the conviction stood on other evidence. Dossier formulation (safe): “Idaho has no per se THC or metabolite limit for driving. A drug DUI under §18-8004(1)(a) requires proof the driver was under the influence of a drug/intoxicating substance. Carboxy-THC alone has been held insufficient to prove that for administrative suspension (Reisenauer), and in criminal cases it is at most corroborative of present impairment when combined with other impairment evidence (Morin). Blood/urine testing, field sobriety tests and officer observations remain the evidentiary routes; no numeric THC threshold converts a test alone into a DUI.” A secondary law-firm blog claiming “any metabolites in the system” alone supports a DUI charge overstates Reisenauer/Morin and is not adopted. Implied consent / refusal and administrative suspension (§§18-8002, 18-8002A) were not opened directly in this run — Verify before stating refusal penalties or suspension periods. Do not import other states' implied-consent periods.

12.4 Expungement and record clearing for marijuana offences

Idaho has NO general expungement statute for convictions, and NO marijuana-specific expungement/clearing law was found. What exists is narrower: (a) non-conviction expungement, (b) Clean Slate shielding (not destruction), (c) withheld-judgment dismissal. Each must be kept distinct.

No marijuana-specific relief: no statute was found that automatically or specifically expunges, seals or vacates marijuana possession, paraphernalia or trafficking convictions, and no Idaho “cannabis expungement” programme was located. A marijuana conviction is therefore treated under the general rules below, not under a cannabis-specific rule. Verify against the current legislature index before stating this as exhaustive.

(a) Non-conviction expungement — §67-3004(10), search result from ISP/Justia (full legislature page Verify): ISP criminal history; Justia §67-3004. Any person arrested/served a criminal summons who subsequently was not charged by indictment or information within 1 year of arrest/summons, and any person acquitted of all offences arising from the arrest/summons, or who has had all charges dismissed, may have the fingerprint/criminal-history record for that incident expunged on written request to the Department (ISP/BCI) and may have the official court file sealed. This provision does not apply to a dismissal granted under §19-2604(1). ISP FAQ (search result): to use §67-3004(10) the person need only provide arresting-agency or court records showing compliance; a dismissed charge otherwise continues to show with the dismissal noted, and is removed only if expungement is approved; information remains indefinitely unless legal action is taken for removal. Limits: this is fingerprint/history-record expungement + court-file sealing for non-convictions only. It does not erase a conviction, and it does not by itself clear a marijuana conviction.

(b) Clean Slate shielding — §67-3004(11), HB 149, effective 1 January 2024 (search results, full legislature page Verify): Eligible: non-violent, non-assaultive misdemeanours AND felony possession of a controlled substance convictions. This is the only conviction-relief route that could cover a marijuana possession conviction, and eligibility turns on the exact offence of conviction, not on the drug alone: a misdemeanour marijuana possession conviction (≤3 oz, §37-2732(e)) is a non-violent, non-assaultive misdemeanour on its face and would fall within the eligible class, subject to the conditions below — Verify the excluded-offence list in §67-3004(11)(b) before stating any specific marijuana offence is eligible, as the list includes assaultive/violent misdemeanours (assault, battery, domestic battery, stalking 2nd, protection/no-contact order violations, telephone harassment, vehicular manslaughter, excessive DUI §18-8004C, domestic assault, etc.). A felony possession conviction (e.g. >3 oz possession under §37-2732(e), or trafficking under §37-2732B) — only felony possession of a controlled substance is listed as eligible; whether a trafficking conviction (manufacture/delivery/bringing into state, §37-2732B) counts as “possession” for §67-3004(11) is not stated in the summaries opened and is Verify — do not assume trafficking is shieldable. Delivery/manufacture convictions are at higher risk of exclusion. Conditions (search results, consistent across sources): petition to the court of conviction no earlier than 5 years after completing the sentence, including all ordered probation, parole, fines and restitution; during that 5-year period and until the hearing, no subsequent felony or misdemeanour convictions, not on probation/parole for a subsequent conviction, no pending misdemeanour/felony cases, no restraining orders in effect at filing; court must find after hearing the petitioner has been held accountable and shielding would not compromise public safety or victims' safety; only one shielding petition per lifetime — one offence, or offences arising from a single incident/transaction; shielding revoked on request of prosecutor upon subsequent felony conviction (mandatory) or misdemeanour conviction (discretionary); shielded proceedings are deemed never to have occurred and petitioner may lawfully reply accordingly, but shielded records remain accessible to law enforcement and certain court officers — shielding is not destruction/expungement. Official forms: Idaho Supreme Court Clean Slate materials (secondary summaries point to isc.idaho.gov/Clean-Slate-Act — Verify URL before publishing).

(c) Withheld judgment / dismissal — §19-2604 (search result, Verify full text): a court may withhold judgment and place a defendant on probation; on successful completion the court may dismiss, which restores civil rights in the statute's terms, but §67-3004(10) expressly excludes §19-2604(1) dismissals from its non-conviction expungement route, and pre-2007 “expungement” readings of §19-2604 were restricted by State v. Parkinson, 144 Idaho 825, and later cases (Turpen, Gurney, Dieter) per a 2013 Statement of Purpose (secondary/legislature PDF, Verify current §19-2604 text). Do not present withheld-judgment dismissal as equivalent to expungement.

(d) Juvenile: juvenile adjudications may be expunged under the Juvenile Corrections Act, §20-525A, and Idaho Juvenile Rule 28, subject to limits/exclusions for serious/subsequent offences and pending charges (secondary court-records summary — Verify full text). This is a juvenile route, not an adult marijuana-conviction route. (e) DNA expungement — §19-5513 (old Justia text, Verify current): a person whose DNA profile is in the database may request expungement of DNA materials where the underlying conviction was reversed and the case dismissed, on court order after notice/hearing — a reversal/dismissal route, not a general conviction-clearing route. Pardon / restoration: a felony conviction's firearm and civil-rights consequences are governed separately (§18-310 restoration on final discharge, except listed offences; Commission of Pardons and Parole for restoration after felony/domestic-violence misdemeanour — secondary summaries, Verify). Pardon is distinct from expungement/shielding and is not a marijuana-specific remedy.

12.5 Landlord and tenant rules

No marijuana-specific landlord/tenant statute was found. The rules that apply are general lease/eviction law plus the controlled-substance eviction provision. Do not present a cannabis-specific tenancy protection or ban. General framework (secondary summaries, Verify §§6-303, 6-320, 55-208, 55-307 on legislature.idaho.gov before quoting): Idaho has comparatively few landlord/tenant statutes; most terms are set by the written lease. Landlords may prohibit smoking and marijuana use by lease term (secondary summaries); because marijuana remains illegal in Idaho, a landlord has a straightforward contractual and illegality basis to prohibit it — but no Idaho statute was found that requires a landlord to prohibit, or prohibits a landlord from prohibiting, lawful hemp/CBD use as such. Eviction for controlled-substance activity — §6-303 (secondary summaries, Verify full text): General lease violation: 3-day notice to perform or quit (§6-303(3) per secondary summaries). Serious damage / unauthorised assignment/subletting: 3-day notice to quit without chance to remedy (§6-303(4)). No notice required before filing an unlawful-detainer action where the tenant is or has been engaged in the unlawful delivery, production, or use of a controlled substance at the rental (§6-303(5) per secondary summaries; one secondary guide phrases this as a 3-day notice to quit for illegal drug activity — conflict between secondary summaries: Verify the exact §6-303 notice requirement before quoting a number of days). Either way, unlawful delivery/production/use of a controlled substance (which includes marijuana in Idaho) at the premises is a freestanding eviction ground; it does not depend on a lease smoking clause. No tenant protection for marijuana use was found: there is no Idaho statute protecting a tenant's marijuana, CBD or hemp use against eviction, and no “medical use” tenancy defence exists because there is no Idaho medical programme. A tenant using zero-THC CBD/hemp is not using a controlled substance, so §6-303's controlled-substance ground would not apply on that fact alone — but a lease smoking/vaping ban, a nuisance clause, or a general lease-violation clause could still apply depending on the lease wording — Verify lease-by-lease; do not state a general CBD-tenant protection. Hemp cultivation on rented land: a tenant growing hemp must hold the Producer Licence in their own name (licences non-transferable, §300(05)); a landlord's consent/lease permission does not substitute for ISDA licensing, and unlicensed growing on rented land falls under §800(05)(a) disposal and §37-2732/§67-2921 exposure. No landlord-consent form or hemp-lease statute was found — Verify if the dossier needs a hemp-lease clause.

12.6 Gun ownership conflicts

Federal prohibition is the operative conflict; Idaho law mirrors it for concealed-carry licensing, and Idaho's permitless-carry law does not override federal law.

Federal — 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) (secondary summaries of federal law, Verify against U.S. Code/ATF form before quoting penalties verbatim): it is unlawful for any person who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance to ship, transport, possess or receive any firearm or ammunition. Marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance federally for adult-use (and, for Idaho purposes, there is no state-licensed medical activity to fall inside the federal Schedule III medical carve-out in the Run 1 baseline). ATF Form 4473, Question 21.f (current form, per secondary summaries) asks whether the buyer is an unlawful user of/addicted to marijuana or any other controlled substance, with a warning that marijuana use remains unlawful federally regardless of state legalisation/decriminalisation for medicinal/recreational purposes. Answering “no” while being an unlawful user is a separate false-statement felony (18 U.S.C. §922(a)(6) / §1001 in different summaries — Verify the correct false-statement citation before publishing). Knowing violation of §922(g) carries up to 15 years under §924(a)(8) per secondary summaries (raised from 10 years by the Bipartisan Safer Communities Act 2022). 2026 developments — Verify before relying, do not present as settled law: secondary summaries (2026) report (a) an ATF interim final rule in January 2026 rewriting “unlawful user” as regular use over an extended period continuing into the present, dropping single-incident triggers; (b) an ATF draft Form 4473 revision published 8 May 2026 recasting the marijuana question to focus on recreational use and omitting medical-marijuana disqualification, not yet in effect pending rulemaking/comment; (c) Supreme Court oral argument in United States v. Hemani in early 2026, decision expected late June 2026, which could narrow/eliminate/leave §922(g)(3) as applied to marijuana users. None of these was verified against the Federal Register / ATF final form / Supreme Court opinion in this run — mark all three Verify, and state the current-form position as the operative rule until a final rule/opinion changes it. The parent dossier must re-check ATF/Supreme Court status on publication day. Hemp/CBD distinction: lawful use of zero-THC hemp/CBD is not use of a Schedule I controlled substance and does not, on its own, make a person an “unlawful user” — but a product containing any THC, or a positive THC test from a non-zero-THC product, could create a factual dispute. No ATF guidance specifically on zero-THC CBD and §922(g)(3) was opened — Verify before stating a CBD-safe-harbour categorically. Lawful Idaho hemp licensees (producers/handlers) are not, by virtue of licensure alone, unlawful users; the prohibition turns on use, not on hemp licensure.

Idaho — §18-3302 (search results from legislature/Justia, full current page Verify): §18-3302(11) (2025 Justia text): a concealed-weapons licence shall not be issued to a person who, inter alia, (c) has been adjudicated guilty of a crime punishable by >1 year, or (e) is an unlawful user of marijuana or any depressant, stimulant, narcotic drug, or controlled substance as defined in 21 U.S.C. 802, or (a) is under 21 years of age (except as otherwise provided) a firearm under state or federal law. Idaho is a permitless-carry state for eligible adults (18+, U.S. citizen/armed-forces member, not disqualified) — but permitless carry hinges on not being disqualified under §18-3302(11), so an unlawful marijuana user is disqualified from permitless carry on the same criteria (secondary summary — Verify current §18-3302(11) numbering, as older texts place the list in §18-3302(1)). A state licence/permit is not a defence to federal prosecution (caution in older §18-3302 text, search result). Felony marijuana convictions (>3 oz possession felony, trafficking felony) also disqualify via the >1-year crime criterion and via federal §922(g)(1) (felon-in-possession) — restoration on final discharge under §18-310 except listed offences is a separate, secondary-summary point — Verify §18-310 before quoting restoration. No Idaho statute was found that creates a state-law firearm right for marijuana users, or that shields Idaho marijuana users from the federal prohibition. Do not present Idaho's permitless-carry law as resolving the federal conflict.

12.7 Pet and veterinary products

Prohibited as feed/remedy ingredients in Idaho; no FDA-approved CBD pet product exists. This is stricter than the human zero-THC rule. ISDA position — FAQ opened directly + ISDA memo (secondary summary of memo, Verify memo itself): ISDA FAQ, opened directly: “Idaho state law does not recognize the use of hemp derivatives in food for animal consumption. ISDA follows FDA and AAFCO guidelines on recognizing the use of hemp and hemp products in animal feed as illegal.” ISDA memo (reported secondary, High There summary of ISDA memo, 2022 enforcement from 1 Nov 2022): “Hemp or hemp-derived products, including CBD, are not recognized by FDA or AAFCO as legal feed ingredients… Similarly, Idaho law does not recognize hemp as a feed or remedy ingredient… Safe levels… have not yet been established… These products are not approved feed ingredients and cannot lawfully be added to or incorporated into commercial feed. This includes feeds, treats and remedies intended for pets, livestock, or any other animal.” And: “Animal remedies in Idaho are exempted from feed requirements except when misbranded with nutritive claims or considered adulterated… Animal remedies containing hemp are adulterated and are being unlawfully marketed and distributed in Idaho.” Products found on/after 1 Nov 2022 subject to stop sale and further action. Source (secondary): High There, Idaho bans hemp/CBD for pets/livestock — Verify the ISDA memo itself and whether the 2022 stop-sale position remains current in 2026 before quoting enforcement dates as current. Federal position (secondary, consistent): FDA has not approved any CBD product for use in animals; safety/effectiveness not established; CBD is not GRAS for animal feed or human food on FDA's stated position; FDA has issued warning letters for unapproved disease claims for pet CBD products. No FDA-approved veterinary CBD drug was found. Verify against FDA CVM before quoting a current FDA statement verbatim. Veterinarian discussion/prescription: no Idaho veterinary-board rule on CBD discussion, recommendation or prescription was opened in this run — Verify with the Idaho Board of Veterinary Medicine. Do not state that Idaho vets may or may not discuss CBD; the only firm Idaho point found is the ISDA feed/remedy prohibition above. A veterinarian cannot lawfully prescribe a non-approved CBD drug, and cannot create a lawful Idaho feed ingredient by recommendation. Human vs pet distinction for the dossier: a zero-THC CBD product lawful for a human in Idaho is not thereby lawful as a pet/livestock feed, treat or remedy in Idaho. The two regimes must not be merged. Hulled hemp seed / hemp seed protein powder / hemp seed oil exclusions in §22-1705(4) (human ingestion/inhalation) do not create a pet-feed authorisation — Verify whether any seed-ingredient exclusion applies to animal feed; on the FAQ wording it does not.

12.8 CBD use in schools

No Idaho statute authorises CBD use in schools. The school regime is a controlled-substance / district-policy regime, and CBD's status turns on THC content and on district policy, not on a school-CBD permission. Idaho Code §33-210, search result from legislature.idaho.gov (full page Verify): §33-210. (1) Each school district board (including specially chartered districts) and charter-school governing board must adopt and implement policies specifying how personnel respond when a student discloses or is reasonably suspected of using or being under the influence of alcohol or any controlled substance defined by §37-2732C. Voluntary disclosure: anonymity on a faculty “need to know” basis, notification to parents/guardian/custodian and availability of counselling. Reasonable suspicion of use/under-influence in violation of §37-2732C: administrator/designee must contact parent/guardian/custodian and report to law enforcement; prior voluntary disclosure is not a factor in later reasonable-suspicion determinations. (2) Students may also be subject to other district/charter disciplinary or safety policies, whether or not they disclosed or are reasonably suspected. (3) Procedures for contacting law enforcement and parents must be developed; policies made available to each student/parent/guardian/custodian. What §33-210 does NOT do: it does not mention CBD, hemp, Epidiolex, or any school-administration permission. It does not create a right to possess/use CBD at school, and it does not exempt a prescribed FDA drug from district policy — district medication policies (which were not opened in this run) govern prescription administration at school generally. District policies (secondary examples, not statewide law): district Student Drug, Alcohol and Tobacco Use policies (e.g. Policy 551, secondary PDFs) define “controlled substances” to include cannabis and synthetic equivalents, “illegal drugs” by reference to §§37-2701/37-2705/37-2707/37-2709/37-2711 (excluding OTC/prescribed drugs), and “vape” to include devices delivering nicotine, THC, CBD, flavoured aerosol or any other substance by inhalation, with all components/accessories covered; students “will not use, be under the influence of, possess, sell, buy, or distribute” listed drugs/prohibited items. These are district policies, not a statewide CBD-in-schools statute — Verify the specific district's current policy before stating any school's rule. A zero-THC CBD oil is not a §37-2705 controlled substance, but a district policy that bans “CBD” by name, bans vapes delivering CBD, or bans mood-altering/herbal-supplement products could still prohibit it at school — the dossier must not state “zero-THC CBD is allowed in Idaho schools” as a general rule. Epidiolex at school: Epidiolex is an FDA-approved prescription drug (Run 1). No Idaho school-administration statute for Epidiolex was found in this run. Administration would fall under general district medication-administration policy and, where applicable, disability/504/IEP planning — Verify district policy and do not present Epidiolex school use as authorised by §33-210 or by hemp law. Staff / employee CBD use at school: no school-employee CBD rule was found; general employment drug-testing law (§§72-1701 et seq., see §11.8) and district personnel policy would apply — Verify, do not assume a staff permission or ban. Bottom line for Part 12: Idaho schools operate under a prohibition-and-referral framework for controlled substances, with district discretion for other discipline. There is no CBD-in-schools permission, no school CBD registry, and no statewide school CBD policy on the sources opened.

RESEARCH NOTES — COVERING BOTH RUNS (Run 1 Parts 0–6 and Run 2 Parts 7–12)

A. Cross-run consistency and corrections

  1. Run 1 ↔ Run 2 consistency — confirmed. No cannabis licences, no medical programme, Schedule I, zero-THC retail, Producer/Handler fees ($500/$1,000 licences; $100 applications; $250/lot and $500/site inspections; $35/hr travel), ISDA 2026 counts (11 licensees, 233 approved acres), HB 7 / HB 772 / HB 879 status, HB 771 not law / HB 478 dead, HJR 4 on ballot, medical initiative failed to qualify — all match across runs. Where Run 2 opened a source directly that Run 1 had only via search snippet, Run 2's text controls (see items 2–4).
  2. Resolved in Run 2 — §67-2921 full text. Run 1 flagged §67-2921 as search-snippet only. Run 2 opened it directly in full (history: added 2021, ch. 242, sec. 4; am. 2025, ch. 211, sec. 34): 20-gram sample limit, total delta-9 including all THCA, transport penalties 1st ≤$150 / 2nd ≤$300 / 3rd ≤$1,000 and/or ≤6 months, contraband/seizure. Remaining Verify: only whether any EO 2019-13 port-of-entry requirement survives — no such requirement appears in the current §67-2921 text.
  3. Resolved in Run 2 — IDAPA 02.01.07 current rule (partial). Run 1 flagged current IDAPA text as not opened. Run 2 opened the current rule PDF (sections dated 3-15-22): Producer/Handler definitions, §300 licence rules, §500 sampling, §700 testing, §800 disposal, §900 negligent violations, §301 fees. Remaining Verify: whether IDAPA has been re-promulgated post-HB 772 (1% grain/fibre negligent-violation carve-out is in statute, not in the 3-15-22 rule text) and post-HB 879 retail scope.
  4. Resolved in Run 2 — current modification fees. Current modification fees are $250 (Producer) / $500 (Handler). The $100 modification fee is PROPOSED only in Docket 02-0107-2601. Do not use $100 as current.
  5. Corrected in Run 2 — HB 122 (2019) history. Run 1 shorthand “died in committee” is replaced by the full history in Part 9: House passed 63–7–0 (18 Mar 2019), Senate passed as amended 32–3–0 (1 Apr 2019), 4 Apr 2019: “Without recommendation on concurrence” after referral to Agricultural Affairs for a concurrence recommendation (4 Apr 2019) — i.e. passed the House, passed the Senate as amended, then died in the House on concurrence. Do not revert to the shorthand.
  6. Corrected in Run 2 — 2021 constitutional amendment claim. The secondary claim that the 2021 legislature “passed” a constitutional amendment banning psychoactive drugs is incorrect on the evidence found: Senate passed 24–11, House did not pass, never reached the 2022 ballot, never became law. The ban measure is SJR 101 (2021): Senate adopted 24–11 on 3 February 2021 (floor sponsor Sen. C. Scott Grow), received in the House 4 February 2021 and referred to State Affairs; no House passage is shown on its legislature page. It is a different measure from 2021 HJR 4, which proposed a two-thirds legislative vote to legalise certain controlled substances and failed on the House floor 42–28 on 15 April 2021 — it was voted on, not never voted on. Neither measure reached the ballot or became law. Do not conflate either with 2025 HJR 4. The 6–2 committee figure conflicts with a secondary 7–2 figure — Verify the committee vote.
  7. Corrected in Run 2 — HB 7 date. One secondary CBD guide's “1 January 2026” HB 7 effective date is rejected; Run 1's legislature date, 1 July 2025, stands.
  8. Corrected in Run 2 — 2022 ISDA count. Run 1's final file already corrected an earlier subagent count of 22 to 23 (re-counted from the opened table). Run 2 re-confirms 23 for 2022, 23 for 2023, 23 for 2024, 16 for 2025, 11 for 2026.

The only new “property” result is a verified count of zero under the strict standard in Part 8. The only new tribal programmes are the two USDA-approved hemp plans (Nez Perce, Shoshone-Bannock); no tribal marijuana programme was found for any of the five tribes.
